<summary>This is the title page for the second volume of the Zenon Papyri catalogue, edited by C.C. Edgar and published in 1926 by the French Institute in Cairo. It identifies the specific inventory range of the papyri included in this volume of the Cairo Museum's general collection.</summary>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page serves as the introduction to a volume of the Zenon Papyri, establishing the chronological scope and administrative context of the documents that follow.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

---

The present volume continues the publication of the dated documents from the archives of Zenon; the 158 texts which are here printed cover the period between the beginning of year 30 and the end of year 35 (256 to 250 B.C.). About forty of the more interesting have been already published in our *Annals*, and many of the others are for the present merely fragments, though I have little doubt that some of them will be completed before very long by the discovery of the missing parts. But besides these, the reader will find here a large quantity of quite new material arranged in the order in which it once passed through Zenon's hands. During the period in question, Zenon was permanently stationed at Philadelphia <note>A Greek settlement in the Faiyum region of Egypt, named after Queen Arsinoe Philadelphos</note> and the position which he occupied there as Apollonios' man of business brought him a multitude of correspondents from all classes of the population. Above all, we have a long series of letters from Apollonios himself; and though these are mainly instructions about the management of the estate, the actual words of a man who for many years played a leading part in public life, revealing as they do something of his real character, cannot fail to be interesting.

In arranging the material, I have gone on the assumption that the dates refer to the <term>regnal year</term> <note>A system of dating based on the years of a monarch's reign, in this case Ptolemy II Philadelphos</note>, unless we find in the text a definite indication to the contrary. It will be observed that the accounts nos. 59206, 59292, 59293 and 59296 are clearly dated by the <term>financial year</term> <note>The fiscal calendar used by the Ptolemaic administration for tax and agricultural purposes</note> which began on the 1^st^ of Mecheir <note>The sixth month of the Egyptian calendar</note>, and we must admit the possibility that the scribes who were accustomed to reckon by the financial year in their accounts may have fallen into the habit of using it for other documents as well. I think indeed that this is not only possible, but probable. If for instance we compare *P. S. I.* <note>Pubblicazioni della Società Italiana, a standard series for papyri</note> 379 and 381, we cannot but conclude that the scribe who wrote the latter text has in fact dated it by the financial year. But fortunately it is not until we reach the reign of Euergetes <note>Ptolemy III Euergetes, successor to Philadelphos</note> that this uncertainty becomes seriously troublesome. For during the later part of the reign of Philadelphos, the financial year coincided with the regnal except for a short space of from one to three months. As regards the correspondence between the Macedonian and the Egyptian calendars, some recently

<summary>This introduction outlines the scope of 158 documents from the Zenon archive (256–250 B.C.), highlighting Zenon's role as estate manager for the high official Apollonios. It also discusses the technical challenges of reconciling the regnal, financial, Macedonian, and Egyptian dating systems used in the papyri.</summary>
<keywords>Zenon, Apollonios, Philadelphia, Ptolemaic Egypt, papyrology, regnal year, financial year, Mecheir, Ptolemy II Philadelphos, calendar systems</keywords>

# ZENON PAPYRI.

**59140. Letter from Alexandros to Zenon.** — 0.23 m x 0.08 m. — Date: 28^th^ May, 256 B.C.

In a letter of which the meaning is clearer than the grammar, Alexandros informs Zenon that he has inquired about the charges which Kritias has been making against Charidemos and has found them to be false. He speaks of Kritias denouncing *the soldiers from your side* <note>original: "τῶν παρὰ σοῦ στρατιωτῶν"</note>, the allusion being to Charidemos; but this phrase does not mean that Zenon held a military command, though he may have had some authority over the <term>cleruchs</term> <note>Soldiers who were granted plots of land in Egypt by the Ptolemaic government in exchange for military service.</note> in his district. Alexandros may perhaps be the <term>chief of police</term> <note>original: "ἀρχιφυλακίτης" (archiphylakites), a local official responsible for security.</note> mentioned in an unpublished papyrus in Michigan.

-> Alexandros to Zenon, <-
-> greetings. You wrote <-
-> to me about Kritias, that he <-
-> was bringing charges against Charidemos. <-
-> 5 Having therefore <-
-> sent for Charidemos, <-
-> when questioned he said <-
-> that none of these things <-
-> were true, "but if Kritias <-
-> 10 has any charge to bring, I am <-
-> ready to have the matter decided <-
-> with him." Be informed... <-

*Line 3.* was bringing charges <note>original: "ἐνεκάλει"</note>: Zenon had probably used the imperfect tense in his own letter.
*Line 7.* he said <note>original: "ἔφασεν"</note>: thus in the original, not the more common form <note>original: "ἔφησεν"</note>.

<summary>This page introduces a Greek letter from 256 B.C. where Alexandros reports to Zenon that accusations made by Kritias against a man named Charidemos appear to be baseless. It provides historical context on the social and military status of the individuals involved in the Zenon archive.</summary>
<keywords>Zenon Papyri, Alexandros, Kritias, Charidemos, cleruchs, chief of police, legal dispute, Ptolemaic Egypt, 256 B.C.</keywords>

*Lines 16-17. pheidestai*: not *pheidestai*, but a misspelling of *pheidesthai* <note>original: "ϕείδεσθαι"</note> (cf. MAYSER, *Grammar*, I, p. 178).

### 59141. Letter (from Mys?) to Zenon. — 0 m. 13 x 0 m. 08 and 0 m. 125 x 0 m. 10. — Date: May-June, 256 B.C.

> As there do not seem to be many letters wanting at the beginning of the lines, the writer may be Mys <note>Mys was an agent of Zenon</note>. He tells Zenon how he has disposed of certain large quantities of grain. I think it is probable that these came from the Memphite district <term>nome: an administrative district in ancient Egypt</term>, whether they had been grown on Apollonios’ estate or on cleruchic land <term>cleruchic: land granted to mercenary soldiers</term> (cf. no. 59132). The import of such transactions will no doubt become clearer in time, when all the material has been studied.

[My]s to Zenon, [gre]et[ings. You wrote to us instructing] the remaining available
[emme]r <note>original: "ὄλυρα" (olyra), a type of hulled wheat</note> from the [twenty-]ninth [year to be sen]t to Alexandria.
We have [th]erefore done th[is]. ... which Charmyl[os] sent to
[u]s ... [amounting to] 2,381 1/2 1/4 <term>artabae: a Greek measure of capacity, roughly 30-40 liters</term>. And regarding the
<margin>5</margin> [remain]ing barley, [know] that it has been [measured out] to Theudoros, the [assistant]
of [Ph]ilon, and to Dionysios ... [amounting to] 3,113 1/2 1/4 1/8 artabae. In order therefore that [you]

*Line 4.* For example, f[ro]m Mo[ithymis].
*Line 5.* For example, kno[w that it has been measured out by] us.
*Lines 5-6.* Perhaps those under Cheilon.
*Line 6.* For example, [to the grain-measurer, being]. For Dionysios the grain-measurer <note>original: "σιτομέτρης"</note> cf. *P. Edg.*, 86, where there are indications that he was employed at Memphis.

[may kn]ow, I have written to you.
->Farewell.<-
Year 30, Pharmouthi [day missing]

VERSO: To Z[enon].

### 59142. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0 m. 14 x 0 m. 32. — Date: 1^st^ July, 256 B.C. (plate I).

> Apollonios <note>The Dioiketes or Finance Minister of Egypt</note> approves the distribution of Milesian wool to the slave-girls in Memphis, and he tells Zenon to give orders to let them have as much as they need. The wool came from a flock of Milesian sheep <note>A highly prized breed from Miletus in Asia Minor</note> which Apollonios owned in the Fayoum (see no. 59195 and *P. Edg.*, 107) and the girls were employed in a wool factory belonging to him in Memphis (see *P. S. I.*, 854).

Apollonios to Zenon, greetings. You did rightly
in giving the Milesian wool to the slave-girls in Memphis.
And now, give orders to give them
as much as they may have need of.
<margin>5</margin> Farewell. Year 30, Artemisios 10, Pachon 9. <note>Double dating using the Macedonian and Egyptian calendars</note>

VERSO:

Year 30, Artemisios 29, Pachon 29. To Zenon. Regarding wool.
Apollonios regarding [Mi]lesian wool, that [it is] of the Milesian [kind].
The slave-girls have it, and so that
more may be given to them.

*Line 7. Mi(lesian)*: a large letter M with an I through the middle.
*Line 9. dothei*: or perhaps "be given."

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Entry Journal, no. 48591; P. Edg.*, 24; WILCKEN, *Hellenistic Economy*, p. 107; *Collection of Greek Inscriptions*, no. 6730.

---

### 59143. Letter from Nikanor to Zenon. — (a) 0 m. 065 x 0 m. 075; (b) 0 m. 07 x 0 m. 145. — Date: about 13^th^ July, 256 B.C.

> Commerce with Asia, "Ishmaelites from Gilead with their camels bearing spicery and balm and myrrh," had long ago made the camel known to the Egyptians, but it does not seem to have been introduced into Egypt until Ptolemaic times. At the date of the present letter the camel was certainly being used to some extent in the interior of Egypt. See also no. 59207, 7 and *B. G. U.*, 1351, 1353.

<meta>catchword: 1.</meta>

<summary>This page contains catalog entries for three papyrus letters (59141-59143) concerning grain shipments, the distribution of high-quality Milesian wool to a factory in Memphis, and early evidence of camel use in Ptolemaic Egypt.</summary>

# 4 CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM.

The fragment (*b*) is in Florence and has been published in *P. S. I.* <note>original: "Pubblicazioni della Società Italiana"</note>, 562. I cannot say whether the Nikanor who writes the letter is identical with the author of *P. S. I.*, 356 and with the Nikanor of nos. 59147, 10 and 59160, 2. He may perhaps have belonged to a <term>cleruchy</term> <note>A colony of Greek military settlers who received land grants in Egypt in exchange for service</note>; for the practice of dating by the Macedonian calendar seems to have been retained among the military longer than among the civil section of the foreign population.

->Nikanor to Zenon, greetings. Concerning the camels, we met with Libanos<-
->the fodder [.......].<-
->Farewell. Year 30, 23rd of Artemisios.<-

**Verso:**

Year 30, 25th of Artemisios, [25th of Pachons]. To Zenon.
5 Nikanor, regarding camels.

Line 4. 25th: might also be 29th.

**Bibl.:** *Entry Journal of the Museum*, no. 49667.

**59144. Letter from Charmos to Zenon.** — 0 m. 155 x 0 m. 085. — Date: 31st July, 256 B.C. (pl. II).

Charmos (see nos. 59016, 59078) announces that he has sent Zenon an acknowledgment of the receipt of ten talents of iron by Asklepiades and Rodon.

->Charmos to Zenon, greetings.<-
->I have sent you the<-
->receipt for the discharge<-
->which was made by Asklepiades<-
5 ->and Rodon for the<-
->10 talents of iron.<-
->Farewell. [ ]<-

**Verso:**

Year 30, 9th of Daisios, 9th of Pauni. To Zenon.
iron
Charmos
10 10 talents of iron.

Line 9. iron: a note of the sender, written before the docket of reception.

**Bibl.:** *Entry Journal of the Museum*, no. 48592.

**59145. Petition to Zenon from Sphragis.** — 0 m. 33 x 0 m. 095. — Date of reception: 2nd August(?), 256 B.C. (pl. II).

Sphragis, either the <term>maidservant</term> <note>original: "paidiske"</note> bought by Zenon three years ago in Transjordania (see no. 59003) or more probably another woman of the same name, had been robbed on her way to Sophthis, a village in the Memphite <term>nome</term> <note>An administrative district in Ancient Egypt</note>, of a few articles which she was carrying, two garments, some wool and a little copper money. She asks Zenon to write to Leontiskos, the chief policeman in that district, to give her back the things stolen, which were now reported to have been found.

->To Zenon, greetings<-
->from Sphragis. I gave you<-
->previously a petition<-
->concerning the things of which I was <gloss>robbed</gloss> <insert>stripped</insert><-
5 ->while traveling to<-
->Sophthis in the Memphite<-
->district for wool. The<-
->itemized list of what I lost is:<-
->2 garments worth 12 drachmae, and<-
10 ->wool worth 2 drachmae, and copper worth 2 drachmae<-
->/ Total: 16 drachmae. I beg you therefore,<-
->if it seems good to you, to pity<-
->me by writing to Leontiskos<-
->the chief of police<-
15 ->to examine the<-
->stolen goods and return them. For<-
->they have reported to me that<-
->they have found them.<-
->May you prosper.<-

**Verso:**

20 Year 30, 11th of Daisios, 1st of Pauni.

Line 5. while traveling: <note>Grammatical note on the Greek case ending</note>. I cannot distinguish an "o", but the letters are mutilated. Cf. no. 59261, 7.
Line 6. Sophthis: perhaps the modern village of Saft el-Meidoum.
Line 10. copper: either "of copper" or "copper coins".
Line 13. by writing: as if preceded by "pity me." — Leontiskos: cf. *P. S. I.*, 440, 6-7.
Line 20. 1st of Pauni: probably a mistake for 11th of Pauni. Or perhaps Year 30 is a slip for Year 31.

->Sphragis <note>original: "ἐπ"</note> regarding Leontiskos.<-

In year 30 Zenon usually assimilated the two calendars, while in year 31 he usually put an interval of ten days between the Macedonian day of the month and the Egyptian (see Index IV).
Line 21. **ep** in monogram, equivalent to "to send" <note>original: "ἐπιστεῖλαι"</note> or "of a letter" <note>original: "ἐπιστολῆς"</note>.

### 59146. Letter from Agathon to Zenon. — 0 m. 12 X 0 m. 255. — Date of reception : 10th August, 256 B. C.

Agathon had received from Mestris, the mother of Menippos’ wife, certain garments belonging to Zenon, a <term>chlamys</term> <note>A short cloak or mantle worn by men, often for riding or travel</note> and two <term>chitons</term> <note>A basic tunic, the most common garment in the Greek world</note>, one of them with sleeves. He has finished his work on them <note>original: "εἴργασμαι οὖν αὐτά"</note> and asks to whom he is to hand them over to take to Zenon. The writer, whom I would not identify with the Agathon of no. 59269, 23, may possibly have been a fuller <note>A worker who cleaned, thickened, or finished cloth</note>.

Agathon to Zenon, greetings. Mestris, the mother of Athenais <note>athenais: restored name</note>, the wife of Menippos, gave me what she said were yours: one <term>chlamys</term>, two <term>chitons</term>, one of these being sleeved. I have therefore finished the work on them and [they are] with me. Write to me then to whom you order me to give them, who will [convey them]
5 to you.
->Farewell. Year 30, [date]<-

**VERSO:**

Year 30, 19th of Daisios, 19th of Pauni. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; To Zenon.
Agathon, regarding what he has from Mestris.

*Line 2. 1: originally written close to chlamys, then rubbed out and rewritten with an interval between.*
*Line 3. [they are present] or something similar.*

<meta>BIBL. : Entry Journal of the Museum, no. 48595.</meta>

### 59147. Letter from Mys to Zenon. — 0 m. 25 X 0 m. 125. — Date : 6th August, 256 B. C. (pl. III).

Mys asks Zenon to strike out from the estimate the quantities of corn of which Mys reports reception, in order that their accounts may not be at variance with each other. Zenon had ordered him to measure out to Nikanor a certain quantity of lentils, but the latter claimed more and Mys asks for instructions. The crops in question

<column-break/>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

here were probably grown in the Memphite <term>nome</term> <note>An administrative district or province in ancient Egypt</note>. The hand seems to be the same as that of the earlier letters of Nikon (see vol. I, p. 181, note on no. 59068).

Mys to Zenon, greetings. You would
do well, if it also seems right to you,
to remove from the estimate the
corn which I report as having received;
5 for the individual items have been 
registered for you in the estimate,
as it is useful for this to happen
so that it is not charged twice. And you wrote
in the letter concerning
10 Nikanor to measure out to him
<gloss>7</gloss>
8 1/2 <term>choinikes</term> <note>A dry measure, approximately one liter</note> of lentils. He then said there had been a 
mistake and asked us for 6 1/2.
Write to us then what we should do.
Farewell. Year 30,
->15th of Pauni.<-

**VERSO:**

Year 30, 19th of Daisios, &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; To Zenon.
19th of Pauni.
Mys, regarding the estimate.

*Line 3. estimate <note>original: "εἰκασμοῦ"</note>: equivalent to "estimation" <note>original: "εἰκασία"</note> in P. Teb., I, 60, 53, an estimate of the expected yield on certain areas of land.*
*Line 4. I report <note>original: "Φέρω"</note>: equivalent to "report back" <note>original: "ἀναφέρω"</note>.*
*Line 11. Perhaps 88 <gloss>7</gloss> 1/2 <term>choinikes</term>.*

<meta>BIBL. : Entry Journal of the Museum, no. 48594.</meta>

### 59148. Letter from Hierokles to Zenon. — 0 m. 085 X 0 m. 325. — Date : 23rd August, 256 B. C. (pl. IV).

Hierokles, the author of nos. 59060-59061, announces that Kallikon has sent a slave to Herakleopolis on some business and that Ptolemaios has asked the slave to pass by Philadelphia in order that Zenon may buy and send him two <term>himatia</term> <note>Outer garments or cloaks worn over a chiton</note>, which are said to be cheaper up the river than in Alexandria.

Hierokles to Zenon, greetings. If you are well and in other respects are faring according to your mind, it would be well; I myself am also healthy...

Preisigke, *Dictionary*, gives Ktesios as a deme-name <note>A local administrative district name</note>, but a deme-name would scarcely be used in a record of this sort.

### 59149. Letter from Artemidoros to Zenon. — 0.12 m x 0.26 m. — Date of reception: 27th August, 256 B.C. (Plate IV).

In the same hand as no. 59120. It is probable that Artemidoros lived at Memphis and that the Sarapeion <note>The temple of the god Serapis</note> mentioned in line 5 was the great temple at Saqqarah. See also no. 59136, introduction. The letter seems to be a complaint against Agathinos (see no. 59134) about a transaction in wine. Zenon, who exercised authority at Memphis as well as at Philadelphia, is asked to force him to pay and to exact an account from him on oath.

->Artemidoros to Zenon, greetings.<-
->...neither from the common wine [...<-
->jars of the wine, but not even one drachma. You would [therefore do well]<-
->by forcing him to return the money to us [...<-

*Line 4.* Perhaps "already and to give" <note>original: ἤδη καὶ δοῦναι</note>. It is not possible to read "or the money."

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>
<sig>2</sig>

5 ...having sworn an oath in the Sarapeion as to the price it was sold at <note>original: ὡς πέπραται</note>, since [we intended] to do other things with this, even though we have trusted [him]...
->Farewell. Year 30, Epeiph [date].<-

->VERSO:<-

->Year 30, 6th of Panemos, 6th of Epeiph. To Zenon.<-
->Artemidoros, [son] of Agathinos.<-

*Line 5.* "As to": "at what price" <note>original: ὡς</note>.
*Line 6.* "Other things" <note>original: ἄλλα</note> seems more probable than "but" <note>original: ἀλλά</note>, but I do not clearly see the meaning.

### 59150. Letter from Theodoros to Zenon. — 0.31 m x 0.19 m. — Date of reception: 22nd September, 256 B.C. (Plate V).

Theodoros, who may or may not be the assistant <note>original: ὑπηρέτης</note> of nos. 59062, 59089, had delivered 90 artabs <note>A dry measure of capacity, roughly 30-40 liters</note> of wheat, from somewhere in the countryside <note>original: χώρα</note>, to be transported to Alexandria and placed to his account. Of these he had received, on Zenon's order and at various times, 55 artabs from Artemidoros the steward of the household, so that Zenon still owed him 35 artabs. He now demands the balance in order to repay Artemidoros the scribe, from whom he had borrowed corn. But the steward refused to deliver any more wheat, even if Zenon should order him. So he urges Zenon to see that the wheat is delivered, as otherwise he will hold him responsible for the price of it. It is noticeable that Zenon's orders were promptly executed in the first half of year 29 when he was closely connected with the household, but that after he had retired to Philadelphia the steward in Alexandria paid no attention to his requests.

->Theodoros to Zenon, greetings. In the<-
->28th year, there were brought down for us to Alexandria<-
->from the countryside, having been measured out,<-
->ninety artabs of wheat.<-
5 ->Of these, we have been measured out by your order<-
->from Artemidoros, the one in Alexandria:<-
->on the 11th of Xandikos, 1 artab; on the 20th, 4 artabs;<-
->on the 30th, 10 artabs; on the 26th of Artemisios, 10 artabs;<-
->on the 5th of Loios, 30 artabs; total 55 artabs of wheat, and<-

*Lines 7-9.* From no. 59089 we see that Theodoros the assistant <note>original: ὑπηρέτης</note> was living in Alexandria during these months, and it therefore seems possible enough that he was the writer of the present letter.

<keywords>Epharmostos, Onesimos, clothing, Memphis, Sarapeion, Agathinos, wine dispute, Theodoros, wheat, artabs, Alexandria, year 30, year 28</keywords>

<margin>10</margin> ...there are still thirty-five artabae <note>original: "ἀρ λε"; an artaba was a standard Greek measure of volume for dry goods, roughly 30–40 liters</note> of wheat owed to us by you. 
You would therefore do well to write to the person from whom 
we are to receive it in Alexandria. For having already borrowed 
grain from Artemidorus the 
secretary <note>original: "γραμματέως"</note>, we are anxious to repay it. 
<margin>15</margin> And now, being present in Alexandria, 
we are making a demand of Artemidorus, the house-steward, 
saying that you owe [the wheat], 
but he said it was not his business to deal with these matters, 
nor would he pay any attention even if you were to write. So, take care 
<margin>20</margin> regarding the delivery, so that we do not 
justly charge the value to you, unless 
<insert>at the very last</insert> 
by Zeus, you judge <note>original: "εὐκρινεῖς"</note> that while the others and 
those with Simylos have received their shares, what is due to us 
from you remains lacking. And Artemidorus the secretary 
<margin>25</margin> says that although you wrote to him to measure it out, 
no one has attended to it.

-> Farewell. <-

VERSO:

Year 30, [Loi]os 2, Mesore 2 <note>These are dates in the Macedonian and Egyptian calendars, roughly August/September 256 B.C.</note>. To Zenon. 
Theodoros, regarding grain.

---

*Line 11. from whom:* <note>original: "παρ' οὗ"</note> equivalent to "from someone" <note>original: "παρὰ τίνος"</note>.
*Line 13. Artemidorus the secretary:* probably identical, as Westermann has pointed out, with the official letter-writer <note>original: "ἐπιστολογράφος"</note> of *P. Cornell* 1, 128.
*Line 22.* From the handwriting, one could equally well read "you judge" <note>original: "σὺ κρίνεις"</note> or "well do you judge" <note>original: "εὖ κρίνεις"</note>. In any case, the meaning must be "unless you think it right." <term>PREISIGKE, Dictionary</term> <note>original: "Wörterb."</note> under the relevant entry treats "well-judged" <note>original: "εὐκρινεῖς"</note> as an adjective, which is clearly impossible here.
*Line 22.* The inserted phrase "at the very last" <note>original: "τὸ ἔσχατον"</note> may be used as an adverb. It is possible, however, that "and the last part" is the object of "to have received" and that "those with Simylos" describes "the others."

<meta>BIBLIOGRAPHY: Museum Entry Journal, no. 48598; P. Edg., 26; Sammelbuch, 6732.</meta>

### 59153. Fragment of a letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0.14 m x 0.21 m. — Date uncertain.

Apparently an order to see that the houses for the <term>neaniskoi</term> <note>literally "young men"; refers here to military cadets or new recruits</note>, the cadets or recruits, are finished without delay. I have inserted some conjectural restorations to show how the text may have run. The letter is not likely to be as early as year 29 or as late as year 32, when Zenon had ceased to use double-dates (both Macedonian and Egyptian calendars) on his filing notes, and I think it most probably belongs to year 30. If so, it was received on the 17th of December, 256 B.C.

> Apollonios to Zenon, [greetings. You will]
> [do] well to take care [immediately so that for the]
> [young] men the dwellings [as quickly as possible]
> [are] fi[ni]sh[ed] ju[st as we previously]
> <margin>5</margin> [wr]ote.
> ->Farewell. Year [..]<-

->VERSO:<-

> [Year 30, Hyper]beretaios 2[3], To Zenon.
> [Phao]phi 23.
> [Regarding the] young men <term>neaniskoi</term>.

*Line 9. Read <term>neaniskois</term> <note>Greek: "to the young men"</note>. The preceding word may have been "houses" or "dwellings."*

### 59154. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0.145 m x 0.34 m. — Date: 17th December, 256 B.C.

Apollonios <note>The Dioiketes or Finance Minister of Egypt</note> orders Zenon to send down to Alexandria a cargo of dry logs, as many and as thick as possible, to be used for the festival of Isis. This was the great winter festival beginning on the 17th and ending on the 20th of the Egyptian month Athyr (see Wilcken, *U.P.Z.*, I, p. 401). In 258 B.C. Apollonios and Zenon were staying in Memphis at the time of the celebration and we see from the records in *P. Cornell 1* that the two last days were observed as an official holiday, most of Apollonios’ offices being closed. The present letter shows that even in Alexandria—and to Greeks as well as Egyptians—the <term>Eisieia</term> <note>The Festival of Isis</note> were one of the great religious events of the year.

> Apollonios to Zenon, greetings. Having loaded
> as many and as thick logs of dry wood as 
> possible onto a boat, send them as quickly as possible to Alexandria,
> so that we may have enough to use for the festival of the <term>Eisieia</term>.
> <margin>5</margin> ->Farewell. Year 30, Dios 3, Phaophi 23.<-

->VERSO:<-

> Year 30, Dios 18, Athyr 18. To Zenon.
> Apollonios regarding wood
> [for] the <term>Eisieia</term>.

On the other side of the same fold, and in the same large letters as the address:
*Haste.* <note>original: "ἤδη"</note> At the right end of the next fold, in a small hand:

> [Wo]od for the
> [<term>Eisiei</term>]a.

*Verso. "Haste" <note>original: "ἤδη"</note> may, as Bell suggests, be equivalent to the "Haste, haste, post haste" of the Tudor period; but in fact the letter took nearly a month to reach its destination. Wilcken surmises that it may have lain neglected for some days in Apollonios’ office till someone observed it and added the injunction "Haste." The words to the right are probably a note written by the dispatching clerk.*

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48601; *P. Edgar*, 93; Wilcken, *Archiv*, VIII, p. 67; *Sammelbuch*, 6808.

### 59155. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0.19 m x 0.34 m. — Date: 27th December, 256 B.C. (Plate VI).

Apollonios writes that the king <note>Ptolemy II Philadelphus</note> has bidden him sow a second crop on the land. After harvesting the early corn Zenon is to water the land, erecting shadoufs <note>ancient counterbalanced sweeps used to lift water from the Nile or canals</note> if necessary,

<summary>This page contains correspondence from Apollonios to Zenon regarding administrative duties, specifically the urgent completion of housing for military recruits and the shipment of firewood for the major religious Festival of Isis in Alexandria.</summary>
<keywords>Apollonios, Zenon, Alexandria, Festival of Isis, Eisieia, recruits, military housing, irrigation, logs, shipping, Ptolemaic calendars</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the archive of Zenon, the manager of a massive estate in Philadelphia (modern Fayum) for Apollonios, the finance minister of Egypt under King Ptolemy II Philadelphus. The previous page ended with a letter from Charmos regarding livestock and the start of an administrative order from Apollonios regarding military housing. This page moves into direct agricultural instructions from the minister to his manager, highlighting the intensive cultivation techniques and landscaping projects ordered by the King himself.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM.</header>

and to keep it flooded for not more than five days, and as soon as it dries he is to sow the three-month wheat. He is also to inform Apollonios when he will be able to harvest the first crop.

The order refers to a particular piece of land which was being cultivated by Zenon. Whether it means the whole *land-grant* <note>original: δωρεά (dorea)</note> is doubtful (see introduction to *P. Edg.*, 88 as well as the works cited below). The king had told Apollonios to sow a second crop on it; but it should be noted that the sowing of a second crop was probably already contemplated when Zenon sowed the *early grain* <note>original: πρώιον σῖτον</note>, which, as Schnebel says, may have been barley. Without knowing more of the circumstances we cannot appreciate the exact force of the king's instructions. The conclusions drawn by Schnebel from the text are too sweeping; it seems to me unlikely that the practice of raising two crops in the year was first introduced into Egypt by Ptolemy II.

> Apollonios to Zenon greetings. The king has ordered us
> to sow the land twice. As soon as you have harvested the early grain,
> immediately water the land by hand, or if that is not possible, 
> set up several <term>kheloneia</term> <note>irrigation machines or shadufs</note> and water it that way, but do not 
> 5 keep the water on it for more than
> five days, and after it has dried, immediately
> sow the three-month wheat. Write to us also 
> when you are able to harvest the grain.
> ->Farewell. Year 30, Dios 13, Hathyr 3.<-

VERSO : ->To Zenon. [Concerning] sowing.<-

Line 1. *has been bidding me* <note>original: συνέτασσεν</note> : 'has been bidding me'. I fail to understand Schnebel's note on this word, the meaning of which is quite clear.
Line 3. *by hand* <note>original: ἀπὸ χερός</note> : lifting the water by hand, i. e. by means of buckets or *nattals*. Cf. *Herodotus*, I, 193: "but it is irrigated by hands and by shadufs" <note>original: ἀλλὰ χερσί τε καὶ κηλωνηίοις ἀρδόμενος</note>.
Line 9. Either *sowing* <note>original: σπόρου</note> or [*double*-]*sowing* <note>original: δισπόρου</note>, a note of the sender. Bilabel's restoration [Apollonios regarding the double] sowing is not possible.

BIBLIOGRAPHY : *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48605; *P. Edg.*, 27; ROSTOVTZEFF, *Large Estate*, p. 49 and pl. III; Johannesen in *Class. Phil.*, p. 156 ff.; SCHNEBEL, *Agriculture*, pp. 145-160; *Sammelbuch*, 6733.

### **59156. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon.** — 0.12 m x 0.295 m (not including the fragment in the British Museum). — Date : 27th December, 256 B. C.

Zenon is ordered to take from Memphis, from Apollonios' own garden and from the palace grounds, pear shoots and young plants, as many as possible, and also to get some sweet-apple trees from Hermaphilos, all to be planted in the orchards at Philadelphia.

<column-break/>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

A fragment of this letter, comprising the first few letters in lines 1, 2 and the docket on the verso, is in the British Museum (Inventory no. 2318, A) and is published here by permission of the authorities.

> Apollonios to Zenon greetings. Transport as many 
> pear shoots and plants as possible from Memphis, 
> both from our own garden [and fro]m the citadel, 
> and also take some sweet-apple trees from Hermaphilos,
> [and] plant them.
> 5 ->Farewell. Year 30, Dios 13, Hathyr 3.<-

VERSO :
->Year 30, Dios 23, Hathyr 23. To Zenon. Regarding shoots.<-
->Regarding shoots.<-

Line 3. *citadel* <note>original: ἄκρας</note> : as Lumbroso has shown (*Bull. Soc. Arch. Alex.*, X, p. 198) that *citadel* was the original name for the royal residence in Alexandria, I infer that *citadel* here means the palace at Memphis, the *palace* <note>original: βασίλεια</note> of which Strabo saw the ruins: "It is built on a height reaching down to the ground level of the city, and a grove and a lake are joined to it" <note>original: ἵδρυται δ᾽ ἐφ᾽ ὕψους καθήκοντα μέχρι τοῦ κατὰ τῆς πόλεως ἐδάφους, συνάπτει δ᾽ ἄλσος αὐτῶι καὶ λίμνη</note>.
Line 3. *Hermaphilos* <note>original: Ἑρμαφίλου</note> : whether this was the Hermaphilos who afterwards became the Arsinoite <term>oeconomus</term> <note>a regional financial administrator</note> is quite uncertain.

BIBLIOGRAPHY : *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48602; *P. Edg.*, 94; *Sammelbuch*, 6809.

### **59157. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon.** — 0.135 m x 0.335 m. — Date : 27th December, 256 B. C.

Apollonios orders Zenon to plant fir-trees all over the park and round the vineyard and the olive-groves, to the number of at least three hundred. « For the tree has a striking appearance and will be of service to the king ». That is to say, it would provide timber for ship-building besides being an ornament to the estate.

> Apollonios to Zeno[n greetings]. Plant fir-trees throughout 
> the whole park and around the vineyard and the olive-groves, 
> and see that you plant as many as possible—
> ideally [more], but at least three hundred. 
> 5 For the tree provides a striking appearance and will be 
> of service to the king.
> ->Farewell. Year 30, Dios 13, Hathyr 3.<-

VERSO :
->[Year 30] Dios [2]3, Hathyr 23. To Ze[non]. Regarding fir-trees.<-
->Regarding fir-trees.<-

BIBLIOGRAPHY : *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48603; *P. Edg.*, 95; *Bull. Soc. arch. Alex.*, XIX, p. 118; *Sammelbuch*, 6810.

### 59158. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0 m. 13 x 0 m. 205. — Date : 27th December, 256 B. C.

### 59159. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0 m. 185 x 0 m. 24. — Date : 7th January, 255 B. C.

Apollonios reminds Zenon that it is time to plant the vines and the olives and the other shoots. Zenon is therefore to send for a supply from Memphis and the other places and to give orders to begin planting. Apollonios promises to send him from the Alexandrian district more vine-shoots and whatever other sorts of fruit-trees may be useful. Compare no. 59162.

[Apol]loni[os] to Zenon, g[reetings. It is] ti[me] to plant [the]
[vi]ne and the oliv[e and t]he other shoots.
[Se]nding for [them] therefore from [M]emphis and from the other
[plac]es, give ord[e]rs to plant. [And] we
5 [our]selves will also send from the separated territory <note>original: τῆς ἀφωρισμένης (tes aphorismenes)</note> [more] vine shoots
and the other kinds [whatever] may be useful.
->Farewell. Year 30, Diou 24, A[thyr 14].<-

**VERSO :** To Zenon.

*Line 5.* the separated territory : the territory separated from the Libyan nome <note>A 'nome' was an administrative district in ancient Egypt.</note> and attached to Alexandria (see *Rev. Laws*, col. 61, 1).

BIBL. : *Journal d'entrée du Musée*, no. 48608 ; *P. Edg.*, 96 ; *Sammelbuch*, 6811.

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>
<sig>3</sig>

### 59160. Letter from Nikon to Zenon. — 0 m. 125 x 0 m. 335. — Date of reception : 11th January, 255 B. C.

Nikon asks Zenon to write to Nikanor to repay some oil which Nikon had given him for the use of the men. He begs Zenon to send him help (in the form of money, no doubt) as he is nearly destitute and is conscious of being in disgrace with Apollonios. Menemachos has urged him to sail down to Alexandria and see Apollonios, promising to speak on his behalf and advising him to talk about the work at Philadelphia. Zenon is asked to write if he approves of this idea and also to send him some corn in order that he may not be obliged to buy at a high price.
In spite of the fact that the handwriting is different, the author of this letter is probably the Nikon who wrote nos. 59049, 59071. Though he is advised to speak to Apollonios about Philadelphia (the subject which was most likely to appeal to the minister), he was not living there, for he asks Zenon to have the corn sent to him. It is therefore likely that he was still at Memphis or in the Memphite nome. Moreover the names of Nikanor and Agathinos recall nos. 59147 and 59149, and there is reason to suppose that both these documents come from the said district.

Nikon to Zenon greetings. If you are well and other things are going according to your mind, there would be
much thanks to the gods;
and I too am healthy. You would do well to write to Nikanor about the 
oil which we gave for the
[workers] <note>original: σώματα (somata) – literally "bodies," a term often used to refer to slaves or manual laborers</note>, so that he may return it to us; there are five <term>choes</term> <note>A liquid measure of about 3.3 liters.</note>. And concerning the things I
wrote to you before, you would do
well, if it seems right to you, to send to us, for I do not think you are unaware that, if
we do not receive something from you,
5 I shall perish from hunger, until I know where on earth I stand, since because of Apollonios we cannot 
[lift up our heads] <note>original: ἀνακύψαι (anakypsai) – literally "to bend back" or "to pop up", implying relief from a heavy burden or debt</note>,
but it happens that we are constantly terrified as if we have committed the greatest wrongs.
Menemachos also urged us to sail down to Apollonios, thinking it best to be seen by him,
and that he will speak on our behalf so that we might achieve something, and that he will do the most for us, he said,
if we mention to him the work in Philadelphia. If therefore you approve of my 
sailing down
10 so that we may meet him, write to me. And if it seems right to you, give us some grain so that it may not be [bought] at a high price.

*Lines 3-4.* For [choes] and [being unaware] see MAYSER, *Grammar* I, p. 110.
*Line 5.* [Until I know] : this phrase, ‘until I know where on earth I stand’, is illogical unless taken in connection with [sending]. Cf. MAYSER, *Grammar* II, p. 269.
*Line 8.* [Will do most] : ‘will have most effect’.

Catalogue of the Museum, no. 59140.

### 59161. Letter from Kleon(?) to Zenon. — 0 m. 12 x 0 m. 31. — Date : 15^th^ January, 255 B. C.

The Kleon whom from the handwriting and the remains of the name I take to be the author of this letter seems to have been one of Zenon's Carian friends (*P. Edg.*, 63, introduction), though probably not the young Kleon who addresses him as father (*P. S. I.*, 528). Kleon had been meaning to send for some pigs to sell and had spoken to Damis suggesting that (as he himself was starting on a journey and had no messenger free) the latter should write to Zenon and ask whether he was to buy for him too. Damis replied that he would wait till Kleon came back and buy for Zenon and himself in common. Kleon had now returned and had put at his disposal 59 pigs at the current price of 4 drachmæ and half an obol each. He has written to keep Zenon informed.

[K]leon to Zenon, g[re]etings. If you are in good health, it would be well; we too are well. 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Intending
to send for [so]me sacrificial swine, <note>original: "ἱερεῖα ὑικὰ" — pigs specifically intended for sacrifice or high-quality market sale.</note> I informed Damis, because I myself was about 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to depart
<margin>5</margin> by night and did not have enough [messengers] to send someone to you, so that
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Damis might write to you
[if] he should buy more for you; but he said that he would wait for me until I arrive
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;and buy
[the] sacrificial animals for you and himself in common. Having returned then, I presented to him at 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;the current price of
4 drachmæ and 1/2 obol <note>original: "⊢δc" — the symbol for drachma followed by the numeral 4 and the fraction for a half-obol.</note> each, 59 sacrificial animals. I have written to you therefore so that you may be informed.
->Farewell. Year 30, Hathyr 22.<-

**Verso :**
[Year 30, D]ios 23, Hathyr 23. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; To Zenon.
[Kleon] regarding sacrificial animals.

Bibl. : *Journal d’entrée du Musée*, no. 48607.

### 59163. Fragment of a letter to Zenon. — 0 m. 24 x 0 m. 08. — Date : 6^th^ March, 255 B. C.

To judge by the hand this may be a fragment of a letter from Panakestor. The reading is doubtful in several places; but if the remainder of the text is found, it will no doubt become easy to decipher what is at present almost illegible. ### 59164. Letter from Panakestor to Zenon. — 0 m. 065 X 0 m. 365. — Date : 18^th^ March, 255 B. C. (pl. VII).

Panakestor thanks Zenon for sending the donkeys. He has received also ten mattocks and he will try to hire donkey-drivers from his own district. We may suppose that Panakestor was at present working not at Philadelphia but on some other part of Apollonios’ estates (perhaps near the river, if no. 59163 was really written by him).

Panakestor to Zenon, greetings. You wrote to us about the beasts of burden. You did <gloss>well</gloss> <note>The scribe originally wrote "you are doing" then corrected it to the past tense "you did well."</note> in sending them to us. We have also received ten mattocks. We will try to hire the donkey-drivers from this district <note>original: "ὧδεθεν" — a colloquial word for "from here" not found in formal Greek literature.</note>.
->Farewell. Year 30, Tybi 24.<-

->VERSO :<-

->. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .<-
->5 . . . . . beasts of burden (?) To Zenon.<-
->mattocks.<-

*Line 1. The first letter of "beasts of burden" corrected.*
*Line 2. "from this district" : a word unknown to Greek literature.*

BIBL. : *Journal d'entrée du Musée, no. 48611.*

### 59166. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0 m. 14 X 0 m. 185. — Date : March or April, 255 B. C.

Apollonios asks Zenon to send him a list of all the yokes of cattle, female and male, so that he may buy as many more as are needed and send them to Philadelphia.

Apollonios to Zenon, greetings. Write out [for us a list of all] the existing yokes [of cattle], female and male, [and send the] list, so that we may pur[chase more] and send [them to you], [so that] they may be delivered quickly.

### 59167. A report about the harvest. — 0 m. 135 X 0 m. 095. — Date : about 3^rd^ April, 255 B. C.

Pasis the foreman of Zenon's farmers reports about the corn that has been carried to the threshing-floor from the 1^st^ to the 10^th^ of Mecheir.

->Report for [the month of]<-
->Mecheir regarding the<-
->harvested crops from Pasis<-
->the chief farmer <note>original: "ἀρχιγεωργοῦ"</note><-
5 ->from the first of the month:<-
->donkeys <note>Refers to "donkey-loads" of grain.</note> 2,198<-
->from the 1st, 40 <term>mōeia</term><-
->until the 10th.<-

*Lines 4-5. The scribe misspelled "chief farmer" and "first of the month".*
*Line 6. "donkeys" : shorthand for "donkey loads".*
*Line 7. "mōeia" : See the note on this term in other papyri. It probably means a receptacle and may have come to be used as a measure of capacity.*

### 59168. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0 m. 175 x 0 m. 175. — Date of reception : 27^th^ April, 256 or 255 B. C.

Apollonios orders Zenon to build a temple of Sarapis <note>Sarapis was a Graeco-Egyptian deity whose cult was heavily promoted by the Ptolemaic kings to unify their subjects.</note> as a companion to that of Isis, giving details about its position with regard to other temples. I have restored the text provisionally, but the recovery of the missing part will probably throw new light on this interesting document. The date is uncertain, owing to Zenon's habit of assimilating the two calendars; and though I have inserted the letter here, it is just as probable that it belongs to year 29. In fact the double date of no. 59165 is in favour of the latter alternative.

Apollonios to Zenon, greetings. [Give orders to]
build [at the] Isieion <note>The temple of the goddess Isis.</note> a Sarapieion [beside the]
temple of the Dioskouroi <note>The "Divine Youths," Castor and Pollux.</note> and the [...]
[vacant space. Let it be your care]
5 that along the canal there be one [approach for both]
temples.
->Farewell. Year [<-

VERSO :

[Year ..., Dystr]os 4, Phamenoth 4.                     To Zenon.
[Apolloni]os regarding the Sarapieion.

Lines 3-4. Cf. no. 59169, 3-4. It is possible that here too the place in question was the site selected for the cult of the sovereigns.

BIBLIOGRAPHY : *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48615 ; P. Eᴅɢ., 91 ; Wɪʟᴄᴋᴇɴ, *Archive*, VIII, p. 66 ; *Collection of Greek Inscriptions from Egypt*, 6806.

### 59169. Fragment of a letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0 m. 16 x 0 m. 21. — Date not yet known.

This piece makes an interesting pendant to no. 59168 and I have therefore placed it

<column-break/>

here, though its date is for the present uncertain. It probably belongs to the early period of Zenon's residence at Philadelphia when the town was being laid out and embellished. A distinguished visitor called Antikritos was coming to look round, and Apollonios asks Zenon to show him the chief points of interest and to impress on him that they have only lately began to found the settlement.

Apollonios to Zenon, greeting[s.
Antikritos (is coming to see) both the village [
and the place where we intend [to set up
the statues or altars] of the King and of Phila[delphos <note>Referring to Arsinoe II Philadelphia, the deceased wife and sister of Ptolemy II.</note>
5 and the approach and the [
and the embankments and [
and show him that [we have] recently [begun]
to build.
->Farewell.<-

VERSO :

10                     To Zenon.

Line 1. E. g., *as soon as he arrives*.
Line 2. E. g., *show him everything*.
Lines 3-4. E. g., *to set up the ... statues or altars*. But as one or two other texts mention an Arsinoeion <note>A temple dedicated to the deified Arsinoe II.</note> at Philadelphia, it may be that Apollonios is speaking of a projected temple or sacred precinct.
Line 7. E. g. *we have begun the village*.

BIBLIOGRAPHY : *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48616 ; P. Eᴅɢ., 92 ; Wɪʟᴄᴋᴇɴ, *Archive*, VIII, p. 66 ; *Collection of Greek Inscriptions from Egypt*, 6807.

### 59170. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0 m. 17 x 0 m. 275. — Date : April or May, 255 B. C.

Apollonios announces that he has sent from the Heliopolite nome a hundred jars of wine which Zenon is to sell at a good price, using the proceeds to pay for the weaving of carpets. He wishes to know how the wine sells and whether he ought to send more, as he still has plenty of it in the aforesaid nome. It is probable that this was a private commercial transaction on the part of Apollonios, the wine being either grown or bought by him and the carpets being those produced by his factory at Memphis (see *P. S. I.*, 854 and no. 59080). But I do not see any clear evidence for Rostovtzeff's suggestion that Zenon was at this time farming the taxes on wine over a large part of the country and that Apollonios was the capitalist in the background (*Large Estate*, p. 103). The papyrus is badly decayed.

[A]pollonios to Zenon, gree[tings]. We have sent [to you from the]

This page transitions into logistical matters concerning the sale of wine, the reception of official delegations, and the technical specifics of infrastructure maintenance (roads and canals) managed by local contractors.</meta>

<header>CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM.</header>

[...from the] Heliopolite nome for the sale of 100 jars <note>original: "κεράμια"</note> of wine. [See to it then]
[that] it is sold at a favorable price <note>original: "τοῦ καλῶς ἔχοντος", literally 'at that which holds well'</note>, and [give the proceeds]
[to] the shaggy rugs <note>original: "ἀμφιτάπους", a type of expensive, heavy fabric or rug finished on both sides.</note>. Write to us how [...]
5 him to give back and [...] to send you further [...]
for we have more wine in the Heliopolite [nome].
Farewell. Year 30, Dystros [...].

->VERSO :<-
->To Zenon.<-

*Line 2.* "Jars" <note>original: "κερ"</note> is written as a monogram, as usual.
*Line 3.* "At a favorable price" <note>original: "τοῦ καλῶς ἔχοντος"</note> : ‘at a favorable price’. Compare no. 59053, 9.
*Line 4.* The letter after the first "t" looks like "o", but might be "e". Perhaps "you estimate" <note>original: "τοπάζεις"</note>, or "you have obtained" <note>original: "τέτευχας"</note>.
*Line 5.* For example, "and [if we should] send you [more] in addition."
*Line 6.* "More" <note>original: "πλείω"</note> : compare no. 59054, 35, note. The practice of dropping the final "n" in this class of words was prevalent even among the best scribes.
*Line 8.* There may have been a docket <note>A brief filing note on the outside of the scroll.</note>, but both ends of the papyrus are destroyed.

BIBLIOGRAPHY : Journal d’entrée du Musée, no. 48617; P. Edg., 29; ROSTOVTZEFF, Large Estate, pp. 103, 116; Sammelbuch, 6735.
</margin>

# 59171. Fragment of letter from Diotimos to Zenon. — 0 m. 13 x 0 m. 12. — Date of reception : 8^th May, B. C.

The writer is Diotimos the local governor <note>term: "ὑποδιοικητής" (hypodioiketes), a regional financial official subordinate to the central Finance Minister.</note>, whose authority in this district came next to that of Apollonios and who probably had several nomes in his charge (see *P. Edg.*, 37, introduction; Druffel in *Archiv*, VI, p. 30; WILCKEN, *Archiv*, VI, p. 452). He asks Zenon to accompany a certain person to Herakleopolis, probably a distinguished visitor (compare no. 59169) and possibly an ambassador from abroad.

> Diotimos to Zenon [greetings...]
> ...ambassador[s...]
> the new [one?]. You will [therefore] do well [to come to Herakleopolis]
> with him so that [...]

->VERSO :<-
5 Year 30, Phamenoth 15. <margin>To Zenon.</margin>
Diotimos, son of Zenon <note>Though addressed to Zenon, the docket identifies the writer as Diotimos.</note>

*Line 2.* "Ambassador" <note>original: "πρεσβευ["</note> : either an ambassador proper or someone on a mission from Alexandria. A papyrus in the British Museum mentions the visit of an ambassador, though not in the present year.
*Line 5.* Phamenoth 15: equal to 27^th Dystros according to the table. The new regnal year commenced just about this time, not later than the first of Xandikos.

<column-break/>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

> ...presence in Herakleopolis.

<margin>
BIBLIOGRAPHY : Journal d’entrée du Musée, no. 48618.
</margin>

# 59172. Memorandum to Zenon from Komoapis. — 0 m. 395 x 0 m. 165. — Date : 256 or 255 B. C.

Komoapis, who is known to have been an architect <note>term: "ἀρχιτέκτων" (architekton), a state engineer or public works overseer.</note> (compare no. 59109, 2), reports that he has given out to a number of separate contractors the work to be done on a road or canal, no doubt in the neighborhood of Philadelphia. There was a separate contract for each "chain" <note>term: "schoinion" (σχοινίον), a unit of length used for surveying, roughly 50–60 meters.</note> and the accepted tenders vary from 2 1/2 to 7 2/3 drachmæ, showing that some sections were more easily worked than others. As each contractor was guaranteed, it is probable that the money was paid in advance (compare no. 59137). The first nine "chains" had first been undertaken by Petechon, but he had apparently given up the work to smaller men. There is little doubt that the money was provided by Zenon and that the object of the work was the amelioration of the estate of which he was in charge.

> Memorandum to Zenon from Komoapis.
> Regarding the work from the middle road
> <margin>previously</margin>
> the upper parts toward the south, which Petechon
> took as a contract, the nine chains: first chain,
> 5 Pais son of Panis, from Troe, guarantor Pasis son of Paraus
> the one in charge of the stone-transport boat <note>original: "λιθηγοῦ", referring to a vessel for carrying stone.</note>, 7 drachmae; second, Arentotes son of
> Arioiris, guarantor Erieus, 6 drachmae and 2 obols; third, Arentotes son of Pasis,
> from Troe, guarantor Athemeus, 5 1/2 drachmae; fourth, Pakoimis son of
> Sinomounis, acting as his own guarantor, 3 drachmae; fifth, Petearmotis son of
> 10 Psintaes, guarantor Amos the stone-cutter, 7 drachmae; sixth, Pais son of
> Panis, from Troe, guarantor Pasis, 3 drachmae and 2 obols; seventh, Stotoetis son of
> Oseuris, acting as his own guarantor, 2 1/2 drachmae and 2 obols; eighth, Pais son of
> Panis, from Troe, guarantor Pasis, 2 1/2 drachmae; ninth, Petobastis son of
> Thotoeutis, from Aphroditopolis, guarantor Meieus the keeper of the temple of Isis,

*Line 3.* Petechon : compare *P. S. I.*, 571, 4. In the docket the report is said to come from Komoapis and Petechon.
*Line 5.* From Troe <note>original: "Τρωίτης"</note> : compare no. 59133, 8.
*Line 6.* "The stone-transport boat" <note>original: "τῆς λιθηγοῦ"</note> : understand "boat" <note>original: "βάρεως"</note>. In another text the phrase is given in full, "Pasis, pilot of the stone-transport boat."
*Line 6.* "Ari-" <note>original: "Ἀρι-"</note> : or "Ario-", but the dot which might be an "o" is probably accidental.
*Line 9.* "Acting as his own guarantor" <note>original: "αὐτεγγύωι"</note> : the adverbial form <note>original: "αὐτεγγύως"</note>, as in line 27, would be more grammatical.
*Line 14.* "Keeper of the temple of Isis" <note>original: "Ἰσιονόμος"</note> : compare *P. Petr.*, III, 82 and *P. Magd.*, 9, 1. See also OTTO, *Priester und Tempel*, II, p. 175, note 2.

### **59173.** Duplicate contract about a money loan. — 0 m. 18 $\times$ 0 m. 16 and 0 m. 24 $\times$ 0 m. 23. — Date: 25th May, 255 or 254 B.C. (plate VIII).

The contract states that Zenon had lent six farmers 10 drachmae each and three others 8 drachmae each, to buy donkeys for the purpose of carrying in their crops. The 
<meta>continuity: The description of the farmers and the terms of the contract continue on the next page.</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>
<sig>4.</sig>

farmers were Egyptians who rented land on the estate of Apollonios at Philadelphia and the money was to be repaid before the end of Pachons <note>An Egyptian month, roughly spanning late April to late May.</note> along with the rent <note>original: "ἐκφορίωι", referring to rent paid in kind or produce.</note>, or else a donkey, approved by Zenon, was to be handed over instead of it. In case of default Zenon could exact restitution from them and their guarantees "as in the case of debts to the Crown" <note>original: "ὡς πρὸς βασιλικά", a legal clause indicating that the debt would be treated with the same severity as money owed to the royal treasury.</note>. Each farmer is guaranteed by a friend and it is noticeable that one of these is a Greek. Then follow the names of the six witnesses, four Greeks and two Egyptians, one of the latter being the town clerk of Philadelphia.

The inner text, as is often the case, is much corrected. I give it as it stands, without restoration on the left side, for it is difficult to restore the corrections which the missing part probably contained. The back <note>original: "verso"</note> is blank, the middle portion, where the names of the witnesses usually stand, being lost. As Pharmouthi 2nd <note>Another Egyptian month, roughly March/April.</note> occurred twice in regnal year 31, it is not quite certain whether the contract belongs to 255 or 254 B.C.; but as the scribe in line 10 does not say ‘Pachons of year 32’, the former date is slightly more probable.

] Year 31, under the priest Glaukon son of Eteo[kles]
] and Berenike daughter of Nikanor in Philadelphi[a]
] 2nd of Pharmouthi. Zenon son of Agreophon lent 
] in the <gloss>31st year</gloss> <gloss>10,000 arouras</gloss> of land in Philadelphia given as a gift <note>original: "dorea"</note> to Apollonios by t[he King]
<margin>5</margin> to Sarois son of Petemenophris
] son of Oseuris from Meia, [[to Erieus son of Erieus]] from Hypsel[e],
] son of ...anchis from Temnauthis, to Arendotes son of Phanephios
] ... being six in number, [[to each for the price of a beast of burden]]
<gloss>[[to others]]</gloss>
] ... ten drachmae, total 60 drachmae, and to Pasi son of Semnephthos of Letopolis,
<gloss>likewise</gloss>
] ... son of ...ais of Hypselokome, being 3, at 8 drachmae each | 24 drachmae
] [repayment] shall be made along with the rent or an acceptable beast of burden
<margin>10</margin> ] [by the month of P]achons. If they do not return it according to the writ[ten terms]
] let the right of execution belong to Zenon from both themselves [and their guarantors]

*Line 1. Glaukon:* the brother of Chremonides the Athenian. It was known already that he took refuge in Egypt after the war, and we learn from this text that he was an honoured member of the court.
*Line 3. 2nd:* possibly Pharmouthi 2nd was written twice by mistake.
*Line 4. ]is:* either a dative plural or an aorist participle, such as "the [one who was added]" <unclear>proslatheis</unclear>. — *Apol :* written in monogram. — *Meias:* a village in the Memphite district <note>original: "nome"</note> (compare no. 59270, 4). — *Hypseles:* see the note for no. 59292, 375.
*Line 5. Temnauthis:* see no. 59292, 237 and often <note>original: "et sæpe"</note>.
*Line 10. "Under-written"* <note>original: "ὑπογεγραμμένα"</note> is sometimes used, as here, where we should have expected "above-written" <note>original: "προγεγραμμένα"</note>. I have noticed a curious instance in an unpublished text: "the one who has been under-written above for you."

<summary>This page catalogs a list of labor payments for canal construction near Philadelphia and a formal contract where Zenon lends money to Egyptian farmers to purchase donkeys for the harvest. These documents illustrate the daily financial management of a massive Ptolemaic estate, including the use of guarantors and the blending of Greek and Egyptian legal practices.</summary>

### 59174. Fragment of letter from Sosos to Zenon. — 0 m. 085 X 0 m. 095.

--- Date of reception: 29th May, 255 B. C.

Sosos, a business agent of Zenon or of Apollonios, is often mentioned in the papyri and is the author of *P. Mich.*, 45 (*Ægyptus*, IV, p. 79) and *P. S. I.*, 362.

->Sosos to Zenon, greetings [<-
->that silver is available the [<-
->...later however the [<-
->we gave back in the [<-
<margin>5</margin> ->...to Dionysios the banker . [<-

Lines 4-5. [Diony]sios: perhaps the person mentioned in *P. S. I.*, 362, 5 in conjunction with Promethion, who was himself a banker at Mendes.

### 59176. Daily account of receipts and expenses. — Breadth about 0.12 m. — Date : July, 255 B. C. (pl. IX).

This long papyrus, described on the back as daily journals <note>original: "ἐφημερίδες"</note>, gives an account of receipts and expenses, entered and balanced day by day, for a period of nearly a month. It is written on the front only, across the fibers, and the entries have been checked. Possibly the roll was cut in two along its length and the account of each month written on a narrow strip. This at least is suggested by the fact that the title is inscribed on the back at the end of the month; but the condition of the papyrus does not allow us to assert that the scribe did not first use one half of the roll and then continue his account on the other half without cutting it in two. We may assume, however, that what we have here is only a fragment of an account continued from month to month. *P. Wis.*, no. 1 (published in *Journal of Egyptian Archaeology*, IX, p. 81) is in a different hand, as Westermann tells me, but belongs to a similar series. The expenses are of many kinds, the most prominently recurring item being the wages of workmen employed in the construction of a bridge; but taking them all in all

---

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

we may say without hesitation that they are the expenses of a private business rather than of a Government office. I assume therefore that the money was spent by Zenon or one of his subordinates in connection with the estate. One would expect then that the receipts by which the expenses were met day by day would be of the same nature, that is to say would represent money due to the estate. But such entries as for the beer tax <note>original: "εἰς τὸν Φόρον τῆς ζυτηρᾶς", a common Ptolemaic monopoly tax</note> and for the pomegranate tax for the month of Pachon <note>original: "σιδικῆς εἰς τὸν Παχώνς"</note> almost certainly refer to Government taxes, and it is at least doubtful whether some of the other items represent private receipts or payments to Government. I can see only one explanation of this apparent contradiction. We learn from no. 59206 that some Government taxes were collected by Apollonios’ private agents from the inhabitants of his estates. It seems then that the author of the present account used some of the money thus received for his current expenses, though of course he would give receipts for it and the estate would remain responsible for it to the Treasury. The theory put forward by H. Maspero, *Finances of Egypt*, p. 23, that in the case of land held as a gift-estate <note>original: "ἐν δωρεαῖς"</note> the revenue from taxes and monopolies went into the private purse of the lord of the gift-estate <note>original: "δωρεά"</note> does not seem to be entertainable, so far at least as Egypt is concerned.

---

> ———————————————————
> for the same [account], in copper [drachmae] <note>original: "χα[λκοῦ"</note>
> amount overspent [
> 7th [day], from the retailers <note>original: "μεταβόλων"</note> from
<margin>5</margin> > cucumber [sales] 2 drachmae <note>The symbol ⊢ represents the drachma</note>
> from Nikanor from the bath-house ...
> *several lines missing*
> | 4 drachmae, amount overspent [1 drachma]
> 8th [day], from the retailers of cucumbers, 2 drachmae
> 9th [day], from Nikanor from the bath-house, 2 drachmae —
<margin>10</margin> > from the retailers from
> cucumbers, 2 drachmae
> | 6 drachmae —

Line 3. from the retailers from cucumber [sales] <note>original: "παρὰ τῶν μεταβόλων ἀπὸ σικύου"</note> : the retailers pay two drachmae a day on the sale of cucumbers, but it is not clear what this payment represents. It might possibly be the payment for a certain quantity of cucumbers provided by the estate. On the other hand in *P. S. I.*, 402 a seller of lentils in Philadelphia complains to the manager <note>original: "œconome"</note> in Krokodilopolis that owing to the popularity of pumpkins he is not able to sell his lentils and pay the Government dues; and I think it is more probable that in the present case the two drachmae represent the Tax <note>original: "Φόρος"</note> paid to Government.
Line 5. from the bath-house <note>original: "ἀπὸ βαλανείου"</note> : here again the money might be income from a privately owned bath leased to an individual (*P. S. I.*, 377, 2 and 584, 10) or payment of a Government tax (*P. Hib.*, 108, 7).

<summary>This page analyzes a daily financial journal (ephemeris) from Zenon's archive, documenting receipts from cucumber sales and bath-house fees. It highlights the complex relationship between private estate management and the collection of royal taxes like those on beer and produce.</summary>

*Line 15.* Perhaps 4 drachmae [— 1 obol], which would be the same rate of exchange as in line 182.
*Line 17.* For the general meaning compare lines 214-275. Perhaps **in the white** [quarry].
*Line 20.* Uncertain whether **Temple of Isis** <note>original: "Ἰσείον"</note> or a personal name.
*Line 29.* The months are **Artemisios** (Macedonian) and **Pachons** (Egyptian), and the alignment, if we take Apollonios' double dates as the standard, is only approximately right. Later on in the account, the Macedonian month is quietly dropped.
*Lines 29-30.* The ends of these two lines have suffered during the mounting of the papyrus and the phrase **for the beer tax** is now illegible. But I am sure that my first reading was right. **For the tax of the beer-shop** is no doubt equivalent to **for the tax of the beer-selling** in line 206 and both phrases refer to the Government tax.

---

*Lines 38-40.* The *pourers* may have been employed in some ceremony in connection with the *libations* (P. Par., I, 75). This festival, called also the **Neilaia** (Festival of the Nile), was celebrated about the time of the summer solstice, but not of course on a fixed date in the moving Egyptian calendar. We learn now that in 255 B.C. the celebration took place on or shortly before the 10th of Pachons, perhaps on the 9th, on which day no money was paid out (line 28), or about four days after the solstice. In 215 A.D. it took place later in the year. The corresponding modern festival, the *Leilet en Noqta* (Night of the Drop), is still held, though the date has shifted.
*Line 40.* This gives us a rough idea of the price of beer.
*Line 46.* This line has been added. For the word "threshing floor," see Mayser's Grammar.
*Lines 47-49.* Probably the sum was to be deducted from his wages.

*Line 64. The unapproved currency is not included in the total. It may have been returned to the bank when Ephesos went next to Krokodilopolis.*
*Line 69. Asynchis: written as such, not Apynchis.*
*Line 70. This line has been rewritten and the next line added.*
*Line 73. Pachons: the dates in lines 80-91 indicate that Pachons was the current month, and it appears therefore that the wages of the regular workmen were paid in advance. The stone-cutters received eight drachmae a month (line 93), so the four drachmae mentioned in line 74 must be the wage for the second half. For less expert labor, one obol a day was the usual wage.*

### 59177. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0.235 m x 0.09 m and 0.23 m x 0.09 m. — Date: 10th July, 255 B.C.

In this fragmentary letter Apollonios <note>Apollonios was the Dioiketes, the chief financial minister of Egypt and Zenon’s employer.</note> orders Zenon to send him samples of various crops which he had been growing at Philadelphia.

> Apollonios to Zenon, greeting. Send me
> a sample of both the barley and the wheat,
> loading them onto a boat, both of the good
> grain and of the [other?], and likewise
> <margin>5</margin> of chickpeas, both the Byzantine variety and the
> local kind, and of the best and worst flax <term>original: "στυππείου" - hemp or flax fibers used for making rope or cloth</term>
> from each [plot].
> Farewell. Year 31, Daisios 2, Pachon 18.

<summary>This page concludes a daily ledger of income from retail sales and taxes, and records a high-level command from the finance minister Apollonios requesting crop samples from his estate at Philadelphia. The documents highlight the diversity of agricultural experiments on the estate, including "Byzantine" chickpeas and various grades of flax.</summary>
<keywords>Zenon Papyri, Apollonios, Philadelphia, Ptolemaic Egypt, wool tax, cucumber sales, bridge construction, crop samples, flax, chickpeas, Byzantine agriculture</keywords>

### 59179. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0 m. 35 X 0 m. 265. — Date : 24th July, 255 B. C. (pl. X).

Apollonios writes to Zenon on the subject of certain vineyards which formed part of his <term>dorea</term> <note>original: "δωρεά" — a large "gift-estate" granted by the King to high-ranking officials like Apollonios, intended to bring new land into cultivation</note> in the Memphite nome <note>A "nome" was an administrative district or province in Ancient Egypt.</note>, but which certain <term>cleruchs</term> <note>original: "κληροῦχοι" — military settlers who were granted land allotments by the state in exchange for their service</note> there claimed as their property. The case had been sent for trial before a judge called Krataimenes. We do not know where he held his court, but we are told that the cleruchs failed to put in an appearance. In consequence of this Apollonios had written two letters, of which he sends copies to Zenon for his guidance. One is addressed to Krataimenes, asking him to give an order that the crop should be kept under watch, and the other is to Paramonos, perhaps an overseer of the Memphite <term>dorea</term>, instructing him to see that the crop is kept safe after being gathered in. We may infer from the text that the judge had not yet given a definite decision and that the trial had been postponed owing to the absence of the cleruchs.

> Apollonios to Zenon, greetings. Regarding the 
> vineyards in dispute with 
> the cleruchs in the Memphite district, 
> I have written to Krataimenes and Paramonos, 
> 5 and I have sent you the copies of the 
> letters to them, so that you may be informed.
> -> Farewell. Year 31, Daisios 16, Payni 2. <-
> Apollonios to Krataimenes, greetings. Since the

<column-break/>

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

> opposing parties <note>original: "οἱ συνταξάμενοι" — literally "those who had agreed" to appear</note> did not meet for the judgment 
> 10 regarding the disputed vineyards, 
> you will do well to order that the crops 
> be kept under watch. 
> -> Farewell. <-
> To Paramonos. Since the cleruchs who have entered 
> 15 upon the land given to us by the King did not 
> meet for the judgment before Krataimenes, 
> let it be your concern that the crops, 
> once gathered, are kept safe.
> -> Farewell. <-

VERSO :
-> To Zenon. <-

Line 9. <term>those who agreed to appear</term> <note>original: "οἱ συνταξάμενοι"</note> : ‘those who agreed to appear’. Or perhaps ‘those acting with us’ <note>original: "οἱ [ἡμῖν]"</note>.
Line 10. Added afterwards.
Line 17. ‘The crops which’ <note>original: "τὰ γενήμα[τα τά]"</note> is also possible.

<meta>BIBL. : Journal d’entrée du Musée, no. 48625; P. Edg., 99; Sammelbuch, 6814.</meta>

### **59182. Contract.** — 0 m. 285 x 0 m. 24. — Date : 4th October, 255 B. C.

Three fragments of a duplicate contract attested by six witnesses. One of the fragments, comprising the left side of the extant portion of the inner text and two columns of the names on the verso, is in the British Museum (Inventory no. 2344), and I am indebted to Mr. Bell for permission to copy and publish it. 
A number of peasants acknowledge that they have received from Zenon an advance of money, at the rate of two drachmæ <note>The drachma was the standard silver or bronze coin of the Ptolemaic period.</note> for each <term>aroura</term> <note>original: "ἄρουρα" — a unit of land measurement equal to about 0.6 acres</note>, for the cutting of the brush-wood on the land which they have taken up in basin 4 of the Philadelphian estate. 
The text is dated Mesore 14 and it seems to be stipulated (but this passage is mutilated and the restoration not obvious, at least to me) that the money is to be returned on the completion of the work to Zenon's satisfaction by the 1st of Thoth. 
If the peasants fail to fulfill their contract, they become liable to a fine of one and a half times <note>original: "ἡμιόλιον" (hemiolion) — a standard penalty in Greek contracts requiring the debtor to pay back the original amount plus fifty percent.</note> the amount of the money. Perhaps this more elaborate form of deed was now being used in place of the small duplicate receipts for advances, of which so many have been found dating from year 29 (e.g., no. 59127). 
A small lump of clay with three fragmentary seal impressions was found attached to the fragment in the British Museum.

[In the reign of Ptolemy, son of Ptolemy Soter] Year 31, under the priest Glaukon son of Eteokles of Alexander and the Gods <term>Adelphoi</term> <note>original: "Θεῶν Ἀδελφῶν" — The "Sibling Gods," referring to the deified Ptolemy II and his wife-sister Arsinoe II.</note>,
[under the basket-bearer of Arsinoe Philadelphos, Berenike daughter of Nikano]r, the 24th of the month Loios, and the 14th of the Egyptian month Mesore, in Philadelphia of the [Arsinoite]
[nome. So-and-so and ]ris and Etpheus and Nechtheneibis and Petenouris, the five brothers, Sebennytians <note>original: "Σεβεννῦται" — residents or natives of Sebennytos, a city in the Nile Delta.</note>

*Line 2. Loios 24, Mesore 14* : compare the concordances in nos. 59165, 59176, 59184.
*Line 3.* ]ris : possibly Onnophris (see line 26).

*Line 4. Horos* : sigma written over upsilon.
*Line 5.* The missing part was probably deleted likewise.
*Line 6. Psenobastis* : see no. 59173, where he appears again as a farmer on the estate.
*Line 7.* For example, <note>original: "δάνειον εἰς κάτεργον ὥστε τούτους κόψαι" — a loan for labor so that these men might cut.</note>
*Line 8. return it* in this context should naturally mean 'return the money', and in fact in the receipts of year 29 the money is often specified as a <note>original: "δάνειον" — a loan.</note>. On the other hand *satisfactorily* would go more suitably with some such phrase as <note>original: "ἐφ' ὧι συντελέσουσιν" — on the condition that they complete [the work]</note> than with *they shall return* or *let them return*, and perhaps it qualifies *to cut*. It cannot well be a question of returning the land.

## 59186. Letter from Onnophris to Zenon. — 0.30 m x 0.105 m. — Date of reception: October 17, 255 B.C. (plate XI).

Onnophris reports that on receiving Zenon's letter he has gone fowling <note>The practice of catching wild birds, usually with nets or traps</note> with Eutychides but up to the present has not been able to send him more than thirty teal <note>original: "κερκήρεις" — a type of small water-bird</note> and twenty larger birds. The fragment containing lines 16-19 was recovered too late to be shown in the photograph.

<margin>Line 4. *I went down*: probably to the shores of the lake <note>original: "κατέβην"</note>.</margin>

Nikon, probably the author of no. 59160, though the present letter is not in the same hand, writes that he or his men have received no allowance of table-oil or lamp-oil <note>original: "κίκι" — castor oil, which was commonly used in Egypt for lamps</note> for some time and goes on to speak of the damage that is being done in his district.

## 59188. Memorandum from Otearmotes. — 0.20 m x 0.22 m. — Date of reception: November 14, 255 B.C.

Otearmotes asks that instructions be given to Zenon to measure the land in Tapeptia with the <term>measuring-cord</term> <note>original: "σχοινίωι" — a schoinion, a rope of standard length used for surveying</note> used in the Memphite district and also that an order be given about the sycamore boat in accordance with a previous agreement.

It is not clear whether the memorandum was addressed to Zenon or to some person of higher authority. If, as I believe, it was addressed to Zenon directly, then "Zenon" in line 2 must refer to another man of the same name, perhaps Zenon the son of Panakestor. Tapeptia is presumably identical with Taptia (no. 59187, 4), which lay on the river, either in the Memphite or a neighboring district. The writing is along the fibers and the papyrus is folded vertically.

->Memorandum from Otearmotes.<-
->To give orders to Zenon to<-
->measure the land in Tapeptia<-
->with the measuring-cord which is also used in the Memphite [district...]<-

<margin>Line 4. *with the measuring-cord*: compare the dispute about local standards of measure in no. 59132.</margin>

### 59189. Fragment of a petition from Seleukos to Zenon. — 0.085 m x 0.145 m. — Date of reception: November 16, 255 B.C.

### 59191. Letter from Artemidoros to Zenon. — (*a*) 0.05 m x 0.09 m; (*b*) 0.17 m x 0.085 m. — Date: December 6, 255 B.C. (plate XI).

The fragment (*a*), containing the first two lines of the letter and the record-note on the back, is in Florence and has been published previously. That the fragment (*b*) belongs to the same text is self-evident.
The writer is no doubt the Artemidoros whom we have supposed to be an agent of Apollonios at Memphis (see no. 59149), while the "young girls" <note>original: "παιδίσκαι" — can mean young female slaves or household servants</note> may be the women of whom Apollonios speaks elsewhere. ### 59192. Letter from Platon to Zenon. — 0.19 m x 0.34 m. — Date: 16th December, 255 B. C. (Plate XII).

Platon writes that the youth called Demetrios who is delivering the letter has a father living in the Fayoum and would like to find some work there himself; he asks Zenon to give him employment of some sort and to look after him. He adds that he has sent Zenon two <term>artabs</term> <note>A dry measure of capacity, roughly 30 to 40 liters</note> of chick-peas and will try to buy some more at Naukratis and bring them up the river himself.
As Platon dates by the Macedonian calendar and speaks of coming up the river and visiting Naukratis, it is evident that the letter was written in Alexandria. From the present text and from no. 59217 we may infer that he divided his time between the capital and the Fayoum and that he had some property in the latter district.

Platon to Zenon, greetings. As it seems, the father of Demetrios, the one delivering this letter to you, happens to be spending his time in the <margin>3</margin> Arsinoite <term>nome</term> <note>The Fayoum region</note>. The young man himself, therefore, wishes to do <margin>4</margin> something there. 
Hearing that you are a fair man, some of my friends asked me to write

<note>Line 3. To do: original: "πράττειν" (prattein). The editor notes that if Platon was not from Athens, he was at least an "Atticist," meaning he used a refined, classical style of Greek.</note>
<note>Line 4. Either there is a break in the sentence structure, or the word "hearing" is a grammatical slip by the author.</note>

<column-break/>

<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>
<margin>5</margin> on his behalf, so that you might station him somewhere with you. You will do <note>original: "κατατάξῃς" — meaning to assign a rank or position</note> well, then, by doing us a favor and seeing to it that he does something—whatever you deem to be suitable—and looking after his other needs, if indeed he is useful to you.
<insert>from Sosos</insert>
<margin>8</margin> And as a sign [of my regard], I have sent you 2 <term>artabs</term> of "ram" chick-peas <note>original: "ἐρεβίνθου κριοῦ" — A large variety of chick-pea so-called because the seed resembles a ram's head</note> bought at 5 drachmae each.
I will also try to buy more for you from Naukratis, if there are any, up to <margin>10</margin> 10 [artabs?], and bring them upriver to you myself.
->Farewell.<-
->Year 31, month of Dios 12.<- <note>Dios was a month in the Macedonian calendar</note>

VERSO (BACK OF THE PAPYRUS): ->To Zenon.<-

<note>Line 8. From Sosos: perhaps meaning "which I got from Sosos."</note>
