### 59193. Letter to Zenon. — 0.18 m x 0.335 m. — Date: 21st December, 255 B. C.

The writer gives Zenon some instructions about a house which was being built for him. As the courtyard was too small and a stable had not been included, the architect at his request was going to increase the length of the site by twenty cubits. He asks Zenon to give orders to this effect and to begin work at once. They will not be delayed by lack of wood. The architect has been ordered to leave a space for a garden round the house and to change the position of the gateway and bakery.
The letter is written in the "chancery hand" (a professional administrative script) characteristic of the letters of Apollonios, but the length of the name and the Egyptian dating show that the author was not Apollonios himself. It is evident, however, that the author was an important man and I am inclined to think that he may have been Diotimos the <term>hypodioiketes</term> <note>A high-ranking deputy finance minister</note>, as there are one or two references in our papyri to a house that was being built or decorated for this official.
A few words practically illegible on the front of the papyrus have been deciphered from their ink imprints on the back.

[...]os to Zenon, greetings. I inspected the plan of <margin>2</margin> [the works, and it] seemed to me to be sufficient. <margin>3</margin> [...] the architect, having been requested by us...

<note>Line 2. For example, "of the works, which".</note>
<note>Line 3. The editor suggests some possible Greek reconstructions, but the damaged letters are too uncertain to be sure.</note>

### 59194. Letter from Demetrios to Zenon. — 0.15 m x 0.10 m. — Date of reception: 29th December, 255 B. C.

> Diotimos <note>A subordinate official to the finance minister</note> had sent word to Demetrios to give certain large jars <note>original: "βανωτοί" (banotoi)</note> to a messenger from Zenon, to enable him to receive the price for the month of Phaophi; Zenon had sent the messenger; and 410 jars had been handed over to him. Demetrios now asks Zenon to send him the money or, if not all of it, at least half. As the price of the jars was a <term>gold piece</term> <note>original: "χρυσοῦς"; a coin or unit of value worth twenty drachmae</note> for every twelve jars, the sum due was 683 drachmae and 2 obols.
> The circumstances of this transaction are not perfectly clear to me. Apparently Demetrios was under an agreement to supply Zenon with so many large jars per month. But the price is too high to allow us to suppose that he was a potter and that the large jars were empty; for in *P. Edg.*, 61 the price of empty jars <note>original: "κεράμια"</note> is stated to be ten drachmae per hundred. We are therefore obliged to suppose that the jars contained some product, such as cheap wine. As Diotimos the <term>assistant administrator</term> <note>original: "hypodioiketes"</note> was interested in the matter, it may be that Zenon was acting for the Government; or it may be that Demetrios was under an obligation to make monthly payments to the Government and raised the necessary money by selling his large jars through Zenon. It seems not unlikely that Demetrios was the farmer of the <term>apomoira tax</term> <note>A tax on vineyards and orchards</note> who is mentioned in *P. Edg.*, 62. In *P. S. I.*, 425 we again find Zenon associated with Diotimos in the distribution of wine to the retailers in the <term>districts</term> <note>original: "nomes"</note>.
> The missing part of the text will probably be recovered, and the restorations given below are entirely provisional and do not pretend to do more than show the general meaning.

<column-break/>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

Demetrios to Zenon, greetings. I wrote [to you because] 
Diotimos ordered [the delivery of large jars] to your 
messenger so that we might receive the price for Phaophi. [You therefore sent] 
to me The[ot]eles, writing to give him [so that we might receive] 
<margin>5</margin> the price. You will therefore do well to [send to me as quickly as possible with] 
the one delivering this letter [the price for these, and] 
especially the whole amount now, for [the accounting is at hand], 
but if it is not possible, at least half. [The remainder we will give] 
in the month of Hathyr, just as you wrote to us. [The price for the 410 jars] 
<margin>10</margin> at 12 per gold piece is 683 drachmae and 2 obols.

VERSO (BACK OF THE PAPYRUS):
->Demetrios regarding<span style="display:inline-block; width:80px"></span> To Zenon.<-
-><gloss>five hundred</gloss><-
->of the large jars.<-
->Year 31, Hathyr 5.<- <note>January 21, 254 B.C.</note>

<note>Line 4. Possibly the name is Theoteles.</note>
<note>Line 10. "12 per gold piece": see document no. 59022. "Gold piece" (chrysous) was a current term for a sum of twenty silver drachmae, or even for a weight of twenty drachmae (P. Lille, I, 6, 13).</note>
<note>Line 12. "Five hundred": probably the total number of jars which Demetrios was expected to supply.</note>

<meta>BIBLIOGRAPHY: Museum Entry Journal, no. 48640.</meta>

### 59195. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon and Panakestor. — 0.225 m x 0.18 m. — Date: December, 255 B. C. or January 254 B. C.

> Apollonios <note>The finance minister of Egypt under Ptolemy II</note> informs his two agents that he has sent Maron to take charge of the Milesian sheep. They are to hand over the sheep and all the material and introduce him to the local shepherds who will be under his orders. Four of the boys belonging to the estate are to be given to him to learn his methods.
> For Apollonios' flock of Milesian sheep <note>Milesian sheep were famous for their exceptionally fine wool</note> see *P. Edg.*, 107, introduction; Wilcken, *Hellenistische Wirtschaft*, p. 107; and compare Rostovtzeff, *Large Estate*, p. 180.

[Apoll]onios to Zenon and Panakest[or, greetings]. 
[We have sent] Maron to super[vise] 
[the] Milesian sheep. [Therefore,] hand over [the sheep] 
to him and [the sheep-fold] 
<margin>5</margin> [and if] any other equipment exists, [to the...]

<note>Line 2. Or perhaps a phrase like "being an expert." All the above reconstructions are only suggestions to show the general sense of the letter.</note>

### 59199. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0.285 m x 0.305 m. — Date: 29th April, 254 B. C.

One of the brewers in the <term>Arsinoite nome</term> <note>the administrative district of the Fayoum oasis</note> has offered to take over the beer-house in the village of Philadelphia. He has committed to brewing a supply of barley provided by the State—amounting to twelve <term>artabs</term> <note>a dry measure roughly equal to 40 liters</note> a day—to the profit of the Crown. Zenon is therefore ordered to draw up a contract, obtain a written oath from him, and hand over the brewery. However, Zenon must also install a reliable collector to supervise the finances. Furthermore, the current brewer must settle his accounts for the time he has managed the business.

Another document (P. Michigan 3102) is a letter from Apollonios on the same subject, which states "that the brewer says he has agreed with us for eleven artabs" <note>original: "ὅτι ὁ ζυτοποιὸς ἕνδεκα ἀρτάβας φάσκει συντετάχθαι πρὸς ἡμᾶς"</note>. It is clear that "has agreed" <note>original: "συντετάχθαι"</note> here means exactly that; and it may be that "agreement" <note>original: "σύνταξιν"</note> in this text refers to a specific agreed-upon quantity rather than a state subsidy, as some scholars have suggested. In any case, the general meaning is that he undertakes to process the produce of twelve artabs of barley daily. The manufacture of beer was strictly controlled by the State, which provided a set amount of barley and demanded a "rent" or "tax" <note>original: "φόρος" (phoros)</note> proportional to the amount supplied. Some historians suppose that the "reliable collector" <note>original: "πιστολογευτής" (pistologuetes)</note> mentioned here took over all the money the brewery made and paid it into the Treasury, with the brewer later receiving a share of the leftover profit. However, it seems more likely that only the required tax was paid into the collection office <note>original: "λογευτήριον" (logeuterion)</note> in regular payments. I imagine instead that the brewer was free to brew as much as he could—using his own money and for his own profit—once he had fulfilled the initial amount required by the Crown.

<sig>8.</sig>

### **59200. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon.** — 0.165 m $\times$ 0.26 m. — Date: 20th May, 254 B.C.

Zenon is ordered to erect a building of some sort at Philadelphia and the architect is to see that it is properly equipped. The name of the building (see the gap at the beginning of line 2) was of five or six letters. It is probably preserved in the note of dispatch on the back, which I have transcribed as "colonnade" (stoas), though the word is not very legible. If this reading is right, we have here another indication of the care which Apollonios was giving to the beautification of his own little town.

->[. . . . . . ] it is [properly] equipped.<-

### **59201. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon.** — 0.145 m $\times$ 0.33 m. — Date: 22nd May, 254 B.C.

A note from Apollonios the finance minister (dioiketes) to say that he will shortly send Apollonios the contractor to Zenon. This contractor (ergolabos) may possibly be the Apollonios who appears in the Petrie papyri as a contractor in connection with the quarries (see ROSTOVTZEFF, *Large Estate*, pp. 162-164, where it is wrongly argued that the contractor was the finance minister himself).

### **59202. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon.** — 0.20 m $\times$ 0.34 m. — Date: 23rd May, 254 B.C.

Zenon has done right to arrest the treasurer attached to the beer-shop. Apollonios has sent Amenneus the brewer to Philadelphia in order that the treasurer may be confronted with him and convict him of the charges which he has brought against him. The case is to be tried before Peton the judge (chrematistes), who is coming specially for this and for another affair (see nos. 59203, 59204). If Amenneus appears to have really spoken as reported, Apollonios threatens that he will have him hanged. From the date of reception it appears that Apollonios was staying in the neighborhood, perhaps at Krokodilopolis. The case came under his jurisdiction, and though he sent Peton to examine it, he evidently meant to pronounce judgment himself. It is surprising to find a circuit judge acting as a mere agent of the finance minister. Amenneus may possibly have been the previous occupant of the brewery at Philadelphia (see no. 59199).

### 59203. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0.25 m x 0.34 m. — Date: 23rd May, 254 B.C.

Apollonios writes that he has sent Zenon a copy of the letter which he has just written to the native peasants in Hephaistias ordering them to appear at Philadelphia by day-break and not let Peton the judge <note>original: "chrematistes"</note> be detained. The copy is subjoined. In it he informs the peasants that he is too busy to hear the case himself but has sent Peton instead. They are therefore to meet him at Philadelphia early next morning in order that he may hear their pleadings and those of Sopatros the agent of Damis, so that, if the latter is proved to be guilty, he may receive the punishment he deserves.
The present case like that of the brewer came under the jurisdiction of Apollonios and he dealt with it in the same way, sending a judge to represent him and hear

---

<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>
<page-num>63</page-num>

the pleadings and then pronouncing judgment himself. Damis appears as a joint district administrator <note>original: "nomarch"</note> with Etearchos in P. S. I., 518, and the probability is that his subordinate Sopatros had been acting oppressively towards the peasants. ROSTOVTZEFF, Large Estate, p. 80, supposes that the peasants belonged to the <term>gift-estate</term> <note>original: "δωρεά"</note>, that they had gone on strike, and that Damis was playing the part of a Government intermediary between them and Apollonios. It may be so, but there is nothing to prove this in the text itself, and after all the peasants may have been ordinary Crown peasants or may have been working on a military allotment <note>original: "cleruchy"</note>. The fact that Apollonios could employ Peton as his delegate and as a preliminary examiner compels us to revise our old conception of the functions of the Ptolemaic <term>judges</term> <note>original: "χρηματισταί"</note>. For the date Year 32 in the filing note see the note on no. 59202, line 11.

## 59204. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0.16 m x 0.34 m. — Date: 23rd May, 254 B.C.

As soon as Peton the <term>judge</term> <note>original: "chrematistes," a member of a royal circuit court</note> arrives in Philadelphia (see no. 59203), Zenon is to show him a lodging and give him supplies for one day. For after hearing the two cases he will return immediately to Apollonios.
From an unpublished papyrus (*P. Michigan*, 3139) we can infer that, though Peton was sent on a special mission by Apollonios, he was simply an ordinary member of the board of judges which was holding assize courts in this district. Wilcken <note>Ulrich Wilcken, a famous scholar of papyri</note> calls him an Egyptian; but is Peton (not Petos) an Egyptian name?

->Apollonios to Zenon, greetings. As soon as<-
->Peton the judge arrives, show him<-
->a lodging with you and give him the necessities for one<-
->day. For after he has heard the cases of the<-
->5 people from Hephaistias and of Amenneas, he will immediately return<-
->to us.<-
->Farewell. Year 31, Dystros 23, Phamenoth 30.<- <note>Double-dated according to the Macedonian and Egyptian calendars.</note>

->VERSO:<-

->Year 31, Pharmouthi 1.          To Zenon.<-
->Apollonios regarding<-
->10 Peton the judge.<-

*Line 5.* There is room for a few more letters at the end of the line, but nothing seems to be required.

## 59205. Fragment of letter. — 0.115 m x 0.145 m. — Date: 255-254 B.C.

The writer had previously addressed a letter to Kriton the <term>fleet commander</term> <note>original: "stolarch"</note> asking him to obtain a lodging for him in Philadelphia and to do something for his messenger Herakleides. But as he may have arrived before Kriton, he now sends Zenon a copy of the letter and asks him to be good enough to provide the lodging at once. Whether the present letter can be ascribed to Peton the <term>judge</term> (no. 59204) is very doubtful.

->] to you [the copy] of the letter which I wrote to Kriton [<-

*Lines 1-3.* I venture to suggest that the text ran somewhat as follows: [So-and-so] to Zenon greetings. I have sent you the copy of the letter which I wrote to Kriton. If it happens that he arrives after us, know that he will show the greatest concern for us by giving the small lodging etc.

## 59206. Account of tax-payments. — 0.20 m x 0.41 m. — Date: probably drawn up in the spring of 254 B.C. (plate XIII).

This fragmentary but interesting papyrus gives an account of the collection of certain taxes by Apollonios’ agents from tax-payers living on his estates. We cannot suppose with H. Maspero, *Finances of Egypt*, p. 23, that such taxes formed part of the private income of the great landlords. No doubt, like the beer-tax mentioned in no. 59199, they were eventually paid <term>into the royal treasury</term> <note>original: "eis to basilikon"</note>. But his agents were apparently charged with the duty of collecting them in the first instance; for we may assume that Panakestor (see line 44) was merely an intermediary and not a professional tax-farmer. The taxes mentioned here are: the <term>pasture-tax</term> <note>original: "ennomion"</note>; the <term>salt-tax</term> <note>original: "halika"</note>; the <term>bath-tax</term> <note>original: "balaneiou"</note>; the <term>natron-tax</term> <note>original: "nitrika"</note>, paid by the <term>fullers</term> <note>original: "grapheis," who used natron for cleaning clothes</note> at a monthly rate; the <term>quarter-tax on bakers</term> <note>original: "tetarte sitopoion"</note> and the <term>quarter-tax on pickled fish</term> <note>original: "tetarte tarichou"</note>, paid in the same way by the bakers and picklers. All these are well known, at least by name; for the two last compare in particular *P. Petr.*, III, p. 302. In addition to the above we find the <term>sales-taxes</term> <note>original: "eponia"</note>; in line 20 this seems to be listed as a separate tax, whereas in lines 45 and 63 it seems rather to be the general name for the particular taxes which follow, though it is difficult to see how the pasture-tax can be classed as a sales-tax. But this and various other points would be clearer if we had the complete text and knew by whom and for whom and with what purpose the account was drawn up. The front (recto) and column 3 of the back (verso) are written in a well-formed hand, while columns 1 and 2 of the back are in a thinner and more cursive writing. All the columns are incomplete.

->FRONT :          Column I.<-

->likewise] they have not been collected [<-
->] ... [<-
->] ... [<-

*Line 1.* Read "they have been collected."

<note>Lines 6-7: The fiscal year used in this account appears to begin in the month of Mecheir and end in Tybi.</note>
<note>Line 8: "As written" seems to be a comment referencing a previous ledger. Some deleted figures appear in the right margin.</note>
<note>Line 14: The total of 18 drachmas, while mathematically expected for the period, is physically difficult to read on the papyrus.</note>
<note>Line 19: This entry was struck through in the original text.</note>

<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

### 59207. Letter from Onnophris to Zenon. — 29.5 cm x 9 cm. — Date: 255 or 254 B.C.

Onnophris was one of the farmers or foremen whom Zenon employed to cultivate land, and more especially such land as he had taken over from the neighbouring military settlers or "cleruchs." He had asked Zenon for a cart and perhaps a camel to carry the crop on the holding of Ptolemaios, and he now presses his request. In the second part of the letter, he speaks about work to be done on the land of Theopompos. The text is very badly preserved; part of it I am unable to read and the remainder I have deciphered with difficulty and considerable doubt; but as it presents some points of interest, even this imperfect transcript seems worth printing.

&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; [O]nnophris to Zenon, greeting.
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; [I] was telling you even when I was present [about]
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; [a] cart, so that it might be given to me
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; [f]or the gathering of the harvest of
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; 5 Ptolemaios's allotment <note>original: "klerou," land granted to a soldier</note>
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; . . . . . . . . . [
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; . . the camel and [you have not]
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; sent it. And now
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; the sheaves <note>original: "dragmata," bundles of cut grain</note> are . . [
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; 10 . . . . . . . . . [
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; . . . . . . . . . [
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; and the cart [
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; [ . . . .] of Kerkion is idle
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; because it does not have . . [

The remaining 15 lines on the front of the papyrus are almost entirely undecipherable, except in one or two places. The letter is continued on the back:

&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; 30 &emsp; . . . . . I have written to Theu[pom]-
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; [p]os about it and he has taken no
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; account of it.
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; [Y]ou know that I have no one
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; who is . . . . . . . . . [

*Lines 5-7.* Perhaps "likewise also concerning the camel." This seems a clear instance of the camel being used for agricultural work in Egypt before the middle of the 3rd century BC. <note>Camels were relatively new to Egypt at this time, having been introduced more widely under the Ptolemies.</note>
*Line 13.* Perhaps Kerkion or Pataikion.
*Line 30.* Possibly Sosos.
*Lines 33-34.* One might read "we shall admit no one who is wise," but this does not sound quite right.

<summary>This page lists detailed tax accounts for salt, grazing, and baking from the villages of Apollonios, followed by a letter from a foreman named Onnophris. The letter is historically significant for its early mention of camels being used in Egyptian agriculture during the 3rd century BC.</summary>
<keywords>Zenon Papyri, Onnophris, salt tax, natron tax, pasture tax, harvest, camel, Ptolemy II, Philadelphia, agricultural logistics, cleruch allotment</keywords>

<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

**59209. Fragment of letter.** — 0.17 m. $\times$ 0.10 m. — Date: 26th May, 254 B.C.

Apparently an order to Zenon to prevent the escape of a certain tax collector <term>logeuetes</term>. Whether this was the treasurer of the brewery mentioned in document no. 59205 is doubtful. The letter is not in the hand of any of Apollonios' scribes, nor is it likely to have been written by Peton, who also used the Macedonian calendar; but it may be an order from the <term>oeconome</term> <note>A high-ranking financial administrator in the district.</note>. The date of Year 32, Pharmouthi 3 is rather puzzling. It corresponds to the Macedonian date Dystros 26. Assuming it to be a regnal date, we are obliged to infer that the new year began on the 25th or 26th, whereas no. 59139 seemed to show that it did not begin before the 27th.

->...of the collector, if indeed it is not possible<-
->...the wife and the brothers<-
->...for the guards to watch him and the guarantors<-
->...lest he himself escapes from the village unnoticed.<-
->5 Farewell. Year 32, Pharmouthi 3.<-

*Lines 1-2*. Zenon is apparently to arrest or examine the wife and brothers of the collector.

**BIBLIOGRAPHY:** *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48655; *Annals*, XVIII, p. 59.

### 59213. Letter from Ptolemaios to Zenon. — 0.12 m. x 0.11 m. and 0.14 m. x 0.115 m. — Date: about 15th July, 254 B.C. (plate XIV).

Ptolemaios asks Zenon to recover and hand over to the bearer a horse which a slave of his had run away with and sold. From the text and the Macedonian date, we get the impression that Ptolemaios was a man of some standing, perhaps a military officer, resident in the countryside <term>chora</term> <note>The "chora" referred to the land of Egypt outside the Greek cities like Alexandria.</note>.

->Ptolemaios to Zenon<-
->greetings. We hear<-
->that the boy <note>A common term for a slave servant.</note> from<-
->us has sold the<-
->5 horse to a certain man<-
->and it is with the cav[alrymen]...<-
->*several lines missing*<-
->...making every effort<-
->so that the horse may be delivered<-
->to the one bringing<-
->10 you this letter.<-
->Farewell. Year 32, Artemisios 17.<-

*Line 6.* "cavalrymen": This is a likely reconstruction of the broken word.
*Lines 6-7.* Before line 7, the text likely said "You would therefore do well to make..."

### 59214. Fragment of letter. — 0.125 m. x 0.11 m. — Date: 14th August, 254 B.C.

The handwriting is like that of document no. 59244, and the letter may have been written by Hermon.

->...bringing...<-
->for he did not pay attention, but neither<-
->did he wish to come<-
->to you nor to work.<-
->5 But Metrodoros has also arrived<-
->to us and has written you a<-
->letter at the second hour <note>Roughly 7:00 or 8:00 AM, based on the ancient system of dividing daylight into twelve hours starting at dawn.</note>, which<-
->we have sent to you by<-
->giving it to a guard <term>phylakites</term> <note>A local police official or guard.</note>,<-
->10 asking you to<-
->wait for him. Farewell. Year 32, Pauni 23.<-

*Lines 7-8.* The scribe likely made a spelling error in the word for "letter." Other possible readings for the location are either too short or too long for the gap in the papyrus; furthermore, the grammar requires a word like "letter" to follow.

### 59215. Letter from Dioskourides to Zenon. — 0.275 m. x 0.11 m. — Date of reception: 14th September, 254 B.C.

Dioskourides writes that he has received twelve yoke of cattle, as well as the pack donkeys, but that four of the twelve yoke are useless. He then enumerates the eight defective animals and complains that he will be obliged to feed them without being able to use them for the sowing. It is probable that the cattle were supplied by Zenon and that Dioskourides was a farmer who was either cultivating some land for him as an employee or had leased some land from him.
Though the lines are short and the papyrus folded vertically, the writing is across the fibers <note>Scribes usually wrote along the horizontal fibers; writing across them often suggests the reuse of a scrap of papyrus.</note>. But probably it is a re-used piece.

<meta>Museum Catalogue, no. 59140.</meta>

### **59217. Letter from Platon to Zenon.** — 0.095 m. x 0.12 m. and 0.10 m. x 0.145 m. — Date of reception: 2nd October, 254 B.C.

Platon, the author of document no. 59192, was a friend of Zenon with some property in the Fayoum, perhaps a <term>kleros</term> <note>A "kleros" was a plot of land allotted to military settlers in Hellenistic Egypt.</note>. He writes here that as he has been obliged to stay some time longer in Alexandria, he would like to sell his corn in Memphis and he asks Zenon to lend him a boat to carry it down and also to see that his affairs are conducted profitably. He asks him also to order one of his people to procure a pig for the festival, no doubt the <term>Arsinoeia</term> <note>A festival dedicated to the deified Queen Arsinoe II Philadelphus.</note> (see no. 59096). The letter is not in the same hand as no. 59192, but I have little doubt that they are both by the same author.

->Platon to Zenon greetings. If you yourself are well and other things regarding you are to your<-
->liking,<-
->it would be well; and I myself have been healthy. Since it happened that we have<-
->stayed on in Alexandria<-
->some extra time, we wish to bring our small stock of grain down to Memphis to<-
->dispose of it. You would do us a favor, then,<-
->by lending us the small boat... and by<-
->taking care concerning other matters so that they<-
5 ->be managed as profitably as possible. Please also order one of your people to<-
->jointly take care that<-
->a sacrificial animal <note>original: "hierion"; often referring to a pig for sacrifice.</note> is obtained for us for the festival.<-
->Farewell.<-

VERSO:

->Year 32, Mesore 10. &emsp;&emsp;&emsp; To Zenon.<-
->Platon.<-

Line 2. There seems to be room for three more letters in the gap (e.g., "now"); but probably there was an empty space after "healthy."
Line 3. The papyrus has "you would do a favor" with a slight spelling error <note>original: "charizoia" instead of "charizoio"</note>.
Line 6. Read "sacrificial animal" <note>original: "hiereion"</note>.

<meta>BIBLIOGRAPHY: Museum Entry Journal, no. 48662.</meta>

# **59218. Memoranda.** — 0.145 m x 0.165 m — Date: September-October, 254 B.C.

Though I publish it here, I do not believe that this papyrus really belongs to the archives of Zenon. It consists of a series of imperfect memoranda. As the text is written across the fibers on both sides in a good, clear hand, it is difficult to say which is the front <note>recto</note> and which is the back <note>verso</note>. In the lower part of column I, possibly continued in column II, we have the list of the members of a certain Egyptian family. Columns II and III of the back (as printed here) contain a list of the priests who have paid their portions <note>original: "tōn thentōn ta merē"</note>. If the memoranda are connected with each other, I can only suggest that they refer to the collection of a tax such as the salt tax <term>halikē: a common poll tax in Ptolemaic Egypt paid by almost all inhabitants</term> (see *P. Frankfurt*, 5).

### Column I.

Year 32, Mesore <note>Mesore was the last month of the Egyptian calendar, roughly August/September</note> [day]
we submitted <note>original: "ennebalomen"</note> the
petition <note>original: "enteuxin"</note> to Hipponikos
concerning Horos son of Pim[...]s.

5 A list <note>original: "hypomnema"</note> of the household
names <note>original: "oikeiōn onomatōn"</note>: to his
mother [...]is daughter of Sok[...]nos,
... Sokeus, brother,
another Sokkeus, brother,
10 [...]ris, sister,
[...]rtoi...os, sister,
[...]...r [...] son of Keutos

### Column II.

[...]
Paches daughter of the
15 same [...]

**Line 2.** *we submitted*: compare with *P. Petr.*, III, 20, A 2, 4, "the petition which they submitted."
**Line 4.** Perhaps *Piminies*.
**Line 5.** *List* <note>original: "hypomnema"</note> here and in line 32 means, as is sometimes the case, simply a list. The word at the end of the line may be a proper name. *Etpheou* seems scarcely possible.
**Line 6.** Read "household" <note>original: "oikeiōn"</note>.
**Line 9.** Read "brother" <note>original: "adelphos"</note>.
**Lines 13-23.** I do not know whether this is a continuation of column I or a note on a different subject.

**Line 19.** *Remmitieion*: probably the name of a temple or a quarter in Krokodilopolis. See *P. Amherst*, II, 35, 21, "at the Premit," which is apparently the name of the place where the governor <term>strategus: a high-ranking military and civil official</term> held his court.

### 59223. Letter from Thrasymedes to Zenon. — 0.165 m x 0.31 m — Date: 20th December, 254 B.C. (pl. XV).

Thrasymedes reports that Etearchos having arrived on the 7th of Phaophi, a receipt had been written to Python for the 100 artabs of sesame which Zenon had measured out in the preceding year, as well as for the 105 artabs of <term>knekos</term> <note>original: "knēkos"; an oil-producing plant, likely safflower</note> measured out on the 10th of last Epeiph. As for the poppy seed, if a note of the amount be given him, he will write a receipt to Philiskos, together with one for the 300 artabs of sesame, if Zenon will instruct him, in order that the sesame may be sent to the factory to be utilized. 
Thrasymedes is probably the official who appears in *P. Lille*, I, 39-51 as a subordinate of the nomarch <note>A "nomarch" was the administrator of a "nome" or district.</note> Diogenes, from whom he receives orders to advance loans of grain to certain <term>cleruchs</term> <note>Greek soldiers granted land by the state in exchange for military service</note> and farmers. Here he appears in conjunction with the nomarch Etearchos, writing receipts to Python the banker and Philiskos the <term>oeconome</term> <note>an "oeconome" was a financial administrator</note> for sesame and other crops which Zenon was handing over to the Government. The price of these would presumably be put to his credit in the royal bank at Krokodilopolis. Sesame and <term>knekos</term> had of course to be surrendered to the oil monopoly at fixed prices and their cultivation was strictly regulated. I formerly suggested that Thrasymedes was an agent of the monopoly and that the poppy <note>original: "mēkōn"</note> formed part of the oil crops, but the comparison with *P. Lille*, 39-51 renders this idea unlikely.

Thrasymedes to Zenon, greetings. In accordance [with what you wrote to us regarding] the 100 artabs of sesame
which you measured out in the 31st year of the [month...,] Etearchos having arrived
on the 7th of Phaophi, we have written the receipt to Python, and likewise

*Line 1.* The restoration is conjectural, but coincides with the length of the lacuna.
*Line 2.* Probably Me[cheir], the year being the regnal one.

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

also for the 105 artabs of <term>knekos</term> which you measured out in the 32nd year on the 10th of Epeiph.
<margin>5</margin> Concerning the poppy seed, if the measurement is reported to us, we will write 
[the receipt] to Philiskos, and for the 300 artabs of sesame, if you write or 
give instructions <gloss>at the same time</gloss> 
to us, so that the sesame may also be used up at the oil factory.

### 59225. Letter from Artemidoros to Zenon. — 0.17 m x 0.335 m — Date: about 25th January, 253 B.C.

The writer is Artemidoros the physician (see no. 59044), who was attached to the household of Apollonios and attended his master on most of his tours. In the present...

In this case he probably writes from Alexandria. He had heard that the sons of Leptines in Pharbaitos, Nikandros and Myrikon, had a black horse with large swellings on the legs which rendered him useless except to breed from, and he asks Zenon to use his influence with the young men in order to procure him the horse, either buying him outright for a small sum or borrowing him for the breeding season, as his own stallion is rather old and wanting in vigour. He also reminds Zenon about the sesame on the <term>allotment</term> <note>original: "klēros"; a piece of land granted to a settler</note>, asking him to see it harvested and inform him of the amount of the crop. From the present letter and from no. 59251 we may infer that Artemidoros had obtained an allotment at Philadelphia through the favour of Apollonios. He had also a house and a considerable quantity of live stock. But as he spent most of his time in Alexandria, he had to leave the management of his property, to a large extent, in the hands of his friend Zenon. Artemidoros writes in lively, idiomatic Greek, which it is a pleasure to read.

### 59226. Letter from Diotimos to Zenon. — 0.155 m x 0.33 m. — Date: 31st January, 253 B. C. (plate XVI).

Diotimos is addressed in another document (no. 59236) as <term>dioiketes</term> <note>a high-ranking finance minister</note>, while in another of our texts he is called, more precisely, the <term>hypodioiketes</term> <note>a deputy finance minister</note>. It is open to question which of the two titles was the official one at this period, but apparently both were in common use. The deputy minister, as it is more convenient to call him, was a high official coming between the chief minister and the <term>oeconomes</term> <note>local financial managers</note> in his district. Diotimos, who held this post in the Fayoum and the neighbouring <term>nomes</term> <note>administrative districts</note>, is often mentioned in the Petrie and the Zenon papyri. With Zenon, the private representative of the chief minister Apollonios, he was naturally on intimate terms. In the present letter he announces that he has sent back Eutychides, who had apparently been summoned to explain certain accounts, and that he is going to Ptolemais on the 10th to transact business there. 
The letter is written in a large official hand like those of Apollonios’ scribes, but it is noticeable that Diotimos, like the local managers, dates by the Egyptian month alone.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Diotimos to Zenon, greetings. We have sent back to you
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Eutychides; for he has rendered an account of the matters 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;for which he was summoned. Know that we ourselves will be 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;present
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;at Ptolemais on the tenth and will transact business there.
<margin>5</margin>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Farewell. Year 32, Choiak 8. <note>Choiak was an Egyptian month; the date corresponds to January 31, 253 B.C.</note>

*Line 2. Eutychides: an employee of Zenon (see no. 59129 and P.S.I., 522), described as a "boy" or "servant" in another papyrus.*
*Line 4. Ptolemais: no doubt the town on the Bahr Youssef at the entrance to the Fayoum.*

11.

<summary>This page contains a letter from Artemidoros the doctor requesting Zenon's help in acquiring a horse for breeding and asking for updates on his sesame harvest, followed by a letter from the official Diotimos regarding the return of an employee and upcoming business in Ptolemais.</summary>

### 59229. Letter from Timokles to Sostratos. — 0.15 m. x 0.10 m. — Date of reception: 3rd(?) March, 253 B.C. (plate XIV).

The writer says that he has at present sent two workmen to stake the vines and that two others will join them in three days' time. He asks Sostratos to supply them with bast <note>plant fibers used for tying</note> and stakes.
Sostratos was an associate, perhaps a relative, of Zenon (see *P. Edg.*, 63, introduction). They were joint owners of a large vineyard at Philadelphia (*P.S.I.*, 393), which was presumably the scene of these operations.

&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;Timokles to Sostratos
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;greetings. I have sent
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;to you the two who will
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;stake [the vines]
<margin>5</margin>&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;first, just as
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;I agreed with you,
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;and in three days
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;two
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;others [[three]] will come to you.
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;Give them then both
<margin>10</margin>&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;bast and stakes.
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;Fa[rewell.

## 59233. Letter from Artemidoros to Zenon. — 0.09 m. x 0.30 m. — Date of reception: 31st March, 253 B. C.

The hand of this badly preserved letter seems to be the same as that of no. 59136, and the author is probably the Artemidoros who lived in Memphis. He speaks here of laying the foundation of some building and begs Zenon to send him the architect. He also asks him to send the fir trees to plant in the garden.

->Artemidoros to Zenon [greetings. ... u]s to lay the foundat[ion] of the ...[...].<-
->You will do well [to send] the archit[ect] to us<-
->so that [we] are not delayed [... and] se[nd...]<-

*Line 1.* <term>know that we</term> <note>original: "ἴσθι ἡμᾶς"</note> seems the most probable restoration, in spite of the following infinitive. — <term>of the house</term> <note>original: "τῆς οἰκίας"</note> is the obvious restoration, especially as one of our fragments bears the docket: <term>Artemidoros concerning the dwelling</term> <note>original: "Ἀρτεμίδωρος περὶ τῆς οἰκήσεως"</note>. But as the second letter of the missing word might be read as "t", <term>of the portico</term> <note>original: "στοᾶς"</note> is also a possibility.

### 59236. Petition to Diotimos from Neoptolemos. — 0.10 m. x 0.39 m. — Date: 254 or 253 B.C. (plate XVII).

<term>Diotimos</term> was the local <term>dioiketes</term> <note>regional finance minister</note> or <term>hypodioiketes</term> <note>deputy finance minister</note> (see no. 59226). The petition was presented by <term>Neoptolemos</term> on behalf of his father <term>Stratippos</term>, who was a vine-grower. He complains that Stratippos had been unfairly treated by <term>Theokles</term>, the late <term>oeconome</term> <note>a local financial manager</note> and <term>Petosiris</term>, the royal scribe of the Aphroditopolite nome. For in assessing the taxes to be paid on vineyards, they had been taking as a basis the average yield of the last three years, but in his father's case, they had taken the last two years only, on the ground that his vineyard was lately planted. He therefore asks Diotimos to give him an order addressed to <term>Hermolaos</term> (the present <term>oeconome</term>) and Petosiris, bidding them assess his father's vineyard like the others on the average yield of three years, beginning either from year 29 or from year 30, as it had now been producing wine for four years. He asks him also to accept formally as payment of the tax the money, or part of the money, paid into the bank by the wine-sellers for the wine which they bought from the vineyard.
The scholar <term>Rostovtzeff</term> has commented on this text, and his view that the tax in question was the <term>one-third tax on vineyards</term> <note>original: "τρίτη ἀμπελώνων"</note> seems to me to be right. But when he says that Stratippos' grievance was that he had had to pay one half instead of one third, I cannot follow

<summary>This page documents the logistics of Apollonios' estate, including the planting of pine trees and the management of draft animal teams, as well as a formal petition regarding a tax dispute over a vineyard's yield assessments.</summary>
<keywords>Artemidoros, Zenon, Phileas, Diotimos, Neoptolemos, Stratippos, pine trees, agriculture, draft animals, taxation, vineyard, Ptolemaic Egypt, Memphis, Aphroditopolite nome</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the documentation of the Zenon Papyri, specifically focusing on tax grievances and personal requests handled by the administration of the finance minister Apollonios and his successor or deputy, Diotimos. The previous entries dealt with agricultural logistics; here we see the legal and fiscal complexities of vineyard management in the 3rd century B.C.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM.</header>

him; nothing to this effect is either said or implied in the text. The grievance of Stratippos was that the officials assessed his vineyard on the average yield of two good years instead on the average of three years, one of which was less good than the other two. We learn from other sources, especially *P. S. I.*, 632, that the assessment <note>original: "ἐπιγραφή" — a term for a tax assessment or registration</note> was paid in money at a varying rate per measure <note>original: "metretes" — a liquid measure approximately equal to 9 gallons</note>, apparently on the actual yield of each year; but how this is to be reconciled with the account of the assessment given in the present text I do not yet see.

Wilcken, resolving the abbreviation *upe* on the back <note>verso</note> into "under-written" <note>original: "ὑπεγραφή"</note>, proposes the explanation that our text, which has no address or note of reception, is a copy and that the original "was processed by subscription and passed on." But if a petition was to be presented by hand, it did not necessarily bear an address, and the present text seems to me to be the original. Noting that it was written by Neoptolemos, who lived in Philadelphia, and not by Stratippos himself, I suspect that it was presented to Diotimos through Zenon or in the presence of Zenon (*cf. P. S. I.*, 587, 591). If *upe* stands for "under-written," it probably means 'a copy was sent under a covering letter'. *Cf. P. S. I.*, 372, 24, note.

To Diotimos the finance minister <note>original: "διοικητῆι"</note>, greetings from Neoptolemos, a Macedonian, one of the military settlers <note>original: "κληρούχων" — soldiers granted land by the king</note> in Philadelphia. My father Stratippos is being wronged by Theokles the district manager <note>original: "οἰκονόμου" — a financial official</note> <gloss>throughout</gloss> the Aphroditopolite district and Petosiris the royal secretary. For when they were making the tax assessment for the vineyards, they took the produce of three years and assessed the third part <note>presumably the average</note>, but for my father they have made the assessment from only two years, claiming the vineyard is a new planting. I beg you therefore, if it seems right to you, to look into these matters, and if these things are true,
<margin>5</margin> since they have made the assessment from three years for the others as well, to give me <gloss>Hermolaos and Petosiris</gloss> <note>These names appear to be a later interlinear clarification or correction</note> an order addressed to them so that they might make the assessment for my father based on three years as well,

*Line 1*. To Diotimos : in larger letters than the rest of the text and probably rewritten. It is possible indeed that the petition was originally addressed to Apollonios.
*Lines 1-2*. Stratippos : *P. S. I.*, 632 is a complaint from the same person addressed to Apollonios in year 37 (as appears from a lately found fragment of the text), about the taxation of his vineyards in the Herakleopolite district.
*Line 2*. Theokles : as Theokles was still the district manager in the month of Phaophi of year 32 (*P. S. I.*, 566), the petition must have been written between the end of 254 B.C. and the vintage of 253 B.C.
*Line 3*. they assessed the third part : this might mean 1) 'they imposed a tax consisting of the third part of the average yearly yield' or 2) 'they took the average yield of the last three years as an assessment for future taxation'. Rostovtzeff, if I understand him right, takes the former view, but the latter seems to me to accord better with the context.

<column-break/>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

whether they wish to start the calculation from the twenty-ninth year or from the thirtieth year—for we have already been making wine from it for four years—and to accept for him the silver that has been paid into the bank by the wine-retailers for the wine which they received from the vineyard, so that through you he may obtain justice.
->May you prosper.<-

**Back of the sheet (Verso):**
<margin>10</margin> Neoptolemos to Diotimos, a petition concerning a vineyard.
Below, at right angles to the filing note: *upe* <note>perhaps "submitted" or "under-written"</note>. To the right a large drachma sign or the letter T, blotted.

*Lines 7-8*. This seems to imply that the price of the wine paid by the retailers was deposited in the bank until Government claims had been satisfied.

### 59237. Letter from Dionysodoros to Zenon. — 0.30 m x 0.105 m. — Date: 29th May, 254 B.C. or 28th May, 253 B.C. (plate XVIII).

Knowing that Zenon was buying honey for Nikon (see no. 59160), Dionysodoros asks him to buy a measure <note>original: "metretes"</note> for himself and send it along with Nikon's, informing him at the same time to whom he should pay the price.
The letter is dated year 32 and the filing note <note>docket</note> says year 33. There is no question in this case of any confusion between the regnal and financial years (as for the Egyptian year, the Greeks had not yet begun to recognize it), and the probability is that Dionysodoros has simply made a slip.

->Dionysodoros to Zenon<-
->greetings. You would do<-
->well to purchase for me<-
->1 measure of honey,<-
<margin>5</margin> ->just as you are<-
->buying for Nikon, and when<-
->you send his,<-
->do the same for me.<-
->Also [write] the price and to whom I must<-

*Line 7*. Read "send" <note>original: "ἀποστείληις"</note> rather than "be sending."

## 59240. Letter from Kleanax to Zenon. — (a) 0 m. 20 $\times$ 0 m. 085; (b) 0 m. 195 $\times$ 0 m. 215. — Date: about 23rd July, 253 B.C.

Only the fragment (a), comprising the beginnings of the lines, is in our collection; the rest of the text is in Florence and has been published in *P. S. I.*, 511. I print the letter now as a whole and I have to thank Miss M. Norsa for a revision of the Florence portion.

Kleanax writes to Zenon about certain mules which he had sent his slaves to buy and hand over to Zenon in order that they might be sent down along with those of Apollonios to the <term>gift-estate<note>original: "δωρεά" (dorea) — land granted by the King to high officials, often exempt from certain taxes.</note></term> of the latter in Memphis. The object of this maneuver, as he frankly explains, was that they might pass as Apollonios’ property and escape being taxed for export and transit. He wishes to know if they have come to hand and been despatched and how Zenon has settled the matter; and if they have not yet come to hand, he begs him to give orders to his people to do as he, Kleanax, has requested.

From the gist of the letter and from the Macedonian date we may infer that Kleanax lived in Alexandria. Though a well-to-do man, in touch with Apollonios and Zenon, he had no scruple about cheating the toll-collectors. In no. 59012 we find Apollonios paying Customs duty and <term>gate-toll<note>original: "διαπύλιον" — a tax paid when transporting goods through city gates.</note></term> like any ordinary merchant, but in the present case his mules pass free or at least without trouble. Probably his goods were not exempt from such taxation, but either the tax-collectors were afraid of giving offence or else the mules were not required to pay toll because they were merely being transferred from one of his estates to another, whereas those of Kleanax had been newly bought. This is the only document in which the property of Apollonios in Memphis is definitely called a gift-estate.

&emsp;&emsp;Kleanax to Zenon, greetings. Concerning the mules
&emsp;&emsp;which I sent the servants to you so that they might be bought for us
&emsp;&emsp;and brought to you, and concerning those from Apollonios
&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;<gloss>in Memphis</gloss>
&emsp;&emsp;that they might be sent to the gift-estate of Apollonios, you would
&emsp;5 do well if you would personally attend to it, or if not, see to it that
&emsp;&emsp;they are safely sent along with yours as if they belong
&emsp;&emsp;to Apollonios, so that they might not be troubled by the taxes <note>original: "τέλη"</note>,
&emsp;&emsp;and if you yourself are present, well, but if not, write to us about
&emsp;&emsp;them if they have arrived and have been sent, and what
&emsp;10 arrangement you have made, so that we may know they are ours.

*Lines 3-4.* Either "concerning" <note>original: "περὶ"</note> is a slip for "with" <note>original: "μετά"</note> or some words have dropped out between "Apollonios" and "sent."
*Line 8.* "If you yourself are present" <note>original: "ἐὰν μὲν αὐτὸς παραγίνῃ"</note>: some such phrase as "mentioning us" is understood, depending on "you would do well."

<summary>This page records letters to Zenon involving construction projects managed by Phileas and a clever tax-avoidance scheme by Kleanax, who attempts to pass off his private mules as the property of the finance minister Apollonios to avoid customs duties.</summary>
<keywords>Zenon Papyri, Phileas, Kleanax, tax evasion, mules, Memphis, dorea, gift-estate, Maimachos, Apollonios, tolls</keywords>

<page-num>94</page-num>
<header>CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM.</header>

...and they have arrived safely; but if they have not yet appeared, give orders to your people as we requested you to manage.
->Farewell. Year 33, Daisios 13.<- <note>June 11, 253 B.C.</note>

->VERSO:<-

Year 33, Pauni 19. <note>August 7, 253 B.C.</note> &emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; To Zenon.
15 [Kl]eanax.

*Line 12*. For "that you may give orders" <note>original: "ἵνα συντάξης"</note> used as an imperative, without a preceding "take care," compare MAYSER, *Grammar*, II, pp. 229-232.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48688; *P. S. I.*, 511.

### 59241. Letter from Zenon to Krotos. — 0 m. 125 x 0 m. 33. — Date: 17th September, 253 B.C. (pl. XVII).

Zenon orders Krotos to obtain twenty-five <term>minae<note>A unit of weight; 25 minae would be roughly 12.5 kilograms or 27 pounds.</note></term> of wool from Pasis the Jew and to contract with Artemidoros for the making of a mattress, which was to be at least long enough for a seat for two and to have a double front. The mattress was required for Peisikles, and Krotos was to try to have it finished in fifteen days.
Krotos (see no. 59077) was a business agent, perhaps of servile origin, while Peisikles often appears as a treasurer or paymaster in the household of Apollonios.
The Artemidoros mentioned here is probably Apollonios' Memphite overseer (see no. 59149), who would be able to have the mattress made in the wool factory. Pasis the Jew with the Egyptian name appears again in no. 59292, 611; whether he is to be identified, as I formerly suggested, with the shepherd Pasis son of Paous of *P. S. I.*, 626 is very doubtful.

&emsp;&emsp;Zenon to Krotos, greetings. As soon as you receive this letter,
&emsp;&emsp;take from Pasis the Jew 25 minae of wool and give it out to Artemi-
&emsp;&emsp;doros so that he may manufacture a mattress, so that in length it is for a two-seater
&emsp;&emsp;or slightly larger, double-faced; for there is a need for it for Peisikles;
&emsp;5 and as soon as it is finished, send it to Memphis to Artemi-
&emsp;&emsp;doros, and try to ensure it is finished in 15 days. We have written
&emsp;&emsp;also to Pasis to give you the wool.
->Farewell. Year 33, Epeiph 28.<- <note>September 17, 253 B.C.</note>

->VERSO:<-

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; To Krotos.

*Line 3*. Two-seater <note>original: "δίεδρον"</note>: cf. no. 74, 35; there also it probably means a seat rather than a stand.
*Line 4*. Double-faced <note>original: "διπρόσωπον"</note>: with a presentable cover on both sides.
*Line 5*. "is finished" <note>original: "γένηται"</note>: 'as soon as the wool is got', while "is finished" <note>original: "γένηται"</note> in line 6 means 'that the mattress be made'.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48689; *P. Edg.*, 108; *Sammelbuch*, 6991.

### 59242. Letter from Apollonios to Zenon. — 0 m. 195 x 0 m. 23. — Date: November-December, 253 B.C. (pl. XIX).

Zenon is ordered to prepare the fittings for the galleys called <term>taurokerkouroi<note>Literally "bull-tailed" or "bull-sterned" boats; a type of river galley.</note></term>, while Theon will put them on board a boat and bring them down the river. He must expedite the work, for an urgent message has come to Apollonios to set about the dispatch of the galleys to Alexandria in preparation for the up-voyage of the king's daughter.
If my restoration of lines 6, 7 is right (and Wilcken has endorsed it without question) the occasion for which the galleys were being fitted up was the wedding journey of the princess Berenike from Alexandria to Pelusium and thence to Syria. See no. 59251. The king escorted her as far as Pelusium: <note>original Latin: "deduxitque usque Pelusium et infinita auri et argenti milia dotis nomine dedit"</note> "and he escorted her as far as Pelusium and gave infinite thousands of gold and silver in the name of a dowry" (Hieronymus). It was a ceremonial voyage for which months of preparation were no doubt required.

&emsp;&emsp;[Apoll]onios to Zenon, greetings. For the ...
&emsp;&emsp;[the fit]tings of the bull-sterned galleys, as many as [they may need],
&emsp;&emsp;[Theon will bring], having loaded them into a boat. [Therefore, introduce]
&emsp;&emsp;him and do not hinder him. [For it has been] written [to us]
&emsp;5 [to] send the bull-sterned [galleys] with [ha]ste
&emsp;&emsp;[to Alexan]dria for the voyage up-river of [the King's]
&emsp;&emsp;[daugh]ter.
->Farewell. Year 33, [Hype]rber[etaios].<- <note>Nov-Dec 253 B.C.</note>

->VERSO:<-

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Apollonios &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; To Zenon.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; ].os

*Line 1*. Possibly "bring down to Tak[yris]" <note>original: "εἰς Τακ[ῦριν κατάγαγε]"</note>, suggested by *P. S. I.*, 544, 6; but the restoration is very doubtful.
*Line 2*. Bull-sterned galleys <note>original: "ταυροκερκούρων"</note>: defined by Suidas as "river boats."
*Line 4*. It has been written <note>original: "γέγραπται"</note>: no doubt from the Court, for from what other quarter could Apollonios receive an order?
*Line 10*. Bilabel suggests "concerning Theon," but the line was probably longer, "Apollonios" in line 9 being preceded by the date.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48690; *P. Edg.*, 39; WILCKEN, *Archiv*, VI, p. 453; ROSTOVTZEFF, *Large Estate*, p. 123; *Sammelbuch*, 6745.

Horos, like Onnophris (no. 59207), who may possibly have been his father (*P. S. I.*, 522, 4), was one of the farmers whom Zenon employed to look after the land...

### 59244. Letter from Hermon to Zenon. — 0 m. 125 x 0 m. 145 and 0 m. 125 x 0 m. 14. — Date of reception : 11^th^ February, 252 B. C.

Hermon had written that he was sending Zenon a certain number of logs and olive

<column-break/>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

shoots and he now adds that, after the first letter was written, both consignments had been increased. Among the logs are seven shoots which it is doubtful if Zenon will accept. If he likes them, he is to write and Hermon will send another consignment of them. The letter ends with a request for more money.
We do not know from where Hermon wrote. *Was additionally loaded* <note>original: "προσενεβλήθη"</note> no doubt suggests that the consignments came by boat; but I think the word might also be used of adding to a waggon load or a donkey load, and *porterage fees* <note>original: "φόρετρα"</note> certainly refers to porterage by land.

*Line 1.* *Greetings* <note>original: "χαίρειν"</note> is omitted, probably by accident. Patrokles is not certain.
*Line 2.* Panakestor, who came from Kalynda in Caria, had apparently called one of his sons after his friend Zenon. Or the families may have been related.
*Line 5.* *shoots* <note>original: "φυτά"</note>: corrected. Perhaps *which [it is necessary to return]* <note>original: "ἃ [δεῖ σε ἀποδοῦν]αι"</note>.

### 59245. Letter from Kollouthes to Zenon. — 0 m. 09 x 0 m. 305. — Date of reception : 23^rd^ February, 252 B. C. (pl. XX).

Kollouthes writes that after leaving Zenon he found that the peasants belonging to the land which had been portioned out among the soldiers had fled to the temple of Isis in the Memphite nome. So at the time when he received Zenon's letter he

<summary>This page contains correspondence to Zenon regarding estate management, specifically reporting on the progress of poppy and castor-oil crops and the transport of timber and olive shoots. The letters highlight the linguistic diversity of the estate's staff and the logistical challenges of transporting goods and managing labor.</summary>
<keywords>Horos, Hermon, poppy cultivation, kiki, castor oil, olive shoots, transport costs, cleruchs, Panakestor, agricultural labor</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the entries of the Zenon Papyri catalog from the 33rd year of the reign of Ptolemy II Philadelphus. While the previous page discussed agricultural yields of poppy and castor oil, these entries focus on labor disputes—specifically farmers seeking asylum in a temple—and the administrative coordination of public works between Zenon and the provincial financial officer, Philiskos.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM.</header>

was setting out for Krokodilopolis <note>The capital of the Arsinoite district, modern Faiyum.</note> to ask Maimachos the <term>nomarch<note>A high-ranking official responsible for a district or 'nome'.</note></term> to rout them out; and as soon as this is done he will come to Zenon.
The story is interesting as an early instance of peasants going on strike and taking refuge in a temple (cf. *P. S. I.*, 502). But whether this <term>Isieion<note>A temple dedicated to the goddess Isis.</note></term> had a right of asylum or was simply an ordinary temple at a convenient distance from the authorities concerned is quite uncertain. Nor again do we know whether the men were bound by oath not to take refuge in a temple nor whether the nomarch was empowered to eject them by force. In the parallel case cited above the authorities used persuasion only, and it proved quite ineffective.
Kollouthes is in all probability the person who is described in a fragment in the British Museum (Invent. no. 2363) as <term>komogrammateus<note>A village scribe or record-keeper.</note></term> of the village of Ammonias.

> Kollouthes to Zenon, greetings. When I departed from you, I found that the farmers
> from the land measured out to the soldiers had retreated to the Isieion <note>The temple of Isis</note> 
> in the Memphite district.
> When I received the letter from you, I set out for Krokodilopolis to see Maimachos,
> so that he might rouse them; and as soon as this happens, we will come to you; for Psenomous 
> 5 was not present in the village. I have written to you, therefore, so that you may know.
->Farewell. Year 33, Choiak [day unknown].<-

VERSO:
[Year 33], Tybi 2. Kollouthes. To Zenon.
In another place: Mys.

*Line 1. the farmers* <note>original: "τοὺς γεωργούς"</note>: probably peasants who cultivated the <term>cleruchic<note>Land granted to soldier-settlers.</note></term> land on terms arranged between them and the holders. As Zenon seems to have taken over part of such land from the cleruchs, he may have been a party in the dispute.
*Line 2. the Isieion* <note>original: "τὸ Ἰσιεῖον"</note>: I suggested formerly that this might mean either a temple or a village round a temple, for in fact Isieion is a common place-name and there was an important village of this name in the Nile valley opposite the Fayoum (*Itin. Ant.* 156). (The further suggestion that the right of asylum belonged to the whole village is attributed to me by Woess, but is really due to his own imagination). But the probability is that *the Isieion* here means simply a temple.
*Line 4. might rouse* <note>original: "ἂν ἐγείρηι"</note>: or perhaps *might awaken*. Read *we will be present* <note>original: "παρεσόμεθα"</note>.
*Line 5. Psenomous* <note>original: "Ψενομοῦς"</note>: apparently the name of a man (so too Bilabel), perhaps a <term>comarch<note>A village headman.</note></term> who might have acted instead of the nomarch.

BIBL.: *Museum Accession Register*, no. 48694; *P. Edg.*, 40; Rostovtzeff, *Large Estate*, pp. 75, 80; Woess, *Asylum*, p. 17 and following; *Sammelbuch*, 6746.

### 59247. Letter from Philiskos to Zenon. — 0.17 m x 0.34 m. — Date: 1st March, 252 B.C. (plate XXI).

Philiskos, there can be little doubt, was the current <term>oeconome<note>A chief financial officer or manager of a province.</note></term> of the Arsinoite province. He writes that he had been about to set out for Philadelphia when a letter arrived ordering him to go to Ptolemais to meet Ariston, a member of the Court, who had sailed up to see the <term>nome<note>An administrative district.</note></term>; but he expects to be with Zenon on the 17th. He asks him therefore to send Hedylos to Krokodilopolis in order that they may draw up a form of contract and then put the work up to tender in Philadelphia. He also tells Zenon to inform him whether he has received the mattocks from the Herakleopolite nome.
Ariston may very well be the traveller who, according to Diodorus Siculus (Book III, 42), was sent by the king to explore Arabia: <note>original: "Ἀρίστωνος τοῦ πεμφθέντος ὑπὸ Πτολεμαίου πρὸς κατασκοπὴν τῆς ἕως ὠκεανοῦ παρηκούσης Ἀραβίας" — "Of Ariston, who was sent by Ptolemy to explore the coast of Arabia as far as the ocean."</note>.
Hedylos we know from an unpublished text to have been an <term>architect<note>original: "ἀρχιτέκτων" — In this context, a public works engineer.</note></term>, and it is therefore probable that the business which was bringing Philiskos to Philadelphia was the giving out of some public work to contractors. Compare the contracts made by the oeconome and the architect in *P. Petr.*, III, 43, 2. The mattocks mentioned in line 7 were probably Government property, lent out to one contractor after another (*location cited*, column I, 12).

> Philiskos to Zenon, greetings. As I was about to come to you, a letter came
> to meet <gloss>the</gloss> [[Ariston]] Ariston in Ptolemais, who has sailed up
> from the King to inspect the nome; I will be <gloss>however</gloss> [[therefore]]
> with you, as I suppose, on the 17th. You will do well, then, to ask
> 5 Hedylos to come to Krokodilopolis [[so that the contract]] so that, having made the
> contract, we may put it up for tender in Philadelphia. Write to me
> also about the mattocks from the Herakleopolite district, if you have received them.
->Farewell. Year 33, Tybi 8.<-

## 59250. Letter from Promethion to Zenon. — 0.135 m x 0.105 m and 0.14 m x 0.115 m — Date: 11th(?) April, 252 B.C.

Promethion is known from various sources, some unpublished, to have been a banker at Mendes in the Nile Delta. He was a friend of Zenon and acted as a commercial agent for him.

Promethion [to Zenon, greetings. If you are in good health] and other things are
to your liking [I would be grateful to the gods]; I myself am also in good health
and am attending to the matters [you commissioned]. . . . of wax and we have
given an earnest-money payment [for it. Write] to me what the price is
<margin>5</margin> so that [I may provide it] to you... you should write. And if you
have need of anything [do not hesitate] to write, [for we will]
gladly do it.
->Farewell. Year 33, Mecheir 19.<-

BACK OF THE SHEET: To Zenon.

*Line 2.* For example, "[It would be as I pray to the gods]."
*Line 3.* Perhaps "the matters you commissioned."
*Line 4.* "Write" or "Therefore, write."

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48697.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Zenon Papyri from Year 33 and 34 of Ptolemy II Philadelphus (253–252 B.C.). <header>CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM.</header>

# 59251. Letter from Artemidoros to Zenon. — 0.13 m x 0.325 m — Date: about 13th April, 252 B.C. (Plate XX).

Artemidoros the physician (see no. 59225) writes that he and Apollonios <note>The high-ranking Finance Minister and Zenon's employer.</note> are both well and that they are now returning to Sidon <note>A major port city in Phoenicia, modern Lebanon.</note> after having escorted the queen as far as the frontier. He expects to be in Philadelphia <note>The village in the Faiyum where Zenon was based.</note> very soon and asks Zenon to do various things for him, to buy him some honey and barley, to look after his house and see that it is roofed over, to keep an eye on his cattle, pigs, geese and other farm stock and to attend to the harvesting of his crops.

The first few lines of this letter refer, without much doubt, to the wedding of the princess Berenike <note>Berenike Syra, daughter of Ptolemy II, who was sent to marry the Seleucid King Antiochus II to seal a peace treaty.</note>. She had been accompanied by her father as far as Pelusium <note>The gateway city on the eastern edge of the Nile Delta.</note> (see no. 59242). There the king took leave of her and entrusted her to the care of Apollonios, if we may assume that Apollonios was in command of the escort. At the frontier line between Syria and the Syrian province of Egypt she was met by Antiochos or his ambassadors and the Egyptian mission returned to Sidon and thence to Egypt. Apollonios, to whom the king had apparently paid the high compliment of choosing him to act as his representative on this historic occasion, took with him as usual his private physician Artemidoros. The letter, which reached Philadelphia in about fifteen days, probably came by special courier along with Apollonios' dispatches to the king.

From this text and from no. 59242 we learn that the marriage of Antiochos and Berenike was negotiated in 253 B.C. and that it took place in the spring of 252 B.C.

Artemidoros to Zenon, greetings. If you are well, it would be good; I too am well, and Apollonios is healthy and everything else is according to our wishes. At the time I was writing to you, we were arriving in Sidon, having traveled with the queen <note>original: "basilisse"</note> as far as the borders, and we expected to be with you soon. You would do me a favor by taking care of yourself so that you stay healthy, and by writing to us if you want anything done that we are able to do. You would do well to buy

<margin>
*Line 2. we were arriving* <note>original: "pareginometha"</note>, which together with *I was writing* <note>original: "egraphon"</note> is a good example of the epistolary imperfect <note>A tense used in letters where the writer describes a present action from the future perspective of the reader.</note>, might either mean 'we are on our way to Sidon' (cf. no. 59247, 1) or 'we have just arrived there'. Whether they returned to Sidon by sea or by land is not clear. The title of *queen* is given to Berenike not as future queen of Syria, but as princess of Egypt (so too <term>Wilcken</term>, *op. cit.*, p. 453).

*Line 3. the borders* <note>original: "ton horion"</note>: the phrase shows that Coele-Syria still belonged to Egypt. The idea that it had been given away as Berenike's dowry was an ingenious but erroneous inference from the fact that the king did not escort her beyond Pelusium (<term>Bouché-Leclercq</term>, *History of the Seleucids*, p. 90).
</margin>

<page-num>103</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

5 for us, so that when we arrive we have 3 <term>metretas<note>A liquid measure equal to roughly 39 liters.</note></term> of the best honey and 600 <term>artabas<note>A dry measure for grain, roughly 30-40 liters.</note></term> of barley for the cattle, paying the price for these from the proceeds of the sesame and the castor-oil seed, and also looking after the house in Philadelphia, so that when we arrive we find it roofed. And also try to look after the teams of oxen, the pigs <note>original: "hiereia"</note>, the geese, and the rest of the things there as much as possible; for in this way we will better have what is necessary. And take care that the produce is harvested in some way. And if it is 10 necessary to give anything for essential expenses, do not hesitate.

->Farewell. Year 33, Peritios intercalary, the 6th.<- <note>Peritios was a Macedonian month; "intercalary" indicates an extra month added to align the lunar and solar calendars. This date corresponds to April 252 B.C.</note>

->[To Phil]adelphia. Year 33, Phamenoth 6. To Zenon.<-
->Artemidoros.<-

<margin>
*Line 6.* Zenon acted as Artemidoros' delegate, selling his crops and handling the money.
*Line 7. Read sacrificial animals* <note>original: "hiereia"</note>, meaning 'the pigs'.
*Lines 7-10.* The end of the letter was added after the address had been written (see pl. XX).
*Line 12. [To Phil]adelphia*: part of the address.

### 59253. Letter from Poseidonios (?) to Zenon. — 0.23 m. x 0.09 m. — Date: 13th June, 252 B.C.

The writer states that Pyron has been given Zenon’s last private account and has taken note of certain details of which he had been ignorant. He has been detained several days because the writer was busy.
Pyron is no doubt the author of *P.S.I.*, 571, where he appears as a paid employee of Zenon with several clerks under him and he may possibly be the Pyron of *P. Cornell* 1, 16, who had charge of the <term>steward's books<note>original: ταμιευτικὰ βιβλία (tamieutika biblia)</note></term> during Apollonios’ tour. I suppose that the <term>private account<note>original: ἴδιος λόγος (idios logos)</note></term> means Zenon’s private account, as distinct from that of Apollonios <note>The Finance Minister under whom Zenon served.</note>. The letter is written in a large, careful hand.

Po[.....]ios to Zenon, 
greetings. Just as you
wrote to us, we have given 
to Pyron your last 
private account, 
and regarding the difference 
he was instructed in 
the things he did not know; 
but he was detained 
for several days 
because we were 
busy.
->Farewell. Year 34, Pharmouthi 22.<-

**BACK OF THE PAPYRUS:**

[Year 34, Pharm]outhi 28. To Zenon.
[               ] regarding the account.

Line 1. Poseidonios seems the most probable reading, though the ‘n’ is doubtful. Ptolemaios is not possible.
Line 6. "Regarding the difference": though "difference" <note>original: διαφόρου (diaphorou)</note> is sometimes equivalent to ‘sum’ in general, the meaning here is probably ‘with regard to the difference in reckoning’.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Accession Journal*, no. 48703.

### 59254. Letter from Phanias to Zenon. — 0.12 m. x 0.285 m. — Date of reception: 13th July, 252 B.C. (Plate XXII).

Phanias, who is often mentioned in the papyri of this period, was the <term>secretary of the cavalry<note>original: γραμματεὺς τῶν ἱππέων (grammateus ton hippeon)</note></term>; and as Zenon had much to do with the distribution and cultivation of the <term>allotments<note>land grants given to soldiers, original: κλῆροι (kleroi)</note></term>, the two men had many questions to discuss. In the present letter he informs Zenon that he has decided to review all the recruits who have received allotments in the Arsinoite <note>The region of the Faiyum.</note> district and to administer the oath to them in Philadelphia. He asks therefore that a lodging be prepared for him, as he is in poor health and would like to be with Zenon as long as possible.

Phanias to Zenon, greetings. I have decided <gloss>I have decided</gloss> <note>original: διέγνωκα (diegnoka), an interlinear correction</note> to count and administer the oath in Philadelphia to all the young recruits who have received allotments in the Arsinoite district. You will do well, therefore, to prepare a lodging for me; for I happen to be in a weak state of health, and at the same time I wish to see you for as long as possible. 
Farewell.

**BACK OF THE PAPYRUS:**

[Year 3]4, Pachons 22. Phanias to Zenon.
Regarding his presence in Philadelphia.

Line 1. "Those who have received allotments": it appears from this and from other papyri that the <term>young recruits<note>original: νεανίσκοι (neaniskoi)</note></term> as such were entitled to land grants.
Line 2. After the insertion of "I have decided" the following verbs ought to have been changed to infinitives. It is debated whether these "young recruits" were a corps of cadets in training to become officers, or if the word means simply ‘recruits’ as distinguished from the ‘elders’. For the administration of the oath, compare other contemporary records.
Line 4. "I happen to be": used here in the "epistolary imperfect" <note>A common Greek grammatical feature where the writer uses a past tense to describe a present state, looking at it from the perspective of when the recipient will read the letter.</note>.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Accession Journal*, no. 48704; *Edgar Papyri*, 109; *Sammelbuch*, 6992.

### 59256. Letter from Philiskos to Zenon. — 0.12 m. x 0.17 m. — Date: 252-251 B.C.

> From Philiskos, the <term>chief financial administrator<note>original: οἰκονόμος (oeconomus)</note></term> of the Arsinoite province <note>Modern-day Faiyum.</note>. Before Zenon's letter reached him, he had heard from the people in Tanis that there was some trouble about the water. He has therefore sent word to Maimachos the <term>nomarch<note>A local administrative official in charge of a district (nome).</note></term> to repair the mouth of the canal and he writes to Zenon to keep him informed.
> The letter is interesting, as it shows that the chief financial administrator was directly responsible for the upkeep of the irrigation works in his district. There is no mention here of the chief engineer. The administrator receives the complaint and gives the necessary orders to his subordinate the nomarch, who would probably see it carried out by a local engineer working under him (cf. no. 59109).

[Philiskos to Z]enon, greetings. Before the l[etter] from you
[arriv]ed, those in Tanis wrote to me that the water in the [ ... ]
[ ... ]. I have therefore sent to Maimachos and [ordered him]
<gloss>the mouth</gloss>
[to take care] that he should repair the <gloss>can</gloss>[al] near Pse[nyris].
5 I have therefore [writt]en to you so that you may know.
-> Farewell. Year 34[ ... <-

**BACK OF THE PAPYRUS:**
To Z]enon. -> To right, inverted, Philiskos. <-

*Line 4. "Repair": compare to a similar text describing a team repairing the "elbows" (bends) of a canal. Psenyris was a village in the district of Herakleides, but these reconstructions are somewhat uncertain.*

### 59261. Memorandum to Zenon from the fish-merchants. — 0.255 m x 0.095 m. — Date: 251 B.C. (plate XXIII).

The sellers of <term>thrissai<note>A type of shad or herring common in the Nile.</note></term> present a bill for 180 drachmae, being the price of 450 fish. From lines 7-9 we learn that, when setting out to visit Apollonios about the end of October 252 B.C., Zenon had made a purchase of 200, and we may infer that these were to be taken with him. It is not known whether Apollonios was staying in Alexandria at this time or in the <term>provinces<note>original: χώρα (chora), the Greek term for the Egyptian countryside outside of Alexandria.</note></term>. The *thrissa*, as the geographer Strabo says, was a fresh-water as well as a salt-water fish, and we see from no. 59040 that consignments were occasionally sent to Alexandria from up the river.

> Memorandum
> to Zenon from the
> fish-merchants.
> Year 34.
> 5 [[Year 30.]] You have, on Pachons 8,
> which Patis son of Hirobastis [delivered],
> 140 *thrissai*.
> On Thoth 3, as you were traveling
> to Apollonios,
> 10 280 *thrissai*.
> On Choiak 7, according to the
> letter from Philiskos,
> 50 *thrissai*.
> <margin>he said
> when we wrote
> to take [them]</margin> On the 25th, Proteas, according to the
> letter from Sosistratos.

<note>Line 7. The original Greek has a grammatical error; it should read "as you were traveling."
Line 14. "when we wrote": likely a scribal slip for "having written."</note>

### 59262. Fragment of letter. — 0.135 m x 0.175 m. — Date: 7th March, 251 B.C.

A fragment of a letter to Zenon from a friend who like himself was a dog-fancier. I suggest, perhaps rashly, that the complete text ran somewhat as follows: <note>The following is a reconstruction of what the full letter might have said based on the surviving fragments.</note> *[So-and-so] to Zenon, greetings. From the dog that we have, I wish to breed a female dog for hunting. If there is one with you that has not yet been sent to another dog, you would do well to send her to us, or, if you wish, I will send [the male] to you, and the puppies shall be reared at your place, and some you shall use, and the others you shall give to me.*
