### 59263. Letter from Philon to Zenon. — 0.11 m x 0.31 m. — Date: 1st April, 251 B.C. (plate XXIV).

Philon says that Maiandria has written to him about a cloak which Zenon has ordered her to weave. At present she is ill, but as soon as she is better Zenon shall have the garment. The writer adds, what to us is more interesting, that Apollonios has taken over the direction of public affairs and that Dionysodoros is acting as chief accountant.
The letter is dated year 34 and the docket year 30. As the former date seems more likely to be correct, I have placed the text here. Philon is without much doubt the <term>sitopoios<note>Literally a "grain-maker," likely a manager of grain supplies or a baker in the service of the estate.</note></term> so often mentioned in the earlier documents. We have a long record of a law-suit between him and Zenon in *Edgar Papyri*, 65, and in line 90 of that text we hear again of his wife Maiandria weaving cloaks. The letter took eighteen days to

<column-break/>

# ZENON PAPYRI.

reach its destination. Possibly it may have been written in Alexandria, though Greeks living in the capital seem as a rule to have used the Macedonian calendar. The phrase "having taken over all the affairs of the city" <note>original: "παρειληφότα τὰ κατὰ πόλιν πάντα"</note> probably means that Apollonios had resumed charge of the administration after a temporary absence. Dionysodoros is no doubt the chief <term>eklogistes<note>An official accountant or auditor responsible for checking government or estate expenditures.</note></term> mentioned in the Revenue Laws.

->Philon to Zenon, greetings. If you are well yourself and other things are to<-
->your mind,<-
->it would be as I wish; I myself am also well. Maiandria wrote to me that<-
->a cloak<-
->you ordered her to weave. At present she is in a weak state <note>meaning ill</note>, but as soon as she has recovered,<-
->the garment shall be yours. Know also that Apollonios has taken over all the affairs of the city<-
<margin>5</margin> ->and Dionysodoros is acting as accountant. I have written to you so that you may know.<-
->Farewell. Year 34, Mecheir 9.<-

VERSO:
Year 30, Mecheir 27. Philon to Zenon (deleted and rewritten on another fold).
Concerning the garment.

Line 2. *ean*: a scribal slip for *eie an* <note>meaning "it would be"</note>.
Line 5. *hein ideis*: read *hin eideis* <note>meaning "so that you may know"</note>.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Accession Journal, no. 48713; Edgar Papyri, 44; Collected Book of Greek Inscriptions from Egypt, 6750.*

### 59264. Letter from Sisouchos to Zenon. — 0.35 m x 0.08 m. — Date: 15th April, 251 B.C.

Zenon had told Sisouchos to see the potter about the jars. If he wishes them to be done at once, he is to write to the potter in order that Sisouchos and the latter may come to him before the time slips away. He has sent Zenon ten chamber-pots and asks him to write if ever he wants anything else.
In *P. S. I.*, 387 we find Sisouchos buying cloth for Zenon, and that is about all we know of him.

*Line 13.* **with him** <note>original: "μεθ’ αὐτοῦ"</note>: as in no. 59210, 2, no. 59292, 180 and often <note>original: "et sæpe"</note>.

## 59265. Receipt. — 0.075 m x 0.095 m. — Date: 3rd December, 252 B.C. or 2nd May, 251 B.C.

Nektosiris, a gardener from the Aphroditopolite district <term>nome</term> <note>A 'nome' was an administrative province in ancient Egypt.</note>, acknowledges that he has received from Zenon a loan of money as stipulated in the contract kept by Nestos, on condition that he will repay half by a certain date and the remainder by another date. The contract mentioned here was probably to the effect that Nektosiris undertook to do a certain amount of work for Zenon and that so much money was to be advanced or lent to him. The receipt may have been duplicate. For the general form compare *P.S.I.*, 863, (b).

Year 34, Phamenoth 10. <note>May 2, 251 B.C.</note> Nektosiris son of Tha...is, from the <gloss>Aphroditopolite</gloss> [[Memphite]] <note>The scribe originally wrote 'Memphite' but corrected it to 'Aphroditopolite', indicating the gardener lived in the district of Aphroditopolis.</note> gardener, acknowledges that he has received from Zenon son of Agreophon, a Caunian, of those under Apollonios 5 the manager <term>dioiketes</term> <note>The dioiketes was the highest financial official in the Ptolemaic government.</note>, the loan recorded in the contract deposited

*Line 1.* **Phaophi** or **Phamenoth**?

<column-break/>

<page-num>113</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

with the contract-keeper Nestos, 15 silver drachmae, on condition that he will repay half in the...
*traces of another line, below which a blank space.*

*Line 7.* **Nestos**: probably the Nestor (or Nestos) of *P.S.I.*, 391, 1 and *P. London Inventory*, 2096, 1.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Accession Journal*, no. 48715.

## 59266. Duplicate receipt. — 0.145 m x 0.08 m. — Date: 7th May, 251 B.C. (plate XXV).

Krotos acknowledges receipt of two fowls and twenty-five eggs. The inner text, which was sealed up, merely gives the total; the outer text states that the two fowls and ten eggs were received on the occasion of the visit of Phileas the auditor <term>eglogistes</term> <note>An eglogistes was an official accountant responsible for checking local finances.</note> and fifteen eggs when Zenon returned from Memphis after escorting Kriton and Stachys.

Year 34, Phamenoth 15. Krotos has 25 bird eggs, 2 fowls.
---
Year 34, Phamenoth 15.
Krotos has, in the presence 5 of Phileas the auditor <term>eglogistes</term>, 2 fowls, 10 bird eggs, and when Zenon arrived [[..]] from Memphis, 10 when he was escorting Kriton and Stachys, 15 bird eggs.

**VERSO:** **Of Krotos.**

*Line 5.* **Phileas the auditor**: see no. 59235, introduction.
*Line 11.* **Kriton**: probably the fleet commander <term>stolarches</term>. **Stachys** may be the person mentioned in *P.S.I.*, 616, 24, possibly also the author of no. 59035.

BIBLIOGRAPHY: *Museum Accession Journal*, no. 48716.

### 59268. Agricultural account. — 0.235 m. x 0.38 m. — Date: 252 or 251 B.C.

Part of an account of receipts and expenses presented by Pyrrhos, one of Zenon's farmers (mentioned in other papyri such as *P.S.I.* 417, 427, and 629), but probably drawn up by Zenon's professional accountant. The handwriting is the same as that found in documents nos. 59269 and 59296. The person addressed as "you" seems to be Pyrrhos himself. The first column lists the sums paid or payable by him, while the second column contains a list of sums received, followed by a statement of the specific agricultural tasks on which the money was spent.

->Column I.<-

> Year 34. Account of farming from
> Pyrrhos, regarding the balance <note>original: "τοῦ ἀπὸ λήμματος"</note>
> You ought to have paid for green crops: 49 drachmae <note>The symbol ⊢ represents the drachma, the standard silver coin of the period.</note>
> and from the cucumber crop: 15 drachmae / [Total:] 164 drachmae <note>The total here likely includes figures from missing lines, as 49 + 15 does not equal 164.</note>
>
> <margin>5</margin> We have received from Limnaios
> in silver: 80 drachmae
> From Pataikion in silver: 100 drachmae
> To Demetrios the donkey-driver in silver: 40 drachmae

<margin>Line 2. "regarding the balance": a phrase parallel to "in the account." It is surely wrong to write this as a single word 'eglogos' as some suggest, nor is there any authority for a single-word form of 'apolemmata'.</margin>
<margin>Line 4. "from the cucumber crop": see document no. 59176 for similar references throughout.</margin>

### 59269. Account with Herakleides the vine-dresser.
— 0.16 m x 0.425 m and 0.19 m x 0.205 m; total height 0.34 m. — Date: probably Year 34 <note>252 or 251 B.C.</note>.

In the same hand as document no. 59268. Herakleides had contracted to pay 375 drachmae for the right of exploiting a vineyard of 37 1/2 arourai <note>An aroura was a Greek-Egyptian measure of land, roughly equivalent to 2,750 square meters or two-thirds of an acre.</note> for one year (cf. *P. S. I.*, 393). Moreover, he had bought vegetables, fruit, and roses on a large scale and had entered into various other transactions, so that the whole amount of what he owed came to 851 drachmae, 5 obols. From this had to be deducted his wages for work on the vineyard in the next year and various other sums due to him, reducing his debt to 305 drachmae, 3 obols. It would be interesting to know on the other side how much the enterprising Herakleides made out of the produce of the vineyard and the sale of the fruit and vegetables.

### Column I.

Regarding Herakleides, the vine-dresser.
The lease <note>original: "ἀπέγδοσις", referring to the farming out of the land</note> of the 37 1/2 arourai
is 375 drachmae.
And for the self-grown onions which
<margin>5</margin> he himself has purchased: 90 drachmae.
For the roses in the park <note>original: "παραδείσωι", a Greek term for a formal garden or pleasure park</note>,
likewise for Year 33: 60 drachmae.
For the figs and pomegranates in the
sandy ground for the same year: 20 drachmae.
<margin>10</margin> And for 150 artabae <note>A dry measure of volume, approximately 30 to 40 liters</note> of wheat which he measured out
from the granary, which he must 
repay—until the time of
repayment, we value them at 5/6 of a drachma <note>original: "ς =" or 6 obols, which equals 1 drachma in the silver standard; however, calculations here suggest a specific conversion rate for the 125 drachmae total</note> per artaba: 125 drachmae.
If it is valued at more or less 
<margin>15</margin> than this, it will be credited to him accordingly.
And he has received an advance for the
25 arourai which he planted additionally
in Year 33: 40 drachmae, of which 

<note>
Line 1. *P. S. I.*, 427, 21 mentions a "Herakleides, v.", which is probably to be expanded into "vine-dresser" <note>original: "ἀμπελουργῶι"</note>. The vine-dresser and the farmer of the ten-thousand arourai (no. 59292, 420) were no doubt different persons.
Line 2. Lease <note>original: "ἀπέγδοσις"</note>: see also *P. S. I.*, 434, 8-13. The vineyard was farmed out to Herakleides at the rate of ten drachmae per aroura.
Line 6. Park <note>original: "παραδείσωι"</note>: the formal garden in Apollonios' estate, as mentioned in document no. 59157.
Line 15. "It will be credited to him accordingly" <note>original: "αὐτῶι ὑπάρξει"</note>.
</note>

of the vegetable garden for Year 32: 22 drachmae, 2 obols.

<note>
Line 22. Vegetable garden <note>original: "λαχανείας"</note>: see also *P. S. I.*, 434, 2-4 and *P. Edg.*, 51, 16.
Line 31. Second year: this cannot be the 2nd year of Ptolemy III Euergetes, but must mean the year following the one mentioned in the first part of the account.
Lines 33-35. Apparently Herakleides had counted on being able to grow some other crops in the vineyard and this privilege had been withdrawn.
Line 35. Either "to sow" <note>original: "παρασπεῖρα[ι]"</note> or "to have sown" <note>original: "παρασπείρασθαι"</note>.

<term>Aroura</term> — An Egyptian unit of land area, roughly 2/3 of an acre.
<term>Drachma</term> — The standard silver coin of the Ptolemaic period.
<term>Obol</term> — A small unit of currency; there were 6 obols in a drachma.
<term>Artaba</term> — A unit of dry volume used for measuring grain.

It provides a vivid look at the diverse economy of a Ptolemaic estate, where a single contractor dealt in everything from wheat and leeks to ornamental flowers.</summary>

### 59270. Letter from Spondates to Zenon. — 0.09 m x 0.30 m. — Date: 8th July, 251 B.C. (pl. XXIV).

Zenon had written to Spondates to send some sycamore wood to Kersat, and the latter promises to do so. Palous was said to have reported that there was no acanthus wood; but in fact the muleteers had carried some from Mea on the 15th and he himself had borrowed a cart and taken some more on the 16th. What was really needed was more sycamore wood, and Spondates asks Zenon to send Theopompos to buy from the ibis-feeders <note>The ibis was sacred to the god Thoth; "ibis-feeders" were specific temple personnel or workers who cared for these birds and, as seen here, engaged in side-trades like selling wood.</note> from Mea, so that the boat-builders may have no excuse for making trouble.
Spondates, who writes in broken Greek, was stationed somewhere in the Memphite nome <note>A "nome" was an administrative district in Ancient Egypt.</note>. He is mentioned again in no. 59130 as an employee of Zenon or Apollonios and in *P. S. I.*, 382, a text which like the present one illustrates the difficulty of procuring wood for building boats.

Spondates to Zenon, greetings. You wrote to me to send sycamore wood to Kersat.
As soon as the mules arrive, having loaded them, we will send it. It was 
reported to you 
that Palous had reported to you that no acanthus wood was available. On the 15th, the 
muleteers 
carried some from Mea, and on the 16th, since they did not arrive, I borrowed a wagon and carried it myself. 
5 I have written to you so that you may know that they do not lack acanthus wood, but rather have 
enough,

*Line 1. Kersat :* probably a village on the river in the south of the Memphite nome.
*Line 2. Read* mules <note>original: "ἡμίονοι"</note> and they reported <note>original: "ἀνηγγέλλοσαν"</note>.
*Line 3. as having reported* <note>original: "ὡς ἀνεγγελκότα"</note> : the meaning and grammar are difficult to follow. Perhaps the second "to you" <note>original: "σοι"</note> is a mistake for "to them" : ‘the boat-builders have told you that Palous has told them’?
*Line 4. carried :* <note>original: "ἤροσαν"</note> active rather than intransitive. Mea or Meia was a Memphite village (see WILCKEN, *Ostraka*, no. 1102 and no. 59173, 4).
*Line 5. I wrote :* <note>original: "ἔγραψα"</note> for "I have written". Read acanthus <note>original: "ἀκανθίνων"</note> and but <note>original: "ἀλλὰ"</note>.

<page-break/>

<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

<page-num>119</page-num>

but there will be a need for sycamore wood, for the cut wood that was bought will not be enough for them. 
The ibis-feeders came to me yesterday, <gloss>those from Mea</gloss>, wanting to 
sell more cheaply. Send Theopompos for the purchase, so that we may 
refute the excuse of the boat-builders; 
<gloss>for</gloss> 
they are wicked and look for an excuse. Farewell. Year 35, Pachons 17.

VERSO:
10 Year 35, Pachons 17. Spondates &emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; To Zenon.
[regarding] wood.

*Line 7. Read* they arrived <note>original: "παρεγένοντο"</note> and yesterday <note>original: "ἐχθές"</note>. The *ibis-feeders* <note>original: "ἰβιοβοσκοί"</note> are of course the feeders of the sacred birds.

<meta>BIBL. : Journal d’entrée du Musée, no. 48720; P. Edg., 45; ROSTOVTZEFF, Large Estate, p. 123; Sammelbuch, 6751.</meta>

### 59271. Fragment of letter from Neesis to Zenon. — 0.12 m x 0.15 m. — Date: 31st July, 251 B.C.

Neesis, who was a potter, writes about certain jars which he has made, about wages for the workmen and about the preparation of ovens for the pitching <note>Pitching refers to the process of lining the inside of porous earthenware jars with resin or pitch to make them waterproof, essential for storing wine or oil.</note> of the pottery. The text shows incidentally how variable was the capacity of the type of jar called a kerameion <note>A "kerameion" was a standard liquid measure, but its actual volume could vary by region or maker.</note>.

---

...[ . . . ] as indeed it is necessary [ . . ] to hold 
... [ . . . ] but you 
ordered them to hold [seven] choes <note>A "chous" (plural "choes") was a Greek liquid measure roughly equal to 3.3 liters.</note>, 
<gloss>uniformly</gloss> 
but I have made [jars of] six-choes. I have written 
5 to you to send me 12 [drachmae]. You, then, 
send them to me, so that I may distribute them to the 
<gloss>so that</gloss> 
workers [[so that]] they may be available for me. 
Let there be prepared for the 
pitching of the pottery ten ovens. 
10 Farewell. Year 35, Payni 10.

*Lines 1-2.* I fail to see how these lines are to be restored. "As indeed it is necessary"? <note>original: "ὡς ἄρα δ[εῖ]"</note>
*Line 4. Read* uniformly <note>original: "ἐφ' ὁμαλίαν"</note> (*P. Petr.*, III, 43 (2), col. IV, note).
*Lines 8, 9. Read* let ovens be prepared <note>original: "κατασκευασθήτωσαν κλίβανοι"</note>.
*Line 10.* "25" <note>original: "κε"</note> is no doubt a slip for "35" <note>original: "λε"</note>.

**59272. Letter from Timotheos to Zenon.** — 0.16 m x 0.45 m and 0.16 m x 0.14 m. — Date: 17th August, 251 B.C. (pl. XXVI).

Timotheos had delivered Zenon’s letter about the 1000(?) <term>drachmae</term> <note>The drachma was the standard silver coin of the Greek world.</note> which he had requested Python the banker to enter as paid by Timotheos (cf. *P. S. I.*, 383, 10). Python, however, had made some objection which is not clear, and Timotheos asks Zenon to write to Python and press him to instruct Theodoros the collector to credit him with payment. At the end of the letter he says that he has sent his son Dionysios to take delivery of something.
From an unpublished fragment in Cairo (*Journal d’entrée*, no. 48914) we learn that Timotheos was a <term>nomarch</term> <note>A nomarch was a high-ranking official responsible for the administration of a "nome" or province.</note>.

1. Timotheos to Zenon, greetings. Since we [delivered] to Python the
2. letter from you concerning the 1,000 [drachmae which] you wrote to him to credit on
3. our behalf, he said that you had previously said [.........]. You will therefore do
4. well by instructing him [to write] more urgently to Theodoros the tax collector
<margin>5</margin> 5. to accept payment for us. [By doing] this, you will have
6. helped us greatly. Concerning the [........] we have sent Dionysios
7. the son to you so that he may [receive it from you]. ->Farewell. Year 35, Payni 27.<-

->VERSO:<-

->To Zenon.<-

*Line 2. the 1,000* : only the upper part of a figure representing one or more thousand is preserved.
*Line 3. he said that you* : or "he stated." — *from these things already*? <note>original: "ἀ[πὸ τούτων ἤδη]"</note>

<meta>BIBL. : Journal d’entrée du Musée, no. 48722.</meta>

**59273. Letter from Iphimedon to Zenon.** — 0.105 m x 0.275 m. — Date: 24th August, 251 B.C.

Asking Zenon to send somebody to take over from a slave sent by Iphimedon two large and three small ovens for the calves. Zenon is also to write and inform him what arrangement he has made about the <term>kleros</term> <note>A "kleros" was a plot of land granted to a military settler.</note>.
The <term>kleros</term> may perhaps have belonged to Iphimedon. As for the ovens, they must have been used by the <term>calf-rearers</term> <note>original: "μοσχοτρόφοι" (moschotrophoi)</note> (*P. S. I.*, 409, 2) for preparing the calves’ food; cf. *P. Petr.*, III, 46, (4), "into the oven built for the reception of the olyra <note>A type of hulled wheat or spelt used for animal fodder.</note> for the nourishment of the calves."

<column-break/>

**59274. Account of pigs.** — 0.115 m x 0.13 m. — Date: 19th September, 251 B.C.

->Terbonis son of Pameus<-
1. Year 35, Epeiph 30. What he delivered
2. to Pammeneis: 44 young piglets <note>original: "χοιροδέλφακες" (choirodelphakes)</note>,
3. 13 large young pigs <note>original: "δέλφακες" (delphakes)</note>, 12 females, / Total: 69.
<margin>5</margin> 5. Areus son of Paphounis: 47 young piglets,
6. 4 large young pigs, 13 breeding sows <note>original: "τοκάδες" (tokades)</note>, / Total: 64.

*Lines 1-2. Presumably Pammeneis*, though the second μ has the form of a π.
*Line 2. young piglets* : the *delphakes* are young pigs and the *choirodelphakes* still younger.
*Line 3. females* : likely meaning female victims <note>original: "ἱερεῖα" (hiereia)</note> or female young pigs.

<meta>BIBL. : Journal d’entrée du Musée, no. 48724.</meta>

**59275. Letter from [....]nis to Sesos.** — 0.335 m x 0.105 m. — Date: 3rd October, 251 B.C.

The writer announces that certain persons, whom I conjecture to have been the farmers of the <term>fourth-part tax on salted fish</term> <note>original: "τετάρτη ταρίχου" (tetarte tarichou). This was a 25% tax on preserved fish.</note> (see no. 59206), have taken him to prison after searching and sealing up the house and after carrying off a quantity of meat. Sesos is therefore requested to intercede with Zenon. Similarly a certain Achommneuis employed by Zenon has been put in prison by Horos the village scribe for some default in connection with the salt tax. The village in question was probably not Philadelphia.

<sig>Catalogue of the Museum, no. 59140. 16</sig>

It also touches on legal troubles involving imprisonment over tax disputes related to salted meat and salt taxes.</summary>

whose scribe at this time was Anosis. No doubt the letter was transmitted to Zenon by Sesos.

[....]nis to Sesos, greetings.

Verso: To Sesos.

*Line 2.* This line must have contained the subject of "sealed up"; and as the persons who sealed up the house must have been endowed with legal authority I think the above restoration is justifiable.
*Line 5.* α[. .] : the second letter might be g, x, p or t. "Tender" <note>original: "hapala"</note>, suggested by "hard" <note>original: "sklera"</note> in line 9, seems rather long for the lacuna. "Vessels" <note>original: "angeia"</note> is another possibility.
*Line 6.* "went so far as to" <note>original: "hoioi esan"</note>. Cf. Harpocration <note>An ancient Greek lexicographer</note>: "you are such as" and "you are able to" <note>original: "hoios ei" and "hoios te ei"</note>; the former without the "te" signifies what you wish and have chosen, the latter with the "te" signifies what you are able to do.
*Lines 8-10.* If my restoration of line 2 is correct, the writer was accused of some offence with regard to the tax on pickled meats; but I do not venture to restore line 8, and "in exchange for it" <note>original: "anti auton"</note> in line 10 is rather doubtful. The beginnings of lines 9, 10 are given from my first copy, but the fragment containing them has disappeared.

**59276. Letter from Timotheos (?) to Zenon.** — 0.14 m x 0.18 m. — Date: September-October, 251 B.C.

The letter is in the hand of no. 59272 and the writer is probably Timotheos, though no. 59279, from Pasis, seems to have been written by the same scribe. He reports that he has received the squared stones, <term>cubes</term> <note>original: "kyboi", here referring to precisely cut stone blocks for construction</note>, brought by Psemtheus and that he has handed them over to Dionysios, apparently to be transported to Alexandria or to some place on the river. For another possible instance of the export of stone from the Fayoum cf. no. 59176, 334.

[Timotheos to Zenon] greetings. You wrote to us about the squared stones so [that]
[we might receive them from] Psemtheus. Having therefore received from him the squared stones [.]
[which he brought down (?)] and having loaded them onto boats, we delivered them to Dion[ysios]
[.... having pa]id freight charges of 15 <term>drachmae</term> <note>A standard unit of silver currency</note> to the workers who lifted the [....].
5 [.... and to the agent(?)] of ours in Ptolemais I wrote to give a wa[ge]
[....] to the great river <note>The Nile</note>. Regarding the squared stones [not yet brought]
[down, we] instructed Psemtheus to inspect them as quickly as possible [....]
Farewell. Year 35, Mesore [..]

Verso: To Zenon.

*Lines 3-4.* Perhaps "to Dionysios the son" (cf. no. 59272, 6-7).
*Line 5.* No doubt Ptolemais on the Bahr Youssef <note>A canal that serves as the main water source for the Fayoum oasis</note>.
*Line 6.* Perhaps [to those bringing them] to the great river. We may infer that the cargo was being carried to the Nile.
*Line 7.* "to inspect" <note>original: "episkepsasthai" or "episkepsasai"</note>. Part of the word may have been written in line 8.

### 59279. Letter from Pasis to Zenon. — 0.165 m x 0.335 m. — Date: 9th December, 251 B.C.

The letter seems to be in the same hand as nos. 59272, 59276, and it may be noted

<column-break/>

<page-num>125</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

that the writer speaks of Timotheos in line 3. There had been some previous correspondence about procuring wild peas <note>original: "arakos"</note> (if my reading of line 1 is right) and Pasis now reports how the matter stands. Towards the end he mentions that he had found that Zenon had sailed down the river. We learn in fact from *P. S. I.*, 361 that Zenon was at this very time staying in Alexandria with Apollonios, while no. 59284, 1 suggests (but this is less certain) that he had been in town earlier in the year. An unpublished papyrus in the Michigan collection shows that he had contemplated going down to attend the <term>Pentaeteris</term> <note>A prestigious pan-Hellenic festival celebrated every four years in Alexandria</note>, which was celebrated in the autumn of this year^(1), but *P. S. I.*, 364 seems to prove that he was in Philadelphia at the time of the games.
Whether this Pasis was the Jew mentioned in no. 59241 is as yet uncertain.

Pasis to Zenon, greetings. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . regarding the wild peas. Having given
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Apollodotos
the letter [. . . . . . . . . . to Herak]leopolis to buy [them]; and until now
no[. . . . .] . [. .] . [. .] . [. . . .] of the peas. And to Timotheos we
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;gave
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<gloss>the</gloss>
the letter for him and he wrote back to you. <gloss>And 2 <term>artabas</term></gloss> <note>A dry measure of volume, roughly 30-40 liters</note> those from
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Maimachos
I have, and your agent has brought [them] to you. If the others do not send [theirs],
write
to us so that we might buy them up; for we have found them being sold here. This
letter I wrote to you [to be delivered] to Philadelphia. We found you had sailed
downstream. But seeing that the land had receded <note>Likely referring to the receding of the Nile floodwaters or irrigation</note> [. . . . . . . . . . .] we therefore [decided] to buy up
as much as the land had need of. We have therefore written to you so that you may know.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;10&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Farewell. Year 35, Phaophi 16.

**VERSO** :&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To Zenon.

<note>Line 1. Apollodotos: cf. no. 59293, 17.</note>
<note>Line 2. Perhaps "I traveled to Herakleopolis to buy [them]" (original: "apedēmesa eis Herakleous polein... agorasai"), but the reading is very doubtful.</note>
<note>Lines 6-7. If "this" refers to the present letter, the meaning will be: ‘I wrote the above letter to be delivered to you in Philadelphia, but discovered, etc.’.</note>
<note>Line 7. Perhaps "we missed you" (original: "katebalomen (de) se"). Or the omission of "but" (de) and the superfluous "therefore" (oun) in the next line may be due to the fact that the author was using a foreign language.</note>
<note>Line 8. [We decided] or [we were compelled] or some such word.</note>

## 59283. Letter from Hierokles to Zenon. — 0.10 m x 0.075 m and 0.10 m x 0.125 m. — Date of reception: 14th March, 250 B.C.

Not in the same hand as either no. 59284 or no. 59285. The latter is certainly by Hierokles, the author of no. 59148. Whether the present letter and no. 59284 are also by him is uncertain; they do not open with his usual form of greeting, but neither that nor the difference in handwriting is decisive. Nos. 59284 and 59285 come from Alexandria, but we have no certain clue to the provenance of the present letter. The writer asks Zenon to intercede on behalf of the bearer, a cleruch <note>a soldier-settler granted land by the king in exchange for military service</note> who was being threatened with confiscation and the exaction of certain arrears.

Hierokles [to Zenon, greeting.
He happens to be at the [
of Tlepolemos to us [
is being exacted for years [
5 if they give a stay of execution [
so that I am persuaded . [

<column-break/>

]...es, the one delivering the letter to you,
a relative of [my?] wife]. But those with [
have paid into the royal treasury <note>original: "basilikon"</note> and moreover
you will do well therefore to take care that
]...of the new [crops?] and Apollodotos is taking care,
]to do.

->Farewell.<-

**VERSO** :
Year 35, Tybi 21. [To] Zenon. 3
Hierokles.

<margin>Line 2. I cannot say how many letters are wanting. "Of our wife" is perhaps too short.</margin>
<margin>Line 3. Tlepolemos or to Tlepolemos; see P. S. I., 513, 8. Perhaps "have transferred the allotment."</margin>
<margin>Line 5. Probably "until the new harvest," in which case "and" begins a new clause, rather than "concerning the collection of the new crops."</margin>

**BIBLIOGRAPHY** : *Journal d'entrée of the Museum, no. 48736.*

### 59284. Letter from Hierokles to Zenon. — 0.125 m x 0.12 m. — Date: 14th November, 251 B.C.

Only the left side of this text is in our collection. The right side is in Florence and has been published in *P. S. I.*, 575. The middle portion is still missing, but I have given a provisional restoration <note>bracketed text indicates portions restored by the editor</note> to make the sense clear.
Hierokles, one of Zenon’s Alexandrian correspondents (see no. 59283), writes him a letter of recommendation on behalf of a certain Hermogenes who was coming as a cleruch <term>soldier-settler who received a land grant in exchange for military service</term> to the Fayoum or to some neighbouring district. If he is not stationed at Philadelphia, Zenon is requested to write to his commanding officer, wherever he may be. The letter was carried by Hermogenes himself and was not delivered till four months after the date on which it was written.

Hierokles to Zenon, greeting. [I mentioned to you previously in] the city <note>original: "em polei"; this refers to the capital, Alexandria</note> regarding Hermogenes, one of the
cleruchs, and I thought it necessary [to remind you again. You would do] well, when
he delivers
the letter to you, [to make yourself better known to him and, if he] has [any] need of you,
doing for him whatever is [within your power; for he has been recommended] to us by certain friends
<margin>5</margin> with whom [he happens to spend much of his time. And if the cleruchs] are not stationed in the
places
around you, write [carefully to the official] <note>original: "epistatên"; a local administrative overseer</note> who is there, so that through you they are not
neglected,
but may receive [every possible kindness].
->[Farewell]. Year 35, Gorpiaios 25.<- <note>Macedonian calendar date</note>

**VERSO :**
Year 35, Tybi 22. <note>Egyptian calendar date, showing a four-month delivery time</note> &emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; To Zenon.
<margin>10</margin> Hierokles
regarding Hermogenes.

<note>Line 1. "In the city": see no. 59279, introduction.</note>
<note>Line 2. "I thought": an "epistolary imperfect," a common Greek grammar feature where the writer uses the past tense to describe his thoughts at the moment of writing from the perspective of the reader.</note>

<meta>BIBLIOGRAPHY: Journal d’entrée of the Museum, no. 48737; P. S. I., 575.</meta>

---

### 59285. Letter from Hierokles to Zenon. — 0.10 m x 0.12 m and 0.095 m x 0.12 m. — Date: 2nd March, 250 B.C.

This letter, which is in the usual hand of Hierokles (Plate IV) and was presumably

<column-break/>

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI. 129</header>

written in Alexandria, is a request to Zenon to give the slave Onesimos fifty drachmæ <term>standard Greek silver currency</term> for a certain piece of business.

well; I
am also healthy myself. Give to Onesimos for the [ . . . . . . . . . ] of Kallikon
fifty drachmæ. <term>├</term> <note>The symbol "├" is the shorthand for drachmæ</note>

and he has written to us that he has from [ . . .

Hierokles regarding Onesimos' 50 drachmæ <term>├</term> 50.

**59288. Letter from Antipatros to Zenon.** — 0.16 m $\times$ 0.085 m. — Date : 26th March, 250 B. C. (pl. XXV).

Written along the fibres and folded horizontally. Antipatros announces that he has sent Thrason the barber, whom the bearers of the letter from Memphis accuse of having maltreated them, in order that Zenon may examine him. He adds that evidence is offered by several people that Thrason had been first wronged by the others. We have no clue as to why the quarrel was referred to Zenon, but evidently he had some authority over one or other of the parties.

**59289. Royal oath.** — 0.255 m $\times$ 0.155 m. — Date : May-June, 250 B. C. (pl. XXVIII).

Metchopsis, captain of a boat belonging to Apollonios, swears the usual oath that he will not take on board any dutiable cargo. The writing is along the fibres and the papyrus is folded horizontally.
We learn from the text that the eponymous priestess <note>A priestess whose name was used to date the year</note> of year 35 was Bilistiche daughter of Philon. Scholars seem agreed that this must be the famous Bilistiche, one of the mistresses of Ptolemy Philadelphus. I formerly advanced the view that the priestess was more probably the daughter of a courtier, as we know from another instance (*Polybius*, XV, 31 : <note>original: "genomenēs de thygatros autōi tautēn Agathokleian prosēgoreusen"</note> "having had a daughter, he called her Agathokleia") that courtiers were in the habit of calling their children after the king’s favourites and as it is not certain whether the *kanephoroi* <term>basket-bearers; a high-ranking religious office for young women</term> were matrons or girls; but on this point I must yield to superior authority.

[In the reign of] Ptolemy son of Ptolemy Soter, year 35,
[under the priest Ptole]my son of Andromachos of Alexander and the Brother-Gods,
[the basket-bearer of A]rsinoe Philadelphus being Bilistiche daughter of Philon, in the month of Dystros.
[Metchopsis son of Pherenouthis, a Bubastite, pilot] of the Kasiotic boat of Apollonios the finance minister, [swears by King] Ptolemy son of King Ptolemy and Berenice,
5 Savior Gods, and Arsinoe Philadelphus, the Brother-Gods, and the Savior Gods
[their par]ents, that he will [not put] into the Kasiotic boat anything subject to customs duty under any pretext...

*Lines 2-3.* In *P.S.I.*, 515 the priest and priestess in Pachons of year 35 are called Neoptolemos and Arsinoe, and the same pair were in office in Mesore of year 34 (*P. Hib.*, 98). Either then the scribe of *P.S.I.*, 515 has made a mistake or else Andromachos and Bilistiche were not appointed till some time after the beginning of the regnal year.
*Line 7.* Kasiotic boat: see the note on "Kasiotic works" in *P. Ox.*, 55, 8. The Kasiotes were renowned for their skill in carpentry, <note>original: "dokous epi dokois synaptontes"</note> "joining beam upon beam," and perhaps the Kasiotic boat differed in its construction from the ordinary Nile-boat. The phrase just quoted rather suggests a boat of the old Egyptian type made of mortised planking without ribs.
*Lines 7-8.* Nothing [methden]: in line 18 the letters after "mē" seem to be "oi", in which case I do not know what to read except wine [oinon]. Nevertheless I have ventured to print "nothing," which...

### 59291. Petition to Zenon from Harmais and Teos. — 0.17 m x 0.295 m. — Date : 251-250 B. C.

The petitioners were two Egyptian brick-makers, as may be inferred from "the ten thousand" in line 5, for 10,000 was the regular unit in reckoning quantities of bricks. They had been ordered by Zenon to work for four months, perhaps under compulsion (cf. *P. S. I.*, 440, 11-15 and no. 59230), but had been kept working by Horos, Zenon’s foreman, for a whole year extra and had been shamefully sweated <note>This means they were overworked to an extreme degree</note>. They ask Zenon to inquire into their case and bring Horos to account.

The petition is a good example of Egyptian Greek, the work of a native interpreter who translated the complaint of the brick-makers into the best Greek at his command, much like a present-day petition written in French or English for the benefit of a European inspector. As there is no address on the back, it was probably handed to Zenon by the petitioners.

To Zenon, greeting from Harmais and Teos. We entered the works from Year 34, you having ordered us to attend to the works for four months. But it has been sixteen months since we have been attending to the works with Horos your assistant; and he makes us complete whatever we undertake and whatever the strangers <note>Likely native brick-makers from a different village, rather than foreigners</note> undertake. <margin>5</margin> And what they give us for every ten thousand is five hundred, and even this he exacts from us. You would do well therefore to look after us and not allow us to be overlooked, perishing by hunger, and calling Horos to account with us.
->May you be fortunate.<-

*Line 3.* Read *since we have been attending*.
*Line 4.* *undertake* : equivalent to 'take upon oneself as a contract'. 
*Line 5.* The meaning might be: 'and what they give us as recompense is 500 for every 10,000 bricks, and even this Horos takes from us', or else: 'and what they leave us to finish is 500 on every 10,000, and he exacts this extra work from us'.
*Lines 6-8.* The grammar here becomes as hopeless as the plight of the petitioners.

**Bibl. :** *Museum Entry Journal*, no. 48745.

### 59292. Long corn account, in many fragments. — Date : 250 B. C. (Year 29).

The following text consists of a large number of fragments of a very badly preserved roll, presented to the Museum by Grenfell on his last visit to Egypt. Little is left of the lower half of the roll, but a considerable amount of the upper half. I have not found it possible to arrange all the pieces in their original order or to determine the exact place of the few remaining parts of the lower half; and the order in which the...

<summary>This page contains a captain's oath to prevent smuggling through the Kasion customs house and a moving petition from two Egyptian brick-makers, Harmais and Teos, who plead for relief after being forced to work for sixteen months instead of four.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy II Philadelphus, Metchopsis, customs evasion, Zenon, Harmais and Teos, brick-making, labor exploitation, Egyptian Greek, Horos, Kasion customs</keywords>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Zenon Archive. Following the legal and administrative documents of the previous pages, we now transition into a massive series of agricultural accounts. These records detail the grain management on the "dorea" (gift-estate) of Apollonios, the finance minister, during the 35th year of Ptolemy II Philadelphus's reign. The text shifts from the specific grievances of workers to the systematic tracking of the estate’s primary wealth: its harvest.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>
<header>CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM.</header>

The way these separate fragments are printed here is quite arbitrary. I have also omitted a number of small fragments of no intrinsic interest. The text, which is one long account or rather a series of accounts dealing with the same subject, covers both the front and back of the papyrus <note>original: "recto and verso"</note>, the writing on the back beginning opposite the end of the front. Thus, if fragment (c) precedes (d) on the front, (d) will precede (c) on the back. The front seems to be written in one hand, but on the back two or more different hands can be distinguished. The subject of the account is the expenditure of grain on the property of Apollonios.

The grain consists chiefly of wheat and barley, <term>barley-wheat</term> <note>original: "κριθόπυρον"</note>, <term>emmer</term> <note>original: "ὀλύρα," a species of hulled wheat common in ancient Egypt</note>, and <term>chickpea</term> <note>original: "ἐρέβινθος"</note>. But besides these, the lists and entries mention almost every kind of field-crop grown in Egypt at that time and add considerably to the information on this subject collected by Schnebel in his work *Agriculture* <note>original: "Landwirtschaft"</note>, chapter four. In fragment (a), which has been fitted together out of many separate pieces, we have a large part of the <term>grain account</term> <note>original: "σιτικὸς λόγος"</note> of Maron (see *P. S. I.*, 500) for the month of Athyr <note>Approximately November in the modern calendar</note>. It begins with a statement of the amount in hand of each kind of grain at the beginning of the month; then follows a detailed list of the quantities given out day by day during the month; and at the end is a summing up. This is followed (columns XIII-XVI) by an account of the corn in the byre given out by the employees of Herakleides. On the back of fragment (a) is an account of the grain put at the disposal of Herakleides, who was the chief farmer of the <term>gift-estate</term> <note>original: "δωρεά," a large tract of land granted by the King to high officials</note>, by order of Apollonios, and of the quantities which he actually expended from year to year. The other fragments are smaller, but seem all to belong to similar accounts, though, as I have already said, I cannot place them in their original order.

The corn was given out for sowing, for wages, for the feeding of pigs and dogs, and various other purposes; and there are many interesting details in the separate entries, as for instance in fragment (a), columns V-VI and fragment (g). But naturally the largest quantities were delivered to the cultivators. In many cases we find the entry "seed for the <term>privately-sown lands</term>" <note>original: "σπέρμα εἰς τὰ ἰδιόσπορα"</note> and in one case "for the private-sown land" <note>singular: "εἰς τὴν ἰδιόσπορον"</note>. Without much doubt these entries refer to that part of the estate which was cultivated by means of paid employees. In contrast to the privately-sown land, we find another category called "that through the local people" <note>original: "ἡ διὰ τῶν λαῶν"</note>, which must mean the land leased to native peasants or at least cultivated by them on contract. These peasants came from many of the villages in the neighborhood, chiefly or wholly from those in the Memphite <term>nome</term> <note>administrative district</note>, such as Kerke, Pitos, and Boutos; several of their names were already known to us from the contracts of year 31, nos. 59173 and 59182. The grain which they received for seed would no doubt be repaid along with the rent. In addition to the seed, considerable quantities of corn were delivered to them as <term>cultivation allowance</term> <note>original: "κάτεργον"</note>, which means an allowance to enable them to prepare the fields for the next crop (see *P. Lille*, I, 39-51, introduction). The cultivation allowance, like the loans for donkeys (no. 59173), was probably also repaid along with the <term>produce-rent</term> <note>original: "ἐκφόριον"</note> and the seed (*P. Hib.*, 119).

The quantities of corn given out were calculated by two different measures, the <term>receiving-measure</term> <note>original: "δοχικόν"</note> and the <term>spending-measure</term> <note>original: "ἀνηλωτικόν"</note>. It is not always possible to distinguish them, but as a general rule we may say that the corn delivered to cultivators is measured by the receiving-measure, while that delivered for private consumption is measured by the spending-measure. From

---

<page-num>135</page-num>
<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

fragment (f) we can infer with absolute certainty that in the spending-measure the <term>artaba</term> <note>the standard unit of dry volume in Egypt</note> contained forty <term>choinikes</term> <note>a smaller volume unit, roughly equivalent to a liter</note>; and we know from various other documents that this was the measure employed in Zenon's household accounts (e.g., *P. S. I.*, 580). The fractions employed in measuring by the receiving-measure—2/3, 1/2, 1/3, 1/4, 1/6, 1/8, 1/12—suggest that, if the lowest common unit was a whole choinix, the artaba must have contained 24 or 48 choinikes, or, if the lowest common unit was half a choinix, 36. But this consideration is of little weight against the evidence of *P. Hib.*, 74, which shows that the receiving-measure stood to the spending-measure in the ratio of 21 to 20. As it is now certain that the artaba of the spending-measure contained 40 choinikes, it is almost equally certain that the artaba of the receiving-measure contained 42.

Mr. Bell has shown me two long accounts in the British Museum (*Inv.* nos. 2360, 2361), resembling ours in many points and much better preserved. For the study of Egyptian agriculture and the management of Apollonios' estate, they seem to form a very important complement to the present text.

To left of column I:

->From Maron,<-
->grain [account].<-

*Line 366.* <gloss>bu</gloss> : see line 454. Probably an abbreviation of a word beginning in *bu* or *bou*. Comparing the ending ]tiou in text no. 59177, 5, I propose to read **Byzantine** <note>original: "Byzantiou"</note>. For "containing clods" see line 370.

# 59293. Account of barley. — 0 m. 09 x 0 m. 86. — Date: 251 or 250 B.C. (pl. XXVII).

Written along the fibers on a narrow strip of papyrus. It is a note, perhaps drawn up by a <term>sitometres</term> <note>original: "σιτομέτρης"; a state-appointed grain-measurer responsible for certifying the quantity and quality of grain</note>, of the quantities of barley issued to various persons in years 33, 34 and 35. The quantities were large, the total being nearly 36,600 <term>artabs</term> <note>A dry volume measure, roughly 30 to 40 liters.</note>. From the fractions 2/3, 1/2, 1/3, 1/4 it seems probable that the artab employed was the <term>dochikon</term> <note>original: "δοχικόν"; the "receiving" standard of measurement, which was slightly larger than the standard used for outgoing payments</note> (see no. 59292, introduction).

### Column I.

Year 33, Mecheir 29
To Koroibides, a loan: 700 [artabs of] barley
[In the month of] Pauni
To Ptolemaios from Arsinoe
In exchange for 127 [artabs of] poppy seed: 765 [artabs of] barley
[In the month of] Hathyr
To Diogenes for the Memphite district: 1,108 1/2 1/4 [artabs of] barley

### Column II.

To Sosos for Memphis: 470 [artabs of] barley
Total for Year 33: 3,043 1/2 1/4 [artabs of] barley

Year 34, [month of] Mecheir, to Pyrrhos
through Sosratos: 500 [artabs of] barley
To Petearmotes: 500 [artabs of] barley

### Column III.

To Koroibides: 6,000 [artabs of] barley

Line 1. The year employed in this document is clearly the financial year which began in Mecheir.
Line 4. *Arsinoe*: presumably the village in the district of Herakleides (*P. Teb.*, II, p. 369). For Ptolemaios see *P. S. I.*, 360.
Line 5. Another half artab added to the 127 would make the ratio exactly 1 : 6.
Line 7. *Diogenes*: possibly the Diogenes of no. 59292 rather than the <term>nomarch</term> <note>A high-ranking provincial administrator</note> (*P. Lille*, I, 39-55); but that the Etearchos mentioned in line 14 was the nomarch of that name seems less doubtful.
Line 8. *Sosos*: a commercial agent; in *P. Mich.*, 45 we find him selling a cargo of grain.
Line 10. *Pyrrhos*: see no. 59268.

---

<header>ZENON PAPYRI.</header>

To Etearchos: 1,436 [artabs of] barley
To Hippias: 200 [artabs of] barley
To Pasis, the agent of Python: 2,000 [artabs of] barley
To Apollodotos, grain collector: 1,060 [artabs of] barley
To Areudotes, grain collector: 2,427 1/2 [artabs of] barley

To Koroibides: [ 3,511 2/3? artabs of barley ]
... of Philiskos
In exchange for 120 2/3 artabs of poppy seed: 844 2/3 [artabs of] barley
and toward the fulfillment
of the account of Apollonios: 617 [artabs of] barley

Year 35, [month of] Mecheir
To Diogenes: 4,700 [artabs of] barley
and transport costs: 392 1/2 [artabs of] barley
To Demetrios, grain collector:
3,175 [artabs of] barley
[In the month of] Epeiph, to Laboitis: 806 1/15

### Column VI.

To Herakleides: 4,325 1/2 1/4 [artabs of] barley
To Python: 1,000 [artabs of] barley
To Areudotes: 565 1/2 [artabs of] barley
To Harmodios: 220 [artabs of] barley
Total: 14,338 1/2 1/4 [artabs of] barley
The grand total: 36,599 1/3 artabs of barley

Lines 16-17. *Pasis and Apollodotos*: these may be the persons who figure in no. 59279.
Line 21. Here the poppy seed is valued higher, the ratio being 1 : 7.
Lines 22-23. Probably this amount had to be taken from the granary to meet payments due from Apollonios.
Line 27. The cost of carriage was in the ratio of 1 to 13 1/3 artabs, the same as the ratio between silver and gold. Cf. no. 59292, 485.

<summary>This document records large-scale distributions of barley from the estate of Apollonios over three years, totaling over 36,000 artabs. It details grain issued to regional officials, transport fees, and exchanges for poppy seeds, illustrating the massive scale of agricultural management in the Ptolemaic period.</summary>
<keywords>barley account, Zenon Papyri, artab, Ptolemaic economy, grain collector, sitometres, poppy seed exchange, transport costs, Apollonios, Koroibides, Etearchos</keywords>

## **59294. Fragment.** — 18 cm x 8.5 cm — Date: 250 B.C.

The text on the front, written along the fibers in a clear, large hand, is part of a letter or memorandum from an Egyptian employee, perhaps a certain Petosiris. That on the back is in equally clear but rather more cursive writing and gives a list of Egyptians from whom or to whom money was owing.

->having gone away from us...<-
->themselves by themselves...<-
->Erieus indeed found...<-
->until Hathyr 30 <unclear>year</unclear>...<-
->5 of which the itemized list is as follows:<-
->and for Horus, found...<-
->until Phaophi 30...<-
->because of this...<-

*Line 2. "Themselves by themselves":* <note>original: "καταυτοί"</note> Likely meaning "on their own" or "individually." Compare similar phrasing in the Gospel of Mark.
*Lines 6-7.* These lines are separated by short intervals from the preceding and the following lines.
*Line 8. Perhaps "because of this I [paid?] him everything."*

## **59295. Enumeration of women working wool.** — 25 cm x 8.5 cm — Date: 250 B.C.

A short list giving the numbers of the women engaged in the working of wool, in accordance with the <term>registration</term> <note>original: "ἀπογραφή"; a formal census or declaration for tax purposes</note> of year 35, in three villages in the Fayoum. Compare another papyrus in which Zoilos the <term>manager</term> <note>original: "oiconome"; the financial administrator of a district</note> asks for the number ("send to me the...")

<meta>Catalogue of the Museum, no. 59140.</meta>

### 59296. Account of payments. — 20 cm $\times$ 21 cm. — Date: 250 B.C. (Plate XXX).

The writing, which runs along the fibers, varies in size, and lines 12-17 are in a different hand from the rest. The payments are partly for the repairing of canals and stone-cutting and partly for guards. Of these, three classes are specified: ten village guards of Philadelphia together with ten Arabs; five guards posted in the prison; six watchmen to look after the canal embankments during the inundation. The regular guards have a salary of six drachmae a month, while the watchmen on the canal banks, who were probably impressed from among the peasants, are only paid two and a half drachmae. It is not clear whether the estate itself paid for the upkeep of the canals and the maintenance of public security in its own territory or whether some of these payments were made on behalf of the Government; but I think that the latter is more likely and that the money came eventually from the proceeds of the <term>chomatikōn</term> <note>A tax specifically for the maintenance of the dike and canal system.</note> and the <term>phylakitikon</term> <note>A tax used to fund the local police and security forces.</note> (cf. *P. S. I.*, 344).

->]<-
->[for] protecting with brushwood<-
->[the] canal and for<-

<margin>Lines 34-35.</margin> In the *Papyri of the Italian Society* (P.S.I.), 423, we find prisoners working under the direction of a stone-cutter <note>original: "λατόμος" (latomos)</note>, and it is possible that in the present case also the rough work was done by prisoners. # **59297. Memorandum about money.** — 43 cm $\times$ 55 cm. — Date: probably 250 B.C.

This memorandum, which is written in a large hand along the fibers of the papyrus, seems to be a note about Zenon’s balance at the bank. It was probably written by his own accountant, not by the banker; for the word "we paid in" <note>original: "ἠριθμήσαμεν" (ērithmēsamen), literally 'we counted out'</note> in line 6 must mean ‘we deposited.’ Lines 1-11 give the sums which had been deposited to his credit. Lines 13-28 state the amounts which he was paying to the Treasury through the bank. Several of the latter are clearly payments of taxes on behalf of other people; and this accords with what we have inferred from other texts, namely that Zenon and his colleagues, though not regular tax-farmers <note>original: "τελῶναι" (telōnai), officials who bid for the right to collect taxes</note>, took an active part in collecting Government taxes in their district. The balance is struck in lines 29-30.

->Column I.<-

[Me]morandum [
[Th]ere is a depos[it
of silver: 2,000 <term>drachmae</term> <note>The drachma was the standard unit of silver currency in Ptolemaic Egypt</note>
of copper: 1,000
5 and that which [is] in Phila[delph]ia
we paid in: silver 594
| total silver: 2,094 <term>drachmae</term>
copper 1,000 / total: 3,094 <term>drachmae</term>

<margin>Line 2.</margin> The money may have been deposited in the bank at Krokodilopolis <note>The capital of the Fayum region</note>, but this is not clear.

<margin>Line 10.</margin> For example: "of which we do not know the amount, but having inspected it, we will report how much it is."
<margin>Line 15.</margin> Perhaps the price (or a percentage of the price) of the shad-fish <note>original: "θρίσσαι" (thrissai)</note> which Zenon bought was paid to the Treasury as part of the dues owed by the fish dealers.
<margin>Line 18.</margin> Nikeratos: written thus in the original. The sums must be the amounts collected by or for Nikeratos, not what he owed personally as a tax-payer.
<margin>Line 21.</margin> Perhaps beer-tax collected by the guards <note>original: "φυλακῖται" (phylakitai)</note> and paid through Zenon.
<margin>Line 22.</margin> The traces of letters suggest "the assessments [on behalf of]."
<margin>Line 28.</margin> Either "[Memphis]" or else a date.
<margin>Line 30.</margin> Read "remaining" <note>original: "λοιπαί" (loipai) or "λοιπόν" (loipon)</note>. We do not know exactly how the balance has been obtained, as several of the entries are imperfect and the sums are given partly in silver and partly in copper.

<summary>This page catalogs a detailed financial memorandum belonging to Zenon, showing his bank deposits in silver and copper and subsequent tax payments made to the royal treasury for items like salt, beer, and fish. It illustrates Zenon's role as an unofficial tax collector and financial manager in the Philadelphia district of Ptolemaic Egypt.</summary>

This section (Section IX) serves as a dictionary of the vocabulary used in the 3rd century BC Egyptian administration and daily life.</meta>

<summary>This index provides an alphabetical list of Greek words found in the Zenon Papyri, ranging from agricultural terms like 'vetch' and 'aroura' to administrative terms like 'royal' and 'chief of police'. It is a vital tool for understanding the socioeconomic language of Ptolemaic Egypt.</summary>
<keywords>Zenon Papyri, Greek vocabulary, Ptolemaic Egypt, artaba, aroura, agricultural terms, administrative titles, index</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Greek index for the Zenon Papyri collection, moving from the letter Beta (β) through Gamma (γ) and Delta (δ), to the beginning of Epsilon (ε). This section functions as an analytical concordance, allowing researchers to find specific administrative, agricultural, and legal terms across the papyri held in the Cairo Museum.</meta>

<sig>25</sig>

<summary>This index continues the alphabetical listing of Greek terms from the Zenon Papyri, highlighting the varied vocabulary of the Ptolemaic administration. Key entries include terms for beer production (zytos), mule-driving, religious offices, and agricultural labor (katergon).</summary>
<keywords>beer tax, brewers, mules, priests, physicians, camels, agricultural labor, temple of Isis, Zenon archive, Ptolemaic Egypt</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the alphabetical Greek index of the Zenon Papyri, finishing the section for words beginning with Kappa (κ), proceeding through Lambda (λ), and covering much of Mu (μ). The entries reflect the agricultural, administrative, and social life of the Fayum region during the reign of Ptolemy II Philadelphus.</meta>

<summary>This index excerpt from the Zenon Papyri (pages 198–199) lists Greek terms related to agricultural production, labor, and administration in Ptolemaic Egypt. It features significant vocabulary for the grain trade (sitologos, sitometres), brick-making, and specialized crops like sesame and pomegranates.</summary>

<meta>This page serves as a list of additions and corrections (errata) for the volume, following the index of Greek terms. It provides updated readings and context for various papyri within the Zenon archive, often based on newer findings in the Michigan and Cornell collections.</meta>

# ADDITIONS AND CORRECTIONS.

---

59001, 54. Read **[I hold this] to be valid** <note>original: "[ἔχω] κυρίαν"</note>, which is the standard legal formula.
59052. I have now found the ends of lines 6-15 in the Michigan collection (*Inventory 3124*).
The letter from Sosipatros is dated **Apellaios 26** <note>a month in the Macedonian calendar, roughly December</note> and that from Antimenes is dated **Peritios 28** <note>roughly February</note>.
A comparison of these dates with that of the filing note (the docket), **Dystros 17** <note>roughly March</note>, makes it probable
that the letters from Antimenes were written from Alexandria and that Doris was
travelling from Egypt to Asia Minor. The full text will be given in my edition of
the Michigan papyri belonging to Zenon's archives.
59080, 3 and 8. Read **Bias** and **Bias** <note>original: "Βίαν" and "Βία", proper names in different grammatical cases</note> and interpret accordingly. This correction is rendered
certain by *P. Mich.*, 3126.
59130, 1-2. See Wilcken’s correction in *Archive*, VIII, page 66 and read **...lest we be without provisions** <note>original: "μη]οψωνουμ[εν]ωμεθα", referring to the purchase of food or rations</note>.
59176, introduction. I have misrepresented Westermann. The text of which he states that it
is written in a different hand is not *P. Wis.* <note>Wisconsin Papyri</note>, no. 1, but the long account of lamp-
oil known as *P. Cornell* <note>Cornell Papyri</note>, no. 1.
59176, 112 *and elsewhere* and 59274, 1. Read **Pammenes** <note>original: "Παμμένει"</note>. Thinking of the donkey-boy as an
Egyptian, I did not notice that his name was Greek.

<summary>This page lists corrections and updates to the cataloged papyri, incorporating new evidence from the Michigan and Cornell papyrus collections to clarify dates, names, and administrative details.</summary>
<keywords>Zenon archives, Michigan collection, Sosipatros, Antimenes, Alexandria, Doris, Macedonian calendar, terminology corrections, proper names, donkey-boy</keywords>

# CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM — ZENON PAPYRI, VOL. II.

### Plate I

<unclear>The left papyrus fragment contains approximately 50 lines of Greek cursive script. The handwriting is small and fluid, with many ligatures <note>connections between letters</note> characteristic of the Ptolemaic period <note>the era of Greek rule in Egypt, roughly 305–30 BCE</note>. Due to the photographic reproduction quality and the condition of the papyrus, a character-by-character transcription is not possible here.</unclear>

ZEN
ON

## Plate IV

<note>This plate displays two photographic reproductions of Ancient Greek papyrus fragments from the Zenon archive. The archive consists of thousands of documents belonging to Zenon, the son of Agreophon, who served as a high-level administrator for the Ptolemaic finance minister Apollonios.</note>

<image-desc>
The plate contains two distinct items:
- **Left (No. 59148):** A long, narrow vertical strip of papyrus containing approximately thirty lines of Greek cursive text. The strip is mostly intact but shows small holes <term>lacunae; gaps in the material where text has been lost</term> and wear along its length.
- **Right (No. 59149):** A wider, more irregularly shaped fragment of papyrus. The text is concentrated on the left side of the fragment, while the right side shows significant fraying and loss of the papyrus fibers. The script is a professional administrative cursive <note>a style of writing used by trained scribes for speed and efficiency in government records</note>.
</image-desc>

# CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM — ZENON PAPYRI, VOL. II.

## Plate VI

### 59155

<note>This plate shows a well-preserved papyrus fragment with clear, upright cursive handwriting. The text is a letter addressed to Zenon, the well-known manager of the vast estate of Apollonios (the finance minister of Egypt). It highlights the frequent petty disputes over loans and personal belongings that Zenon had to mediate from his office in Philadelphia.</note>

<image-desc>A monochrome photograph of a rectangular papyrus fragment with jagged "zig-zag" top and bottom edges. The Greek text consists of eight lines of body text and a single centered closing line. The writing is legible and maintains a consistent baseline.</image-desc>

To <unclear>Z</unclear>enon, greetings. You wrote to us to collect from <unclear>S</unclear>tratonikos the tunic <term>chiton; the standard Greek undergarment or shirt</term>, the shaggy cloak <term>sisyra; a thick wrap made of skin or coarse wool, often used for warmth or as bedding</term>, and the sack, along with anything else he owes. But Stratonikos claimed he owes nothing; in fact, he says that you actually owe *him*. He also stated that the garments <term>imatia; a general term for outer clothing or cloaks</term> which you brought to him are not yours. Although we demanded that he return your things, he claimed not to have them. Be aware, therefore, that he has returned nothing to us.

-> Farewell. Year 29, Phaophi 17 <note>original: "L κθ Φαῶφι ιζ". The "L" symbol denotes "Year." In the reign of Ptolemy II Philadelphus, Year 29, Phaophi 17 corresponds to approximately December 4, 257 BCE.</note> <-

<summary>A letter to Zenon reporting a failed attempt to recover a tunic, a cloak, and a sack from a man named Stratonikos, who denies the debt and claims Zenon is actually the one in debt to him.</summary>

<keywords>Zenon Archive, Stratonikos, debt dispute, clothing, chiton, sisyra, Ptolemaic Egypt, Year 29, Phaophi</keywords>

59206 (back side, columns 2, 3)

<note>This photographic plate displays fragment 59206 from the Zenon Archive. Zenon was the chief administrator for Apollonios, the finance minister of Egypt under Ptolemy II. This specific fragment is quite large but shows significant signs of age; the dark, horizontal fibers of the papyrus are interrupted by many holes and tears. The text is written in a rapid "cursive" script—a style used by ancient Greek clerks for business and legal documents rather than formal literature.</note>

<note>The following section represents the physical state of the papyrus. Because the ink has faded and the material is torn, much of the original Greek text cannot be recovered from this image alone.</note>

<note>Plate XVII features high-quality photographic reproductions of two papyrus documents. These are part of the Zenon Archive, the personal and professional papers of a Greek official living in Egypt during the 3rd century BCE. The archive provides an unparalleled look into the economy and daily life of the <term>Ptolemaic period</term> (the era when Greek kings ruled Egypt following Alexander the Great).</note>

->59236<-

<note>This item, catalogued as P.Cair.Zen. II 59236, is a long, narrow vertical strip. It contains approximately 30 lines of Greek <term>cursive</term>—a rapid, flowing style of handwriting used by ancient clerks and administrators. Such long, thin strips were often used for detailed lists of expenditures, grain distributions, or payroll records for workers on the Philadelphia estate.</note>

The roughly 20 lines of text here are likely part of a memorandum or a letter. Despite the physical decay, the clear, professional hand of the scribe remains visible.</note>

## PLATE XIX

->CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM. — ZENON PAPYRI, VOL. II. PLATE XIX<-

<margin>59242</margin>

<note>This plate displays fragment 59242 from the Zenon Archive (3rd century BCE). The archive consists of the files of Zenon, a Greek official managing a massive 10,000-acre estate in the Faiyum region of Egypt. This particular document has survived only in seven thin, vertical strips. Because the papyrus was likely rolled or folded when it was damaged, the vertical breaks are somewhat regular, but they leave significant gaps in the text. Even in this state, the <term>cursive</term> hand—the everyday handwriting of the time—is visible, and certain administrative terms allow us to identify it as part of Zenon’s correspondence.</note>

# CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM — ZENON PAPYRI, VOL. II.
## PLATE XXI

->CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM. — ZENON PAPYRI, VOL. II. PLATE XXI<-

<column-break/>

->59247<-

<margin>59247</margin>

<note>Fragment 59247 is a significant administrative record from the Zenon Archive. Zenon was the estate manager for Apollonios, the <term>dioiketes</term> (the chief financial officer or finance minister) of Egypt under the reign of Ptolemy II Philadelphus. This archive is one of the most important discoveries for understanding the economic life of the Faiyum region in the 3rd century BCE, covering everything from international trade to the minute details of agricultural irrigation.</note>

<note>This specific fragment displays approximately 23 lines of text. The handwriting is a professional <term>cursive</term>, a rapid style of Greek script where letters are often linked together to save time. This style was the standard for the Ptolemaic bureaucracy. The physical condition of the papyrus shows horizontal striations and small losses of material—common in ancient documents that were rolled and stored in dry conditions for millennia.</note>

<unclear>... [Approximately 23 lines of Greek cursive script] ...</unclear>

It serves as a prime example of the professional bureaucratic handwriting used by the staff of the finance minister Apollonios in the 3rd century BCE.</summary>

<column-break/>

->59254<-

<margin>59254</margin>

<note>Fragment 59254 is a significant piece of the Zenon Archive, a collection of thousands of documents belonging to Zenon, son of Agreophon. Zenon was the estate manager for Apollonios, the <term>dioiketes</term> (the highest-ranking financial official) of Egypt during the reign of Ptolemy II Philadelphus in the 3rd century BCE. This archive provides an unparalleled look into the Greek administration of Egypt and the agricultural development of the Faiyum region.</note>

<note>This specific fragment contains at least four distinct columns of text written in a professional <term>cursive</term>. This was a rapid, fluid style of Greek handwriting where letters were often joined together, used by trained scribes for administrative lists, accounts, and letters. The high-contrast photography of this plate highlights the vertical fibers of the papyrus, though it makes some of the ink strokes appear thicker than they are in person.</note>

<note>The name "Zenon" <note>original: "ΖΗΝΩΝ"</note> can be found at the bottom of the rightmost column, identifying either the author, recipient, or the subject of this specific record.</note>

# CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM — ZENON PAPYRI, VOL. II.
## PLATE XXIV

->CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM. — ZENON PAPYRI, VOL. II. PLATE XXIV<-

<note>The Zenon Archive is a collection of over 3,000 papyri from the 3rd century BCE. Zenon was the manager of a massive 10,000-acre estate in the Faiyum region of Egypt. These fragments, though broken, give us glimpses into the daily administrative grind of the Ptolemaic era.</note>

### Fragment 59263

<note>This fragment is a long, narrow strip that appears to be the beginning of a formal letter. Because papyrus was expensive, it was often cut or torn into strips for short messages or reused for different purposes.</note>

1. <unclear>...]mak[...]a[...]</unclear>
2. <unclear>...] in the [...]</unclear> <note>original: "εν τωι"</note>
3. <unclear>...] to Zenon, greeting [...]</unclear> <note>original: "Ζηνωνι χαιρειν". This is the standard Greek opening formula for correspondence. The name "Zenon" is clearly visible, identifying the recipient.</note>

---

### Fragment 59270

<note>This larger fragment contains several numerical words, suggesting it might be a report or an account of goods or currency. In the ancient world, "fifteen" and "one hundred" could refer to measures of grain (artabae) or sums of money (drachmae).</note>

1. <unclear>...] of those from [...]</unclear> <note>original: "τ̣ω̣ν̣ π̣α̣ρ̣α̣σ̣..."</note>
2. <unclear>...] fifteen [...]</unclear> <note>original: "δεκαπεντε" (dekapente)</note>
3. <unclear>...] and the [...]</unclear>
4. <unclear>...] and instead of [...]</unclear> <note>original: "α̣ν̣τ̣ι̣" (anti)</note>
5. <unclear>...] less [...]</unclear> <note>original: "ε̣λ̣α̣τ̣τ̣...", likely from 'elasson,' meaning smaller, fewer, or less.</note>
6. <unclear>...] and upon [...]</unclear>
7. <unclear>...] one hundred [...]</unclear> <note>original: "ε̣κ̣α̣τ̣ο̣ν̣" (hekaton)</note>
8. <unclear>...] in to the [...]</unclear>
9. <unclear>...] hundreds [...]</unclear> <note>original: "α̣κ̣ο̣σ̣ι̣α̣ς̣", the suffix for a larger number in the hundreds (e.g., three hundred or six hundred).</note>
10. <unclear>...] of the [...]</unclear>
11. <unclear>...] of him/itself [...]</unclear>
12. <unclear>...] in [...]</unclear>
13. <unclear>[...]</unclear>

---

# CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM — ZENON PAPYRI, VOL. II.
## PLATE XXV

->CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM. — ZENON PAPYRI, VOL. II. PLATE XXV<-

<note>This page displays three fragments of papyrus from the mid-3rd century BCE. They belong to the archive of Zenon, a high-ranking official under the Ptolemaic finance minister Apollonios. Because these fragments are heavily damaged, they appear like a jigsaw puzzle of ancient administration.</note>

### Fragment 59288

<note>This fragment is a horizontal strip from a letter or an official report. Even in its broken state, we can identify formal administrative language, including a reference to a bank and a formal memorandum.</note>

1. <unclear>...</unclear> to Zenon, greeting <note>original: "Ζηνωνι χαιρειν". This is the standard opening for a Greek letter.</note>
2. <unclear>...</unclear> and of the bank <unclear>...</unclear> <note>original: "τραπε[ζ...]" (trape...). The "trapeza" was the Greek term for a bank or money-changer's table.</note>
3. <unclear>...</unclear> on behalf of the [matter] to Zenon <unclear>...</unclear>
4. <unclear>...</unclear>
5. <unclear>...</unclear> has been written upon <unclear>...</unclear> <note>original: "επιγεγραπται"</note>
6. <unclear>...</unclear> regarding the exhortation <unclear>...</unclear> <note>original: "παρακαλ[...]" (parakal...). This could also mean to summon or request.</note>
7. <unclear>...</unclear> towards <unclear>...</unclear>
8. <unclear>...</unclear> has done/made <unclear>...</unclear> <note>original: "πεποιηκ[...]" (pepoiek...)</note>
9. <unclear>...</unclear> from me <unclear>...</unclear>
10. <unclear>...</unclear> the memorandum <unclear>...</unclear> <note>original: "υπομνημα" (hypomnēma). A common document type in the Ptolemaic bureaucracy, used for petitions or internal reminders.</note>

---

### Fragment 59280

<note>This fragment is severely damaged. The fibers of the papyrus have frayed, and the ink has faded significantly, making a continuous translation impossible. It represents the physical decay that many of these ancient records suffered over 2,000 years in the Egyptian soil.</note>

<unclear>
[Approximately 10-12 lines of text are visible but too fragmented to yield a coherent translation.]
</unclear>

Zenon served as the estate manager for Apollonios, the "dioiketes" (finance minister) of Ptolemaic Egypt during the 3rd century BCE. These fragments are vital for understanding the Greek administration of the Egyptian countryside, even when their condition makes reading difficult.</note>

# CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM — ZENON PAPYRI, VOL. II.
## PLATE XXIX

->CATALOGUE OF THE CAIRO MUSEUM. — ZENON PAPYRI, VOL. II. PLATE XXIX<-

<margin>
59292 (a), columns 5-7.
</margin>

<note>
The papyrus fragments shown here are part of the Zenon Archive, a massive collection of thousands of documents from the 3rd century BCE. These were discovered in the ancient town of Philadelphia, Egypt. The text is written in Ancient Greek. Due to the flowing nature of the cursive handwriting and the current state of the material, a character-by-character transcription is not provided on this plate. These manuscripts generally contain administrative records or personal letters related to the estate of Apollonios, who held the position of <term>dioiketes</term><note>original: "διοικητής"; the highest-ranking finance minister or state treasurer in Ptolemaic Egypt</note> under King Ptolemy II Philadelphus.
</note>

<column-break/>

<note>
This high-resolution photograph shows several dark brown fragments where the horizontal and vertical patterns of the papyrus fibers are still visible. The text is written in a professional Greek cursive hand using black ink. The fragments are arranged to show the flow of text across what the marginal note identifies as columns 5 through 7. You can see small, rectangular white paper strips; these are modern reinforcements used by conservators to stabilize the fragile fragments. The writing is densely packed, and the edges of the papyrus are irregular and worn by time.
</note>

<meta>Printed by Catala Brothers, Paris.</meta>

<summary>This page features a photographic plate of three columns from a Greek papyrus manuscript belonging to the Zenon Archive, dating to the 3rd century BCE. It depicts fragments of administrative or personal records from the estate of Apollonios, the finance minister of Ptolemy II Philadelphus.</summary>

<keywords>Zenon Archive, papyrology, Ptolemaic Egypt, Greek cursive, Apollonios, dioiketes, Philadelphia Egypt, 3rd century BCE, administrative records</keywords>

->LIBRARY OF THE UNIVERSITY<-

<note>This is a circular library stamp from the University of Caen, added to the document or the plate in more recent history.</note>
</insert>

---

<note>
This plate displays a large, irregularly shaped papyrus fragment from the Zenon Archive (3rd century BCE). The material is dark and fibrous, showing the passage of over two millennia through numerous holes and frayed edges. While the Greek script is too damaged for a full transcription here, the layout reveals several vertical columns of professional cursive handwriting.
</note>

<note>
Documents of this nature within the Zenon collection typically involve the administrative affairs of the <term>dioiketes</term><note>original: "διοικητής"; the chief financial minister of Ptolemaic Egypt</note> Apollonios. They provide a window into the daily management of a massive agricultural estate, covering everything from irrigation and crop yields to the wages of workers and local legal disputes.
</note>

<column-break/>

<note>
The physical preservation of this fragment is a testament to the dry climate of the Fayum region, where the archive was discovered. Despite the heavy damage, one can clearly see the "recto" side of the papyrus—where the fibers run horizontally—which was the preferred surface for writing in the ancient world.
</note>

->PRINTED BY CATALA BROTHERS, PARIS.<-
